# One generator: Forge, feature packs, and a profile-free console

**Issue:** apache/grails-core #16348 · **Target:** 9.0.x · **Revision:** 51, 2026-11-23

2. [2. Target architecture](#architecture)
   1. [2.1 One command tree](#one-cli)
   2. [2.2 Feature packs](#engine)
   3. [2.3 Catalog and fetch](#metadata)
   4. [2.4 The .grails directory](#project-state)
   5. [2.5 The console](#console)
   6. [2.6 feature add](#feature-add)
   7. [2.7 Hosted Forge](#hosted)
3. [3. What profiles offered](#gaps)

This plan makes Forge the sole application-generation engine, lets plugins contribute descriptors and templates without compiling code into Forge or invoking Gradle during generation, and moves interactive and lifecycle commands into a profile-free command layer. The existing shell's useful services survive; its profile dispatcher, published shell entry point and artifact do not define the new architecture.

**Release boundary, decided 2026-10-26:** PR #16551, merged on 2026-10-14, converted Forge to a Grails plugin hosted by Grails applications and belongs to 8.1.x. Every new capability and removal described here belongs to 9.0.x: packs, presets, the catalog, project state, `feature add`, migration, the unified CLI and profile removal. There is no 8.1 implementation-replacement phase, dual-written manifest, or new command tree alongside the old dispatcher. The 8.1 guide continues to describe profiles and both existing CLI paths.

<a id="verdict"></a>
## 1. Verdict on the proposal

Profiles bought two things in Grails 3: a skeleton engine and an extensibility story, since anyone could publish a profile jar without a Grails pull request. Forge is the better skeleton engine (exclusive options, BOM switching, preview and diff, a website, version slots), but its features are compiled `@Component`s in `grails-forge-core`. A declarative descriptor plus executable GSP source templates restores extensibility without compiling a feature class into Forge or running Gradle during generation. The descriptor is data; the pack as a whole is trusted code, not a data-only format.

| Proposal item | Verdict | Adjustment |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. forge-cli searches plugin metadata and selects plugins | Yes | The metadata is `apache/grails-plugins-metadata`: 256 reviewed plugin entries with coordinates, labels, docs and an indexer that already downloads each released jar to read its descriptor. Nothing in the framework reads it yet: today's shell `list-plugins` command still scrapes an HTML directory listing of the legacy `org.grails.plugins` repository (`grails-profiles/base/commands/list-plugins.groovy:7`), and Forge reads only its own `pom.xml` for third-party versions. Both go away. The plan makes Forge read `grails-plugins-index.json` from the repository's `main` branch and adds one artifact: a **feature pack** (per-plugin descriptor plus templates) that the indexer learns to detect. |
| 2. forge-cli updates existing apps with a plugin's feature | Yes, scoped | `grails feature add` uses a committed generation baseline, locked inputs and a transactional three-way merge. Existing `FeatureDiffer` supplies text-diff presentation, not a ready-made merge or historical baseline. `project migrate` replaces the never-implemented `update-cli-config` guidance. |
| 3. Remove `create-app` from grails-shell-cli | Yes | Keep the command name; it becomes Forge's. Delete the dispatcher (`DelegatingShellApplication`), `-t/--type`, and `GRAILS_PREFERRED_SHELL`, which still defaults to the profile shell (`:56`). |
| 4. Remove profiles | Yes, staged | The widest blast radius. Every app build has a `profile` configuration with a default `org.apache.grails.profiles:web` dependency (`grails-gradle/plugins/.../GrailsGradlePlugin.groovy:461-490`); `grails.profile` is injected as an active Spring profile at runtime (`grails-core/.../GrailsApplicationPostProcessor.groovy:451`); 109 in-repo `application.yml` files set it; the BOM publishes profile constraints; the release verifier inspects the base profile jar (`etc/bin/verify-jar-artifacts.sh:208-236`). Nothing is removed in a minor release: the modules, shell code, `profile` Gradle configuration and runtime `grails.profile` injection all go in 9.0.x together, with `project migrate` and the upgrade guide carrying existing applications. |
| 5. Keep the shell's interactive capabilities | Yes, re-home them | In-project dispatch currently goes through a profile, and lifecycle commands live in profile scripts. Move the console services, built-ins and completion behind a profile-free command catalog and the unified CLI entry point. The existing `grails-shell-cli` artifact and `org.grails.cli.GrailsCli` remain for 8.1 projects and are retired on 9.0. |
| `.grails/` directory, gitignored | Partly | Commit `project.yml`, `forge.lock.yml` and `baseline/`; ignore caches, transaction journals, run state and history. Team members need the same generation inputs and merge ancestor, not just a list of feature names. |

<a id="architecture"></a>
## 2. Target architecture

Section 2 describes the 9.0 target. Section 5 sequences its implementation and defines the gates before profile removal. Unless explicitly labelled historical, proposed CLI behavior below is 9.0 behavior.

<a id="one-cli"></a>
### 2.1 One `grails` command tree

- Picocli root = Forge's `ForgeCommand`, named `grails`. Subcommands: `create-app`, `create-restapi`, `create-plugin`, `create-web-plugin`, `create-rest-api-plugin` (#14932), `feature list|search|info|diff|add`, `plugin search|info`, artefact commands, `project init|migrate`, and console commands. `plugin` searches all plugin metadata; `feature` searches usable generation features, so these are separate views over one index, not identical aliases. With no arguments inside a project the CLI opens the interactive console; outside it prints help.
- One `CommandCatalog` supplies dispatch, help, completion and IDE metadata. Register built-ins without application classpath resolution; load extension descriptors lazily. Refresh the catalog and completion after `feature add`, project initialization or explicit dependency refresh rather than freezing them for an entire interactive session.
- Delete `DelegatingShellApplication.groovy`, the shell/Forge alias start scripts, `-t/--type` dispatch and `GRAILS_PREFERRED_SHELL` on 9.0. The 8.1 distribution retains its existing entry points. A 9.0 CLI in an unmigrated 8.x project offers migration guidance rather than silently running the new generator against old metadata.
- `create-app --features` uses Forge's additive semantics and rejects unknown names; `--exclude-features` suppresses optional defaults. Document this major-version change, including the replacement of `--profile` by `--preset`. Align dependency intent with #14944/#14933, but do not promise byte-identical profile and Forge output.
- Startup budget: ordinary `--help`, `help --json` and migration preflight must not require Gradle, artifact downloads or a booted application. Load the Forge application context only when generation needs it. Measure cold and warm startup on documented reference machines; 500 ms for built-in help is a target to validate, not an existing guarantee. Dynamic catalog completeness is explicit (section 2.5).

<a id="engine"></a>
### 2.2 Generation engine: compiled features plus declarative feature packs

Forge keeps compiled `Feature` beans for framework-owned concerns: application types, build files, BOM switching and options. It gains a `DeclarativeFeature` adapter over a shared, identifier-based selection model. Construct a generation-scoped registry from built-ins and the resolved pack set; never mutate a global registry with a request's external packs. Packs are loaded from three places:

1. built-in packs bundled in `grails-forge-core` resources (official plugins migrate from compiled classes to packs wherever the feature is "dependencies plus config plus files");
2. packs fetched from plugin jars at generation time (third-party, section 2.3);
3. a local directory (`--feature-pack-dir`) for authoring and testing a pack before publishing.

Descriptor, `META-INF/grails-forge/feature.yml`:

```
format: 1
kind: feature
name: acme-audit
templateApi: 1
compatibility:
  grails: '[9.0.0,10.0.0)'
  forge: '[9.0.0,10.0.0)'
title: Acme Audit Trail
description: Adds the Acme audit plugin and its default configuration
category: Logging                      # a Forge Category string
documentation: https://acme.example/docs/grails
applicationTypes: [web, rest-api]      # default: all
requires: [gorm-hibernate7, database-migration] # resolved before contribution/rendering
conflicts: [gorm-mongodb]              # validated against the final selection
oneOf: com.acme:audit                  # optional, namespaced exclusive group
dependencies:
  - scope: implementation
    coordinates: com.acme:acme-audit-grails
    version: ${pack.version}           # explicit substitution; omit only when BOM-managed
  - scope: runtimeOnly
    coordinates: com.acme:acme-audit-h2 # version managed by acme-bom below
    when: { feature: h2 }              # conditional on another selected feature
    excludes: ['org.slf4j:slf4j-simple'] # new: Dependency has no exclusions today
platforms:
  - coordinates: com.acme:acme-bom
    version: 2.3.0
buildPlugins:
  - id: com.acme.audit
    version: 2.3.0
build:                                 # typed, merged build-file contributions (see "Build-file contributions")
  properties:
    acme.audit.verbose: 'false'        # gradle.properties
  extensions:
    acmeAudit: |                       # one acmeAudit { } block, merged with other packs' entries
      retention = 90
    tasks.bootRun: |                   # tasks.named('bootRun') { }
      systemProperty 'acme.audit.console', 'true'
configuration:                         # merged through Forge's configuration model
  acme.audit.enabled: true
  environments:
    production:
      acme.audit.flush: 30s
files:                                 # relative to META-INF/grails-forge/templates/
  - from: AuditConfig.groovy.gsp
    to: grails-app/conf/{packagePath}/AuditConfig.groovy
  - from: audit.css                    # no suffix: copied verbatim, binary-safe
    to: grails-app/assets/stylesheets/audit.css
readme: |
  Audit events are written to the `audit_event` table. See the documentation link.
instructions: |                        # printed after create-app, as profiles did
  Run `./gradlew dbmGormDiff` to generate the audit tables migration.
artefacts:                             # adds `grails create-audit-listener <Name>`
  - command: create-audit-listener
    description: Creates an audit listener and its specification
    files:
      - from: artefact/AuditListener.groovy.gsp
        to: src/main/groovy/{packagePath}/{className}AuditListener.groovy
      - from: artefact/AuditListenerSpec.groovy.gsp
        to: src/test/groovy/{packagePath}/{className}AuditListenerSpec.groovy
```

Mapping onto what exists in `grails-forge-core`:

| Descriptor key | Forge call | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `dependencies`, `platforms` | Extend `GeneratorContext.addDependency` and the `Dependency`/Gradle models with exclusions and provenance-aware conflict checks | Collect contributions before the `BUILD` snapshot. Reject late contributions instead of silently dropping them. |
| `buildPlugins` | `GradlePlugin.builder().id().version()` |  |
| `build.properties` | `getBuildProperties().put` (`GeneratorContext.groovy:145`) | Snapshotted at `BUILD` like dependencies (`Gradle.groovy:80`). |
| `build.extensions`, `build.blocks`, `build.imports`, `build.settings` | a new keyed contribution API on `GradleBuild`, replacing the single `renderExtensions()` slot (`GradleBuild.groovy:122-134`) and `renderSettingsExtensions()` | See "Build-file contributions". |
| `configuration` | `getConfiguration()`, `getConfiguration(env)` (`GeneratorContext.groovy:152-188`) |  |
| `files` | `addTemplate` with `GspTemplate` over a pack-scoped page locator, or a byte-preserving copy | Destination tokens include `{packageName}`, `{packagePath}`, `{className}`, `{naturalName}`, `{propertyName}`, `{name}`. Record text/binary kind and executable mode separately from whether a file is templated. |
| `readme` | `addHelpTemplate` |  |
| `requires`, `conflicts`, `oneOf` | Identifier-based selection and group validation shared by compiled and declarative features | Existing class-based `isPresent` and `OneOfFeature` validation are insufficient. |
| `artefacts` | new entries in the codegen command set, which is a static list today (`ForgeCli.CODE_GEN_COMMANDS`, `ForgeCli.groovy:45-57`) | Must become dynamic. |

**Descriptor reading, as implemented so far (step 1).** `org.apache.grails.forge.core.pack.PackDescriptorParser` reads the descriptor into a `PackDescriptor` that cannot be changed, down to its nested configuration, and has value equality, in which the whole number 1 and the fraction 1.0 are different values. A descriptor is untrusted input, so:

- The YAML is loaded with SnakeYAML's safe constructor. A descriptor with an anchor, an alias or a tag anywhere is refused before anything is built: an alias repeats a value any number of times for a few characters, and a tag gives a value a type or a reading other than the one it is written in, and a descriptor needs neither. While the text is scanned, a key that is a mapping or a list is refused, and so is an unquoted key that would be read as a number, `true`, `false` or empty: with only text for keys, a mapping can order keys that share a hash code, and with anything else among them the loader compares each key with every other. Duplicate keys are then refused by the loader. A descriptor has at most 262,144 characters and mappings and lists at most 20 deep, counting the descriptor itself; the depth is checked while the text is scanned, so a long run of `[` is refused at its twenty-first. Whatever exception the loader throws is reported as a `PackDescriptorException`. Every later check works on a value only after its length is known to be within its limit. These are meant to keep the time and memory of reading a descriptor in proportion to its size; six review rounds each found a way around that and each is now a test; the sixth also went through every loop and pattern of the parser and found the others linear, and a seventh found nothing more: the slowest input it could build, 65,450 text keys sharing one hash code, is read in about 0.2 s and grows as n log n. What is known to remain: reading a descriptor at the size limit needs some 64 MB of heap or more for SnakeYAML's node tree and the maps built from it, which matters if a server reads several at once, and an `OutOfMemoryError` is not turned into a `PackDescriptorException`.
- A value without quotes is text unless it is `true`, `false`, empty (written as nothing, `~` or `null`) or a number in decimal digits that a `long` or a `double` keeps as written: a whole number of up to 18 digits, or a fraction of up to 15 digits in all. A fraction keeps its value but not its spelling: it loses a trailing zero (`1.10` is 1.1), which is why a version must be quoted, and the error for an unquoted one says so, and whatever later writes a configuration value has to print it plainly, since Java prints `0.0001` as `1.0E-4`. The other YAML 1.1 forms stay text: `yes` and `on`, octal and hexadecimal numbers, numbers in base sixty, dates, longer numbers, `-0`, and the `<<` merge key, which is a key like any other.
- Every key is text (a number, `true`, `false` or `null` as a key has to be quoted, and an entry cannot be without a key; the refusal comes from the scan and names the key but not its place), not blank, and one line of at most 200 characters without control, format or private-use characters, and the place of a problem shows at most 40 characters of each key on the way to it.
- Values that end up in a generated file as data are held to the characters such a value has, so they cannot carry code or a second line into `build.gradle` or `gradle.properties`: coordinates, exclusions, versions, plugin ids, build extension names, feature and group IDs, and the documentation address (http or https, URL characters only, without quotes, angle brackets or `$`). A build property has a name of letters, digits, `.`, `-` and `_` and a value, possibly empty (with or without quotes), of printable ASCII on one line with no backslash and no space at its ends, which `gradle.properties` reads back unchanged. The parser does not restrict which properties a pack sets; `org.gradle.jvmargs` is as valid as any. Build extension bodies, the readme, the instructions and the templates are the pack's content; the first three are limited in length and held to the same characters as the text people read, below.
- Text people read (title, description, readme, instructions) may not contain control characters (a tab is allowed in the readme and the instructions), private-use characters, unpaired surrogates, line or paragraph separators, the characters that reorder the text around them, or the invisible tag characters; it keeps the other format characters scripts and emoji need. Refusing the tag characters also refuses the three subdivision flag emoji (England, Scotland, Wales), which are spelled with them.
- A file path is relative with `/` between its at most 32 steps, and a step is not `.` or `..`, has no backslash, colon, wildcard, quote, angle bracket, bar, control, format or private-use character, does not start with `~` or contain `~` followed by a digit, as a Windows short name does, does not start or end with a space or end with a dot, is at most 255 characters, and is not a Windows device name or `.git`. A destination may use the documented placeholders; a source may not. Two destinations that differ only by case or Unicode form are duplicates (the comparison is cautious: `straße` and `strasse` count as one), and one destination cannot be a directory of another. The values substituted for placeholders are not known when the descriptor is read, so the code that writes the files has to check the resulting path again; whether a destination collides with a file the framework generates (`build.gradle`, the wrapper) is also for that code.
- What is refused while the text is scanned, and what SnakeYAML cannot load, is reported one problem at a time. Then the form of the keys is checked, and when that is right every other problem is reported, each with its place. At most 50 problems are listed, followed by how many more there are. A message quotes at most 80 characters of a value (300 of what SnakeYAML says, 200 of where the descriptor came from), never a whole map or list, with unsafe characters replaced.

It reads `format`, `kind` (required, `feature` only), `name`, `templateApi`, `compatibility.grails`/`forge` (required; each is one release or intervals of releases, see below), `title`, `description`, `category`, `documentation`, `applicationTypes`, `requires` and `conflicts` (feature names or `group:artifact#name`), `oneOf` (a group ID), `dependencies` (with `excludes`; one version, not a range), `platforms`, `buildPlugins`, `build.properties`, `build.extensions`, `configuration`, `files` (`from`/`to`), `readme` and `instructions`. Not read yet, and therefore errors: `when` predicates, the `oneOf` form of an entry of `requires`, `flags`, parameters, `artefacts`, `build.blocks`/`imports`/`settings`, `executable`/`binary` on a file, `${pack.version}` as a version, and `kind: preset`. The descriptor keeps a `tasks.<task>` extension name as it is; mapping it to `BuildFileContributions.task` is the adapter's job. 

**Packs in generation, as implemented so far (step 1).** `PackLoader.fromDirectory` reads a pack from a directory (`feature.yml` and, under `templates`, the files it names) once: the content of the files is kept as it was read, so what is written later is what was checked. The descriptor, `templates`, and every directory and file on the way to a named file must be real, not symbolic links, when they are looked at, which is before and again after a file is read, and a file with a second name (a hard link) is refused where the file system counts names; a file has at most 1 MiB and the entries of a pack 16 MiB together; a file whose name ends with `.gsp`, in any case of letters, is a template and has to be text in UTF-8.

`PackFeature` is the feature of a pack. It contributes its dependencies with their exclusions, platforms, build plugins, build properties, build extensions (`tasks.<task>` becomes the configuration of that task), configuration, files, and readme. A pack adds to an application and does not change what another feature gives it:

- The features it requires are selected with it, before the defaults: `ContextFactory` now selects what every selected feature names in `getDependentFeatures()`, transitively, and only then gives the features nothing has chosen their defaults. A pack that requires `postgres` therefore gets no `h2` next to it. A required name that is not available for the application type is an error.
- It refuses to be applied with a feature it conflicts with.
- A setting or a build property that the pack and another feature both declare must be the same in both, compared strictly (`true` is not `'true'`). The pack checks when it is applied and again once every feature is applied, so the outcome does not depend on which of the two comes first. A setting cannot be a value for one and a group of settings for the other (`a.b` and `a.b.c`), which the YAML writer would misplace; a mapping without settings sets nothing. The settings under `environments.<name>` are kept under that name in `application.yml`, where the framework keeps most of its own, and are also compared with the separate file the framework has for an environment (`application-development.yml` of DevTools, `application-test.yml` of Testcontainers); the parser holds the name of an environment to a name.
- A dependency the pack gives a version must not be in the application in another version or without one, in whatever scope, so a pack cannot pin what the application leaves to its platform, also not by declaring it in another scope.
- For every feature, declaring one dependency twice in one scope in different versions or kinds, or applying one build plugin in two versions or once in the `plugins` block and once with `apply plugin`, is now an error in `GeneratorContext`, as different exclusions already were. A pack therefore cannot repeat a plugin the framework applies with `apply plugin`, or set the properties the Gradle feature sets (`version`, `org.gradle.*`, `grailsVersion`) to another value. A context whose features could not be applied fails the same way when it is used again, where it generated a partial application.

A file whose name in the pack ends with `.gsp` is a template; any other file is written as it is. `PackPages` compiles a template from its source with the GSP engine Forge renders its own pages with, as if it started with `<%@ page trimLogicLines="true" expressionCodec="none" %>`, and renders it when the feature is applied, so every feature is chosen by then and a template that cannot be compiled or rendered, or writes more than 1 MiB, fails the generation before anything is written. One engine is made for a generation, shared by its packs and made only when one of them has a template; it reads the source as UTF-8 whatever the platform's encoding is, and a byte order mark is dropped. Every destination of a pack is checked before its first template is rendered, and what the files of a pack come to after rendering is bounded at 16 MiB like its sources. A context is generated only as the project it was made for. What a template can refer to is template API 1, `PackModel`: `project` (`name`, `packageName`, `packagePath`, `className`, `naturalName`, `propertyName`), `features` (the names of every feature of the application, sorted), `applicationType`, `options` (`jdk`, `data`, `servlet`, `reloading`), `grailsVersion` and `pack.name`, all of them text, numbers, lists and maps that cannot be changed, and none of them a class of Forge. The options are written as the create commands take them (`hibernate7`, `tomcat`, `devtools`). A name that template API 1 has not, at the top or inside `project`, `options` or `pack`, fails the template with the names there are; it does not render as nothing. That holds for the names a web page has too (`params`, `request`, `grailsApplication`), which a template has not; `out`, `it` and `pageScope` are the page's own and are there. A template asks whether a name is there with `containsKey` or `in`. An assertion, a linkage error and a stack overflow in a template fail it as an exception does, with the cause kept; an error of the virtual machine other than a stack overflow (running out of memory, an internal error) is passed on as it is. A template can still import a class of Forge or declare its own page directives, and is then outside the contract. A template is code of the pack and is not confined: it can do what the generator can.

The compatibility of a pack is evaluated when its features are made (`PackVersionRange`): `9.0.0` is that release only, `[9.0.0,10.0.0)` an interval with the square end in and the round end out, `[9.1,)` and `(,10)` open at one end, and several intervals are separated by commas. The versions of a range are releases of up to four numbers, a number left out being zero; a qualifier in a range is refused by the parser. The version that is compared is taken for the release it leads to, so `9.0.0-SNAPSHOT`, `9.0.0-M2` and `9.0.0-RC1` are in a range that has 9.0.0: a pack for a release can be tried on what leads to it. `grails` is compared with the version of Grails the generator generates for and `forge` with the version of the generator, which are one version today. A pack that is given and is not for this Grails or this Forge is refused, selected or not and whatever kinds of application it is for, with the range and the version that is not in it in the message. A version that is not a release with nothing or a qualifier after it (`9.0.1a`, five numbers) is in no range.

Forge has packs in itself (`BundledPackFeatures`): a feature of the framework that is only dependencies, settings and files is written in the format a plugin uses, under `META-INF/grails-forge-bundled/<name>/` of the resources of `grails-forge-core`, and named in the index `META-INF/grails-forge-bundled/packs`. Each is registered as a bean of the generator, so it is a feature like the compiled ones for the command line and for the hosted Forge: listed, selected, validated and counted among the names a pack of a plugin cannot take. A pack of Forge that cannot be read or is not for this Grails stops Forge from starting. One thing is different for such a pack: a dependency can say `versionOfForge: true` in place of a version and has the group and version Forge knows for the artifact, as a compiled feature gets with `lookupArtifactId`, so the version stays in one place. A dependency without that key and without a version has none, as before, and the parser refuses the key in the pack of a plugin. A pack of Forge can be in an exclusive group of the framework (`cache-ehcache` is in `cache-provider`); the registry asks a feature that is a pack for no class of its family. The descriptor has `thirdPartyDocumentation`, a link, for any pack. `grails-web-console`, `grails-quartz` and `cache-ehcache` are converted, and the applications generated with each, alone and beside every other visible feature, for the four kinds of application (23,142 files), are the same as before the conversion but for the time of day in the header of a `.properties` file. The conversion is checked with a temporary spec that dumps every such generation before and after; it is not kept in the tree. What is kept is `BundledPackGoldenSpec`: for every pack of the index a golden file under `src/test/resources/golden/bundled` with what the feature says of itself and every line an application has with it that it has not without it, for each kind of application, the versions of dependencies and of the documentation of Grails written as `VERSION` (a version inside a file a template wrote, or of a build plugin, is as it is), and from the descriptor what those lines cannot show: its compatibility, requirements, conflicts and group, where each version comes from, and where its build blocks go; and the exact list of the names of the index. A pack of Forge can also say `guideOfGrails: guide/<page>.html` in place of `documentation`; the link is then to that page in the documentation of the version of Grails this Forge generates for (`snapshot` for a snapshot), as the compiled features computed it. The default of the layout group, `sitemesh3`, stands aside for a feature of its group and not of its type, and a group whose compiled features say so with `OneOfFeature.isFoundByGroup` can have packs beside them. Converted so far, thirteen: `asciidoctor`, `cache`, `cache-ehcache`, `grails-layout`, `grails-quartz`, `grails-spring-ai`, `grails-web-console`, `hibernate-validator`, `openapi`, `shade`, `spring-ai-anthropic`, `spring-ai-ollama`, `spring-ai-openai`; each was compared, alone and beside every other visible feature for the four kinds of application, with what the compiled feature generated. One thing an application can see changed: the three Spring AI providers now name `grails-spring-ai` as a feature they depend on (`getDependentFeatures`, which the hosted Forge reports), where the compiled providers added it without saying so. Known and kept, because the compiled features did the same: a link added to the readme with `readmeLinks` has no line break after it (the view `markdownLink.gsp` ends without one), so two links, and the heading that follows, are on one line; and Forge knows no version for `asciidoctorj`, so the versions the Asciidoctor descriptor falls back to are always the ones used. A dependency that says `versionOfForge` has to name the group Forge knows the artifact by. An address in a descriptor cannot have square brackets, with which it could end the text of the link it is written into and start another. What was looked at then and left compiled because the format could not say it (the database drivers, the servlet containers, DevTools, and every feature that takes part in selection) can be said since: see "The keys only a pack of Forge has" below, and the table after it for each feature that is compiled still. An index that is missing, empty or names a pack twice stops Forge from starting. An exclusive group cannot have both packs and compiled features that are looked for by their type, since such a default would not see the pack: the registry refuses that at startup, and takes a group whose compiled features say they are found by the group. A feature a pack requires has to support the kind of application wherever the requirement is resolved. Not done for packs of Forge: their templates are compiled from source at each use and are not precompiled.

A pack of Forge can say four more things, each of which the parser reads only for such a pack (`parseOfForge`) and refuses, as a key it does not read, in the pack of a plugin. A build plugin can say `artifactOfForge: <artifact>` in place of a version and has the version Forge knows for that artifact, as `GradlePlugin.builder().id(…).lookupArtifactId(…)` gives; an artifact Forge does not know fails the generation. `versionsOfForge` maps up to 20 artifacts to a version each: the templates of the pack then have `versionsOfForge`, a name that is not of the template API, with the version Forge knows for each artifact or, when it knows none, the one written in the descriptor; asking it for an artifact the descriptor does not name is an error, and a pack without the key has no such name. `build.blocks` maps `afterPlugins`, `afterDependencies` and `end` to text that is added to the build file at that place as it is (`BuildFileContributions.block`); this is the `build.blocks` of the table above, not yet open to the pack of a plugin, and its text is not a template. `readmeLinks` is a list of up to 20 `label` and `href` pairs, each added to the readme with `GeneratorContext.addHelpLink`; `readme` cannot stand in for it, because it ends its text with an empty line and a help link ends with none. The label has no bracket, angle bracket or backslash, and the address is held to what `documentation` is. `shade` and `asciidoctor` are converted with these keys: the two GSP views of Asciidoctor are now templates of its pack, and the applications generated with each of the two features, alone and beside every other visible feature, for the four kinds of application (15,726 files), are the same as before the conversion but for the time of day in the header of a `.properties` file.

**The keys only a pack of Forge has.** Implemented: the descriptor of a pack of Forge can say how its feature takes part in the selection of the features of an application and what it gives beside other features (`PackOfForge`, `PackCondition`), so a compiled feature that is more than dependencies, settings and files can be written as a pack. Like the four keys above, `parseOfForge` reads them and `parse` refuses each, as a key it does not read, in the pack of a plugin; none of them is in the guide, which has what the author of a plugin can use.

- *Selection.* `default: true`, or `default:` with a condition, makes the feature one that is selected without being named (`DefaultFeature.shouldApply` is the condition). `visible: false` hides it. `order` is the name of a `FeaturePhase` in lower case (`lowest`, `low`, `default`, `language`, `test`, `build`, `high`, `highest`). `requiresJdk: 25` refuses a selection with an earlier JDK, with the message the compiled features had ("`<name>` requires JDK 25 or later (selected: JDK 21)."). `adds` is a list of up to 20 features, each a name or a `feature` with a `when`: when the selection is processed each is added, in the order written, if it is not there and its condition holds, whatever kinds of application it is offered for, as a compiled feature adds another with `featureContext.addFeature`; what one addition brings in, the next one sees, and a name Forge has no feature for fails the generation. `reliesOn` is such a list for `getFeaturesReliedOn`, and its `when` can ask for the type of the application only. `requires` stays what `getDependentFeatures()` returned and the hosted Forge reports: a feature that a class added without saying so is written under `adds`.
- *Conditions.* One language wherever a descriptor has a condition: `{ feature: gsp }`, `{ group: database-driver }` (a feature of the exclusive group is there), `{ anyOf: [...] }` and `{ allOf: [...] }` (each entry a feature name or a condition), `{ not: ... }` (a feature name or a condition), `{ applicationType: [web, rest-api] }`, `{ option: { data: hibernate7 } }` (`data`, `servlet` and `reloading`, with the values the create commands take, one or a list), `{ jdkAtLeast: 25 }`. A condition has at most 50 conditions in it, at most 5 deep. It is asked of the features there are at that moment: those selected so far for `default`, those of the selection as it is processed for `adds`, and the features of the application for a contribution.
- *Contributions.* `when` on a dependency, a build plugin and a file. A dependency can be in the scopes `testAndDevelopmentOnly`, `integrationTestImplementationTestFixtures` and `buildscript` (a dependency of the build itself, `addBuildscriptDependency`). A build plugin can say `apply: true` (`useApplyPlugin`). A file can say `template: false` (a `.gsp` that is copied and not rendered) and `executable: true`. `configuration` can be a list in place of the mapping, each entry `values` with an optional `when` and an optional `environmentFile`: the settings of an entry without `environmentFile` go to the configuration of the application, in the order written, and those of an entry with it to the configuration file of that environment (`application-development.yml`; for `test`, `src/test/resources/application-test.yml`, as the framework has it). `build.propertyComments` is up to 20 lines written in front of the pack's build properties. An entry of `build.extensions` can be a list of 1 to 20 parts in place of text, each `text` with an optional `when`: every part whose condition holds is a contribution of its own to the one block, and the lines of the contributions to a block follow one another, so a line in the middle of a block can depend on a feature. A name of a build extension with a call in it (`tasks.withType(Test).configureEach`) is a block of that name and not the task. `guideOfGrails` takes any page under the documentation of the version, `grails-data/graphql/manual/` as well as `guide/async.html`.
- *The data source.* A feature of the group `database-driver` says under `dataSource`, written as a configuration is, the settings it gives the Grails Data implementation that connects through it; the feature that says `dataSourceOfDriver: true` (`gorm-hibernate5`, `gorm-hibernate7`) takes those of the driver of the application in front of its own settings. This is what the Hibernate features read from the class of the driver; a driver is not given its settings by any other feature.

A pack of Forge is a feature of the framework like one written as a class, and like that one it is not held to what the other features set: its dependencies, build properties, settings and destinations are not compared with those of other features and are not looked at again after every feature is applied; where a file may be written is checked by the generator, as it is for every file of the framework. The pack a generation is given is held to all of it, as before, and is the one a refusal names. The engine no longer asks for the class of a converted feature: the features of the option `--servlet` and `--reloading` are those of the exclusive group of the option (`OptionName.getGroup`), the feature that reloads is known by its name (`DevelopmentReloading.getFeatureName`, `ofFeature`), `Features.reloading()` returns a `Feature`, and `GeneratorContext.getOptions()` gives the options the features are applied with. `TestContainers`, `GrailsBase`, `GrailsGradlePlugin`, the two validators of Hibernate and Micronaut and `CreateCommand` ask for the converted features by name.

Converted with these keys, sixteen: `database-migration`, `gorm-async`, `gorm-graphql`, `gorm-hibernate5`, `gorm-hibernate7`, `grails-undertow`, `grails-url-mappings`, `grails-web-testing-support`, `h2`, `mysql`, `postgres`, `spring-boot-devtools`, `spring-boot-starter-jetty`, `spring-boot-starter-tomcat`, `sqlserver`, `views-json`, which makes twenty-nine packs of Forge. The base classes that only they had are gone (`DatabaseDriverFeature`, `DatabaseDriverConfigurationFeature`, `JpaFeature`, `MigrationFeature`, `SpringBootEmbeddedServlet`), and the two families that still have a compiled member say `isFoundByGroup` (`GrailsViews`, `ReloadingFeature`). What was compared before and after, for the four kinds of application: what every feature says of itself, hidden ones included; the defaults alone and every visible feature alone under the default options and under every other value of `--gorm`, `--servlet`, `--reloading` and `--jdk`, each also marked as given; every visible feature excluded; and each of the sixteen beside every other visible feature. That is 5,343 files, 5,082 of them generations, of which 432 are refused with the same message before and after. Every generated file is the same but one: `.grails/forge.lock.yml` names a converted feature with `source: bundled-pack` where it had `source: framework`, and its seal follows; nothing else differs.

Not done: a `when` on a platform, on a build property, on a build block or on a link of the readme; a value of a setting or of a build property that is computed (the package of the project, a version Forge resolves); a destination with the name of a plugin; a platform that is enforced or of the build script; the contract of the engine a feature can be asked for by its type (`ApplicationFeature`, `ConfigurationFeature`, `BuildFeature`). The table says which features need each.

**What each compiled feature needs of a descriptor.** Every feature that is a class under `org.grails.forge.feature` still, thirteen after the four conversion batches that followed (the rows of the thirty-two features those batches made packs are gone from this table; each has a descriptor under `META-INF/grails-forge-bundled` that shows the keys it uses), with the keys its descriptor needs beside the ones every pack has, or what the format cannot say. A base class or interface is deleted with the last compiled feature of its family, and a validator stays a class that asks by name. Thirty-two can be written today; thirteen cannot.

| Feature | Class | Keys of a pack of Forge it needs | What the format lacks |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `base` | `grails/GrailsBase` | | The BOM is one of four, chosen by two features, enforced with Micronaut, with the version `$grailsVersion`, and declared for the build script too (`classpath`): `platforms` has no `when`, no `enforced` and no build script scope. `CreateCommand` calls `GrailsBase.bomArtifactId`. |
| `gradle` | `build/gradle/Gradle` | | It writes the build file, `gradle.properties` and the wrapper from what every other feature contributed (`GradleBuildCreator`): it is the engine of the build file, not a contribution to it. |
| `gradle-build-src` | `build/gradle/GradleBuildSrc` | | As `gradle`: it renders `buildSrc/build.gradle` from the build model, and `GeneratorContext` asks for it by its class to decide where a dependency of the build goes. |
| `gradle-settings-file` | `build/gradle/GradleSettingsFile` | | As `gradle`: it renders `settings.gradle` from the build model. |
| `grails-application` | `lang/groovy/GrailsApplication` | | It is the `ApplicationFeature` of the application (`mainClassName`), which `Features.application()` finds by its type. Its files could be written: templates with `when` on `applicationType`. |
| `grails-dependencies` | `grails/GrailsDefaultPlugins` | | The message bundles of a plugin are written under the name of the plugin (`NameUtils.getPluginName`), which no placeholder of a destination is. |
| `grails-micronaut` | `micronaut/GrailsMicronaut` | `requiresJdk: 25` | The build property `micronautPlatformVersion` has the version Forge resolves for `micronaut-platform` and is left out when it resolves none: a build property is text. `GrailsMicronautValidator` and `GrailsBase` ask for it by name already. |
| `grails-project-settings` | `grails/GrailsProjectSettings` | | The value of `grails.codegen.defaultPackage` is the package of the project: a setting is not a template. |
| `grails-wrapper` | `grailsWrapper/GrailsWrapper` | `default: true`, `visible: false`, `executable: true` | Its three files are not in the sources: the build of `grails-forge-core` copies them from the `grails-wrapper` module into `src/main/resources/grails-wrapper`, and a file of a pack is a resource under the `templates` of the pack. The build would stage them there. |
| `micronaut-http-client` | `micronaut/MicronautHttpClient` | `requiresJdk: 25` | As `grails-micronaut`: the build property with the version Forge resolves. |
| `properties` | `config/Properties` | | It gives the engine the function that writes a configuration as a file (`ConfigurationFeature.createTemplate`). |
| `readme` | `other/Readme` | | It writes the readme from what every other feature added to it. |
| `yaml` | `config/Yaml` | | As `properties`; it is also the default of the group `configuration`. |

A pack with `oneOf` is in that exclusive group. `ProjectGenerator.createGeneratorContext(…, packs)` builds the features of one generation from the framework's and the given packs (`PackFeatures`), so a pack is never registered globally; a pack cannot take the name of a framework feature of any application type, hidden ones included, or of another pack, whatever application types the two are for, and cannot name an exclusive group of the framework yet.

A destination is checked again once its placeholders have their values, which come from the name the application is given (`.{name}/hooks/x` for an application named `git` is refused), by the feature and again by the generator for the project it writes. Before it writes anything the generator refuses, for all features: a path that is absolute or has a `..` step or a backslash; two templates for one file; and a file where another template needs a directory, with placeholders replaced, empty and `.` steps dropped, and the case and composed form of letters ignored. An application name with a backslash is refused by this, with a message about a path; `NameUtils.parse` should refuse it as a name. Before, the second of two templates was silently skipped. The zip output refuses a path that is not inside the archive on its own as well; it does not check the directory it is given as a prefix, which comes from the application name.

A review generated 557,239 selections of framework features without a pack (every single feature, pair and triple for the four application types, each also with every non-default option value) on the code before and after these rules: every output file was identical and nothing that generated before is refused.

The create commands of the Forge CLI (`create-app` and the others) take `--feature-pack-dir <directory>`, any number of times: the pack of each directory is read, named with where it came from before the application is created, and its feature can then be selected with `--features` and is listed by `--list-features`. A directory that holds no usable pack is a mistake in the command line (exit code 2, with every problem of the descriptor), and nothing is created. Only the packs the application is made with are named: the selected ones and those they require. The hosted Forge takes no packs. Under the line that names a pack the command says what it adds, before anything is written: first what of it runs as code, the templates and the build file blocks by name, then every file it writes outside the sources (outside `src/main`, `src/test`, `src/integration-test` and `grails-app` other than `grails-app/conf`) by destination, none left out, the source files it writes, its build plugins, build properties with their values, platforms, dependencies with their excludes, the number of its settings, whether it adds to the readme, and the features it requires and cannot be used with. Every other line names at most 20 things and says how many more there are; a thing longer than 120 characters is cut, and one with a comma in it is put in quotes. The option is in the help and in the guide (`plugins/forgeFeaturePacks.adoc`); `--feature-pack-jar` is hidden. What a descriptor says is shown by the command line, which reads `@|style text|@` as markup, so the parser refuses `@|` in every key and value of a descriptor.

A pack is build code the user trusts: the bodies of its build extensions are Gradle code, it can apply any build plugin, give a version to any dependency the application does not declare itself, and write any file the selected framework features do not write, `settings.gradle`, `buildSrc/**` and `.github/workflows/**` among them. The summary names every one of those files by destination. With `--inplace` a file of a pack that already exists is skipped without a message, as the framework's files are.

Not done yet, or known limits: the compiled pages of the templates of a pack a generation is given are not kept between two generations, and are released only when memory is needed; there are no precompiled templates for the packs of Forge, no class loader of its own for a pack and no timeout (what is done for templates is under "Template language and compatibility" in section 2.2); the pack's version and coordinates, the operating system and the language are not in the model; `flags`, parameters and `when` are not in the model or the descriptor; build extension bodies and the readme are not templates; `instructions` are not shown; a requirement on the feature of another pack by `group:artifact#name` does not resolve; two framework features that set one setting differently are still not compared, only a pack's settings are; build extensions of a pack are added to the block of another feature without being compared; the executable bit of a pack's file is not kept; and a process that rewrites a pack's directory while it is read can still get a link past the loader, or stall it with a named pipe.

**Feature identity and selection.** The adapter is not the entire engine change. Before step 1 `FeatureContext.isPresent(Class)` and `OneOfFeatureValidator` used class assignability; `addFeature` still recurses immediately into selection. Step 1 gave every exclusive family a string group ID (`OneOfFeature.getGroup()`; the IDs are `cache-provider`, `configuration`, `data-nosql`, `database-driver`, `embedded-servlet`, `geb`, `gradle-build-src`, `gradle-settings-file`, `api-views`, `gsp-layout`, `logging`, `migration`, `reloading`, `spring-ai-provider`, `spring-security` and `threading`), which the validator, the registry and the web API's `oneOfGroup` now use, and added `FeatureContext.isPresent(String)` and `isGroupPresent(String)`. Defaults that look for a family by type (`H2`, `Yaml`, `Logback`, `GebWithTestcontainers`, `Sitemesh3`, `ViewJson`, the Hibernate driver check) still do, and must move to group IDs before a pack can join one of those groups. The new shared contract is:

- Framework feature IDs retain their existing names. External IDs are `<group>:<artifact>#<name>`; short names are accepted only when unambiguous. A pack cannot shadow a framework ID, and two versions of the same pack cannot coexist. Errors name the contributing artifacts. External requirements identify a canonical feature ID and an exact pack version or a unique catalog resolution, which is then locked.
- Exclusive groups are stable string IDs, with framework-owned IDs for existing groups and namespaced IDs for external groups. Adapt compiled features to the same group registry before converting any to packs; do not generate Java classes to represent descriptor groups. Retain typed access only where a compiled feature genuinely provides a typed implementation.
- Resolve explicit features and explicit option choices first, expand mandatory requirements with a visited set, then choose optional defaults for unfilled groups. A cycle among requirements is a mutual requirement and is accepted: its features are selected together; a feature that requires itself is refused when its descriptor is read. An explicit incompatible choice fails rather than being silently replaced. `--exclude-features` can suppress optional defaults but cannot remove a mandatory requirement. Preset defaults participate below explicit user choices.
- Finish selection, including requirements introduced by defaults, to a bounded fixed point; then validate compatibility, conflicts and exclusive groups before any template executes. Evaluate contribution predicates and flags against this final set. Feature-dependent negative predicates are allowed for contributions, not for adding/removing requirements, to avoid oscillating selection.
- Process each feature once per stage. Deterministic order is phase/order, then canonical ID; required collaborators must be resolved before contribution processing. Compiled hooks that add features must use this shared resolver rather than bypassing it. Test a cycle, a diamond dependency, excluded requirements and mixed compiled/pack groups.

Implemented: `ContextFactory.createFeatureContext` selects to a fixed point: the named features, what they require (`getDependentFeatures()`), then the defaults for what nothing has chosen, each with what it requires as soon as it is selected, until a round adds nothing, which is after at most as many rounds as there are features. The defaults are looked at in one order: those that are in no exclusive group first, those that require something before those that do not, then by name; so what a default requires of an exclusive group is there before the group is given its own default (a default that requires `postgres` gets no `h2`). A cycle among requirements is a mutual requirement and is accepted: a feature is looked at once, so features that require each other, directly or in a longer circle, are selected together whichever of them is named, since a requirement says only that one feature is not without the other and puts them in no order. The parser refuses a feature that requires itself. A requirement that cannot be met names the way that led to it (`acme-a requires acme-b requires acme-c`), a long way by its first and last three features and the number between them. What is refused does not depend on the order of the names: names that are no features are all reported, sorted, at most ten of them, for the selected and for the excluded names. Every refusal of a selection is a `FeatureSelectionException`, which is an `IllegalArgumentException` as before, whichever validator found it; the create commands report it as a mistake in the command line (exit code 2), and a failure while the features are applied, such as a template that fails, stays an error (exit code 1). A name that was given is shown in a message through `FeatureSelectionException.shown`: one line, control and format characters replaced, `@|` broken, at most 80 characters. The `conflicts` of every pack are checked against the final features, with the defaults and with what compiled features add in `processSelectedFeatures`, when the context is made: before any feature is applied and so before any template runs, and for a caller that only builds the context. The message names the way to the feature that conflicts when another feature brought it in (`The feature acme-x cannot be used with the feature database-migration (acme-x requires acme-y requires database-migration)`). `PackFeature.apply` still checks too. A name among the conflicts of a pack that is no feature of the application, or no feature at all, conflicts with nothing and is not reported: a pack can name a feature another type of application has. `ProjectGenerator.createGeneratorContext(…, packs, excludedFeatures)` and `--exclude-features` of the create commands leave a default out, and with it what only that default brought in. An exclusion is refused when the name is no feature of this type of application, is a hidden feature, is selected too, is required by a feature, through `getDependentFeatures()` or by adding it in `processSelectedFeatures` (`FeatureContext.addFeature` refuses it and names the feature that was being processed), or when the application would be without a hidden feature it has when nothing is excluded: hidden features are what an application is built on, not what it can choose. `views-json` therefore cannot be excluded from a REST API, where it alone brings in `grails-web` and with it the Grails Gradle plugin, unless another selected feature brings that in. An exclusion is also refused when what a feature that remains generates makes use of the excluded one. A feature says what it relies on in `Feature.getFeaturesReliedOn(ApplicationType)`, none by default; this is not a requirement, which selection would add, and it is looked at only for a feature that was excluded, so no selection without exclusions is changed by it. The URL mappings rely on `gsp` in a web application and a web plugin (they map `/`, `500` and `404` to GSP views) and on `asset-pipeline-grails` in a web application (the icon); `gsp` relies on `asset-pipeline-grails` (its layout and pages use the asset tags); `sitemesh3` and the Geb features rely on `gsp`. The message names the feature that relies on the excluded one and the way to it (`The feature asset-pipeline-grails cannot be excluded: what the feature gsp generates relies on it (acme-pages requires gsp)`). The set was found by generating every type of application with each default excluded alone and in pairs, searching the generated files for the tags, plugins, classes and settings of the excluded feature, and building and running the applications: without `gsp` a web application answered `/` with 500, without `asset-pipeline-grails` it wrote the asset tags into the page as text; the exclusions that remain accepted (`mockito`, `scaffolding`, `spock`, `geb-with-testcontainers`, and their pairs) built, passed their tests and answered `/`. `FeatureSelectionSpec` holds every view and mapping of every accepted exclusion to the features that are there: no tag of a namespace, no view of a layer and no mapping to a view or to the assets whose feature is absent. The feature of the Grails Data implementation the option names cannot be excluded either (`gorm-hibernate5` by default), because the options and the record of a project cannot say that there is none; the message says to choose another with `--data`. The implementation the option does not name can be excluded. The exclusions are `FeatureContext.exclude(String)`, on the predicates `exclude(FeaturePredicate)` already had. No exclusive group is mandatory in itself; a group is needed where a feature needs it, so excluding `h2` is refused because `gorm-hibernate5` adds a driver. The excluded names are in `GeneratorContext.getExcludedFeatures()` and, when there are any, in `grails-forge-cli.yml` as `excludedFeatures`; a file without exclusions is byte for byte what it was. `ProjectStateRecorder` writes them, sorted and each once, as `excludedFeatures` of `.grails/project.yml`, in the shape of `requestedFeatures`, and `CodeGenConfig.getExcludedFeatures()` gives them from the manifest and, for a project without usable state, from `grails-forge-cli.yml`. `--exclude-features` takes names: an entry with a colon, such as the coordinates of a plugin, is a mistake in the command line and is said before a repository is asked. The feature of a plugin that is selected by its coordinates is excluded, required and checked against the options by the name of its feature. `--list-features` checks the exclusions as creation does and marks the defaults without what is excluded. An option that is given is a choice, in the generator: `Options.withGiven` takes the options that were given (`OptionName`: `DATA`, `SERVLET`, `RELOADING`), and `ContextFactory.createGeneratorContext` refuses an application that would be without the feature a given option names, or, for a given `none`, with a feature of that kind (`--servlet none` with a pack that requires Jetty). The create commands mark the options that are on the command line, and their message names what was typed (`The option --gorm hibernate does not agree …`); the hosted Forge answers 400 with the message of the generator. Over HTTP an option counts as given only when its value is not the default (`ForgeRequestSupport.given`, used by `ForgeRequestSupport.options` and `CreateProjectService`): the Forge UI sends `gorm`, `servlet` and `reloading` with every request, also when the user left them as they were, so a parameter that repeats the default is the same as leaving it out, and ticking `gorm-hibernate7` in the UI gives a Hibernate 7 application as before. On the command line an option that is typed is given, whatever its value. A caller that marks nothing has defaults for the servlet container and the reloading, and those give way: `ContextFactory.standsAside` leaves out the default servlet container and the default reloader when the option was not given and a feature of the same group was selected or is required (a pack that requires Jetty gives an application with Jetty and without Tomcat; before, also before this work, that was refused as two of one group). The Grails Data implementation is treated otherwise, by decision: only the plain default, Hibernate 5, gives way, to a selected Hibernate 7, as it always did; a value of the option that is not the default never stands aside, whether it was marked as given or not, so `Options(MONGODB)` with `gorm-neo4j`, `Options(HIBERNATE7)` with `gorm-hibernate5` and `Options(NEO4J)` with `gorm-mongodb` are refused through the generator as they were. A caller of the generator that passes an implementation other than the default has no other way to say that it chose it, and a persistence layer that is swapped without a word is a worse surprise than a refusal. The defaults that no option chooses (database driver, configuration format, logging, layout, Geb, JSON views) already looked for a feature of their group. A given option that asks for none names the way to the feature that is there all the same. An option whose value names a feature the type of application has not, as Tomcat for a plugin, chooses nothing and is not checked; a JDK the generator does not support is a mistake in the command line like any other value an option cannot have. What is recorded of the options is what the application has (`GeneratorContext.getApplicationOptions()`): `grails-forge-cli.yml`, `.grails/project.yml`, the lock and the `options` a template of a pack sees name the implementation that is there (`gorm-hibernate7` for `--features gorm-hibernate7`), and `none` where the application has no feature of the kind: the reloading of an application with `grails-micronaut`, and the servlet container of a plugin, which has none unless one is asked for (`servlet: tomcat` was recorded for every plugin before). The Grails Data implementation is always one the application has; there is no value for none, and its feature cannot be excluded. The features themselves are still applied with the options as they were given or defaulted. Of 84,622 selections of framework features without exclusions (every feature and pair for the four types and every option value) every selection that was accepted has the same features and the same generated files as before this change, and every selection that was refused is refused with the same message; the one difference is the servlet line of the record of 1,244 plugins. `FeatureSelectionSpec` generates five selections with packs, in every order of the selected and excluded names and with the packs given in reverse, and compares every file; and for every visible default of every type of application, excluded alone, it holds the result to what an application of the type always has: every hidden feature, the Gradle plugins of Grails in the build file, the configuration, the application class, and a record that names a Grails Data implementation the application has.

Not done: an exclusion that the selection cannot be made without (a pack that conflicts with a default, which is selected with that default excluded) is not held to the hidden features of the whole application, because there is no whole application to compare with. A feature that is not for the type of application, or is hidden, is refused as one that does not exist, without saying why. What a compiled feature adds in `processSelectedFeatures` counts as required, also where it is a convenience (`testcontainers` with `postgres`), because the hook cannot say which it is. `addFeature` still recurses into selection as it is called; the compiled hooks have not moved to the resolver. A default that begins to apply only in a later round, because of another default, can still meet a group default that an earlier round selected. The options cannot say that an application has no Grails Data implementation. The hosted Forge takes no exclusions, and cannot tell an option the user chose from one that repeats the default. What a feature relies on is said by the compiled features only: the descriptor of a pack has no key for it. A feature that is selected into a type of application that has not by default what the feature relies on (`gsp` in a REST API, whose pages use the asset tags) is generated as before, without it. Presets, group requirements with a default (`oneOf: { group, default }`), external feature IDs, and `when` predicates on requirements are not in the descriptor.

**Template language and compatibility.** External packs use GSP source, compiled by the CLI in a pack-scoped engine; hosted Forge renders only bundled official templates. Keep `trimLogicLines="true"` and `expressionCodec="none"`. Introduce a versioned public pack model (`templateApi: 1`) exposing project, final feature IDs, options, flags and declared parameters. Existing templates that import internal Forge model classes must migrate to that API; source compilation alone does not make them version-independent.

Descriptor format, template API, supported Forge versions and target Grails versions are separate compatibility checks. Runtime `grailsVersion` metadata is an additional filter, not a substitute for generator compatibility. Publish the range/comparator rules, including prereleases; snapshots require explicit selection and a compatible declaration. A pack is supported only on the combinations its author tests. No promise is made that one source pack works on every runtime line its plugin supports.

Bundled official packs are precompiled with the matching Forge build. External source is compiled on first use and cached by artifact/source digest, exact Forge/GSP/Groovy versions, template API and Java compilation target. Local-directory content changes invalidate the cache. This is a separate source-capable engine/locator path: toggling `ForgePages` development mode does not itself supply file lookup, class isolation or persistent caching. Verbatim resources remain byte-preserving copies, with optional `executable: true` and explicit `binary: true`; text is UTF-8 unless declared binary.

GSP avoids a wholesale language rewrite, but conversion still needs model-API adaptation and selection/output tests. Build-time descriptor validation and GSP check compilation catch structural and type errors; generation tests catch rendering behavior. First-use compilation cost must be measured. Pack code must render deterministically from declared inputs; replay comparison in section 2.6 detects drift but is not a sandbox.

There is one template engine in v1. A future logic-less engine would require its own format/API decision and is not an automatic fallback for incompatible GSP packs.

Implemented (the templates of packs, step 1): A template is compiled once for a `PackPages`, however many destinations its source has and however often it is rendered; the compiled page is kept by the SHA-256 of the name of its feature, the directives and the source. The packs a generation is given keep a `PackPages` of their own for that generation, so a page compiled from the source of another party is never rendered by a second generation, and two packs do not share a page even when their sources are the same text: a compiled page keeps the page object each thread ran last, and with it the writer of that run, behind a soft reference (`GroovyPageMetaInfo`), and its class is code of the pack. The packs Forge has in itself share one `PackPages` for as long as the application context of Forge is there (`BundledPackFeatures`), made when the first of their templates is rendered. It holds as many pages as those packs have templates (two today, both of `asciidoctor`), since only the packs of the index of Forge are rendered with it, and `BundledPackFeatures.getCompiledTemplates()` says how many. It is used by many threads at once: one page is compiled at a time, a page is published only when it is compiled, and rendering takes no lock, which is how GSP renders one compiled page for many requests. What the encoders of GSP look their state up with, which is one for the program, is now a lookup with a state for each thread, where it was the context of whichever template was rendered first. Measured in the test JVM on the development machine, 200 generations of a web application with `asciidoctor` after a warm-up: on one thread 3,690 ms and 881 classes loaded before, 644 ms and 96 after (the same 200 without the feature take 640 to 700 ms and load about 400 classes, which are not of templates); on eight threads 1,207 ms and 803 classes before, 162 ms and 2 after. The pages of the packs a generation is given are still unloaded, and as before only when memory is needed: 9,000 generations with a pack of two templates under `-XX:MaxMetaspaceSize=200m` load 72,612 classes and end with 59,233 of them unloaded and the metaspace at 65 MiB; it grows by about 33 KiB a generation to some 180 MiB and is then emptied to 50 to 60 MiB, twice in that run.

An error in a template names the line of the template. `line 3: ` stands in front of what a scriptlet threw (in a closure, in the body of an instruction, in the test of one, and for a stack overflow too), of a compile error, which reads `it does not compile: Missing '}'` without the name and the line of the generated class, and of what the parser refuses in a tag; an error in an expression names its line itself, as before. The line is looked up in the mapping the GSP parser keeps for a page (`GroovyPageMetaInfo.getLineNumbers()`), with the line of the generated class the compiler names (`ExceptionUtils.extractLineNumber`, as the engine does) or the first frame of the class of the page in the stack trace. That mapping was wrong for most templates and is put right in grails-gsp: in `GroovyPageScanner` a token on the last line was reported on line 1 and what follows a comment too early, and in `GSPWriter` the line breaks inside an expression or a scriptlet of several lines were not counted and an entry was lost each time the table of lines grew, at 1,000, 2,000 and 4,000 generated lines. What an error is, is asked of everything it was caused by, since a page hands on what an expression throws inside an exception of its own: writing too much and a stack overflow are said as such however deep they are, an error of the virtual machine is passed on as it is, and an interrupt leaves the thread interrupted. A template the parser refuses is kept like one that does not compile, so it is compiled once. Known limits: for a scriptlet or an expression of several lines the line is the one it starts on, and what is only found missing where the template ends (a block or an instruction left open) is reported on its last line.

What the generator reads in a template is fixed and is all in the guide: an expression `${}`; what starts with `<%` (scriptlet, `<%=`, directive, comment); what starts with `%{` (scriptlet, comment); and the ten instruction tags `g:if`, `g:elseif`, `g:else`, `g:unless`, `g:each`, `g:while`, `g:collect`, `g:findAll`, `g:grep` and `g:def`, which are exactly the tags the GSP compiler compiles into a page and stay in the namespace `g`. Everything else is text and is written byte for byte. A GSP page reads more than that, so before a template is compiled `PackTemplateSource` has the page write, with a scriptlet `<%='<'%>`, the first character of everything else a page would read: every other `<prefix:name>`, start or end, in `g` or any other namespace (a page without tag libraries would evaluate its attributes and write it anew); the short directive `@{ }`, which every view scaffolding generates starts with; the declaration `!{ }!`; and the form `^name%{ }%`, for which a page takes the HTML tag around it out of its text. It walks the source as the scanner of GSP pages reads it, so a scriptlet, an expression, a comment, a directive and an instruction tag are never touched, and it adds and removes no line break. Backslashes directly in front of what the generator reads, and in front of any tag, are an escape two by two: each two write one, and one left over has what follows written and not read, so `\<%` writes `<%`, `\\${x}` writes a backslash and the value, and `\<g:link>` writes the tag as `<g:link>` does, which makes it safe to escape every tag and lets the list of instructions grow without changing what an escaped template writes. The page itself never sees a backslash in front of an expression. Two attributes of an instruction are refused with the line: `env`, which would ask for the environment of the generator (production in the command line, development in a check), and a `test` that is not one `${}`, which is text and so always true. A template is compiled with `grailsLayoutPreprocess="false"` and `compileStatic="false"`. Measured against the scanner itself with 2.2 million made-up sources, a page reads no tag that is no instruction, no `@{` and no `!{`, and has the line breaks of its source; what is left is `^name%{ }%` inside the code of a scriptlet, an expression or an attribute of an instruction, with an HTML tag of that name beside it, which cannot be rewritten without changing the code. `PackTemplateViewsSpec` makes a template of each of 31 views copied from the sources of Grails under the resources of the tests, chosen for what a page reads in them (the four of scaffolding with their `@{ model }` line, layouts, tags of plugins, scriptlets, comments, maps in attributes), the way the guide says, with the instruction tags escaped and with every tag escaped, and has each written back byte for byte; with `-PallRepositoryViews` it does so for every `.gsp` file of the sources. The two that start what is read with a percent sign overlap in `<%{`: an escaped `<%` writes the `%` as well when a `{` follows, so that `\<%{`, which is what the way of the guide makes of `<%{`, writes those three characters and leaves no scriptlet that would take the text up to the next `}%` out. The guide and the Javadoc of `PackModel` are inputs of the tests of `grails-forge-core`, the examples of the guide are run from the guide, and in a checkout a guide that is not where it was fails those specs. A page is one method, and a method has a size: about 1,500 expressions, 2,000 instruction tags or 3,800 tags that are text fit (1,926 pairs of a start and an end tag, where the page of a tag that was evaluated held 898), and a template over that is told so in words, as is one nested too deeply for the compiler. `PackCheck` runs in the production environment when it is started for a check, as the command line that generates does.

The descriptor keeps the `templateApi` it declares (`PackDescriptor.templateApi`), `PackModel.of` makes the model for that number and is the one place a template API is added, and `PackModel.APIS` is what the parser accepts: any other number is refused with `templateApi: must be one of the template APIs this version of Forge renders, [1], but is the number 2`. `PackTemplateApiSpec` holds the names a template is given, the table in the Javadoc of `PackModel` and the table of the guide to each other.

The templates of the packs of Forge are compiled when Forge is built (revision 50); a template of any other pack is compiled once in each run. Forge renders every template in production, its own environment, whatever environment its JVM is given (revision 52). GSP writes an expression one way in development and test and another way in every other environment, and a compiled page of the build is taken only by a run in production. A `grails.env` or `GRAILS_ENV` that reaches the command line, `checkForgePack` or `forgePackTest` is meant for a project, and the commands that run a project's Gradle build hand that build its environment; `ForgeEnvironment` sets Forge's own over it. Not done: a compiled page of a pack a generation is given is not kept between two generations or two runs of the command line (no cache by artifact digest on disk); a pack has no class loader of its own; there is no sandbox and no timeout; `^name%{ }%` inside the code of a template is still taken for the form a page strips a tag for; a template cannot ask for a tag to be run that is no instruction, since the generator has no tag libraries; and the model has nothing new (no `flags`, parameters or version of the pack).

**Precedents.** Quarkus extensions declare `metadata.codestart` in `quarkus-extension.yaml`, ship the codestart in a `codestarts` classifier jar (`codestart.yml` plus Qute templates), and users select them with `quarkus create app -x group:artifact:version`. Spring Initializr keeps its dependency catalog declarative (`initializr.dependencies` with `compatibilityRange`, `bom`, `repository`) and reserves code (`ProjectContributor`, `BuildCustomizer`) for the service itself. Grails 3 profiles' `feature.yml` plus `skeleton/` is the same shape as this proposal; it lived in the wrong engine.

**Conditional logic in packs.** Selection and model contributions use descriptor predicates; GSP templates may branch on the resolved model. These are three distinct uses:

1. *Auto-adding a collaborator*: Hibernate 7 requires a database driver, Geb requires Spock, Scaffolding requires GSP, and Spring Security UI requires Spring Security. Resolve these before rendering; a group requirement chooses its default only after explicit selections have been considered:

   ```
   requires:
     - gsp
     - oneOf: { group: database-driver, default: h2 }
   ```
2. *Conditional contributions* (`apply`): `TestContainers` adds `testcontainers-spock` only when Spock is present and `testcontainers-mongodb` only when a Mongo feature is (`database/TestContainers.groovy:81-87`). That is a `when` predicate on a dependency, file or configuration entry, so the entry exists or does not; no template logic is involved:

   ```
   dependencies:
      - { scope: testImplementation, coordinates: 'org.testcontainers:testcontainers-spock', when: { feature: spock } }
      - { scope: testImplementation, coordinates: 'org.testcontainers:testcontainers-mongodb', when: { anyOf: [gorm-mongodb, mongo-sync] } }
   files:
     - { from: MongoSpec.groovy.gsp, to: 'src/integration-test/groovy/{packagePath}/MongoSpec.groovy', when: { feature: gorm-mongodb } }
   ```
3. *Branching inside a file*: the security `user.gsp` emits a `collection 'users'` mapping for MongoDB and a `table 'users'` mapping for either Hibernate (`feature/security/template/user.gsp:41-50`). Pack templates are GSP, so this is written exactly as it is today, and selection finishes before rendering starts, so the `features` list a template sees is final:

   ```
   <% if (features.contains('gorm-mongodb')) { %>
       static mapping = {
           collection 'users'
       }
   <% } else if (features.contains('gorm-hibernate5') || features.contains('gorm-hibernate7')) { %>
       static mapping = {
           table 'users'
       }
   <% } %>
   ```

   A descriptor may still name a condition once as a flag and reuse it in several files (`<% if (flags.sql) { %>`), which keeps templates short, but nothing requires it:

   ```
   flags:
     sql: { anyOf: [gorm-hibernate5, gorm-hibernate7] }
   ```

One predicate vocabulary serves contribution `when` and `flags`: `feature`, `anyOf`, `allOf`, `not`, `applicationType`, and `option` (for example `option: { data: hibernate7 }`). Requirements may be conditional on immutable inputs such as application type and explicit options, not on the absence of a feature still being resolved. Configuration supports both unconditional mappings and conditional entries with `when` and `values`; normalize both into the same configuration model. Declare parameter names, types, defaults and required status in the schema and reject unknown parameters before rendering. Parameters used for repeatable generation are non-secret values recorded in the lock.

Framework-owned decisions such as BOM and Gradle-plugin selection stay compiled, but contribute through the same build model as packs. Move feature-name branches out of the central build template into their owning contributors.
