Packs cannot replace another feature's templates implicitly; conflicting output paths are diagnosed before writing.

**How the template decision changed.** Revisions 1 to 5 of this plan recommended Mustache, on the grounds that a pack had to be safe to render on start.grails.org. Once external packs became a CLI-only capability (section 2.7), the remaining arguments were weighed again:

- *Precompiled external pages* bind to a Groovy/GSP binary stack. Shipping source avoids that artifact coupling, while the versioned model API and compatibility tests address source compatibility. Author-side check compilation still needs the compiler in an isolated tool process.
- *Code execution* is accepted in the CLI, including during `feature diff` and `--dry-run`. This executes author code earlier than building the generated project. The CLI identifies the source artifact and checksum before first use; it does not describe packs as sandboxed.
- *Reviewability and tooling*: descriptors can be inspected without executing templates. Check compilation and rendering execute compiler/template code; an in-memory output handler cannot prevent arbitrary GSP side effects. Dry run promises no application of the planned project changes, not no execution or no cache writes.
- *Whitespace* is handled by the `trimLogicLines` page directive that already exists for Forge's templates.

The hosted-service exclusion is unchanged. Supporting another engine or community packs on the hosted service is outside this design.

**Build-file contributions.** `build.gradle` is the file every pack wants to touch, and it is where the current design is weakest. Three mechanisms coexisted before step 1 (the line numbers are those of the template before it): the model (dependencies, plugins, repositories, buildscript, properties); one generic slot, `${gradleBuild.renderExtensions()}` after the dependencies block (`feature/build/gradle/templates/buildGradle.gsp:107`), fed by `GradlePlugin.extension(Writable)`; and eight branches hard-coded in the central template on feature names (`gradle-build-src`, `asciidoctor`, `jrebel`, `spock`, `geb-with-local-browsers`, `grails-compile-static`, `gsp`, `asset-pipeline-grails`, `buildGradle.gsp:40-122`) plus the BOM choice in `buildSrcBuildGradle.gsp:50-58`. The hard-coded branches are the "everyone edits the central file" problem in miniature, and a pack cannot use them at all. There was also a silent drop: `Dependency.Builder.extension(Writable)` was accepted and never rendered, so the `sourceSets` block `DatabaseMigrationPlugin` passed to it never reached the build file. That block is not needed, because the Grails Gradle plugin has made `grails-app/migrations` a resources directory since Grails 7, so the hook and its template were removed rather than wired up (step 1).

The rule for packs: no pack ships a `build.gradle` template or a text patch. The build file stays generated from a model, and packs contribute through typed channels that Forge merges:

| Channel | Renders | Merge rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `dependencies`, `platforms` | lines in `dependencies {}` | validate before deduplication; key by group, artifact, scope and dependency kind (ordinary/platform/enforced platform); conflicting versions or exclusion sets fail with provenance rather than silently losing a contribution |
| `buildPlugins` (`id`, `version`, `apply`) | a `plugins {}` entry or an `apply plugin:` line | same id collapses; different versions error |
| `build.repositories`, `build.buildscript` | `repositories {}`, `buildscript {}` | by URL or coordinate |
| `build.properties` | `gradle.properties` | same key and value collapse; different values error |
| `build.imports` | import lines at the top of the file | set |
| `build.settings` | settings contributions at named structural locations, including `pluginManagement` repositories | merge repository entries by normalized URL; keep `pluginManagement` in Gradle's required leading position; arbitrary fragments are restricted to the end |
| `build.extensions.<name>` | one `<name> { }` block per key with the bodies concatenated; `tasks.<task>` keys render `tasks.named('<task>') { }` | merged across packs and framework features, in feature order |
| `build.blocks` | a free-form fragment at an anchor: `afterPlugins`, `afterDependencies`, `end` | ordered, never merged; the escape hatch for `sourceSets {}` or a custom task |

Every fragment is a GSP with the versioned pack model. Ordering follows the shared phase/order and canonical-ID ordering, then descriptor declaration order. Since step 1 `FeatureContext` processes, returns and applies features by order and then name; before it, equal-order features came out of a hash set in an order that varied between runs. Existing `Dependency.equals` ignores versions and dependency kind; conflict detection must happen before insertion into the current set. Configuration mappings follow the same provenance rule: identical leaves collapse, conflicting leaves or scalar/map/list shapes fail; list replacement/concatenation is never inferred.

Implemented so far in step 1 (`BuildFileContributions`, reached through `GeneratorContext.getBuildContributions()`): `extension(name, body)` for `build.extensions`, where the name is the start of the block as written, and `task(taskName, body)` for `tasks.named('<task>') { }` (the descriptor's `tasks.<task>` key maps to it); `block(anchor, text)` for `build.blocks` at `AFTER_PLUGINS`, `AFTER_DEPENDENCIES` and `END`; and an error for a contribution made after the build file is rendered. `GeneratorContext.applyFeatures` now applies each feature once. Asciidoctor, JRebel, Spock (with the Geb local-browser lines), compile-static (with its GSP option) and asset-pipeline (with its web-plugin and OpenAPI lines) contribute their own blocks, and `buildGradle.gsp` no longer branches on them. The generated blocks are unchanged, but they now come in feature order (`assets`, `grails`, `tasks.named('bootRun')`, `tasks.withType(Test)`) where the template had them in a fixed order, and the Asciidoctor `apply from:` line is now followed by an empty line. The conditions on other features moved with the blocks: Spock adds the Geb lines, compile-static the GSP option, and asset-pipeline the OpenAPI exclusion, so no two framework features merge into one extension yet. The old `GradlePlugin` extension slot (`GradleBuild.renderExtensions()`) still exists next to the new channel and is rendered after it; no framework feature uses it. Still to move: `build.properties`, `build.imports`, `build.settings`, `build.repositories` and `build.buildscript` as channels, the `gradle-build-src` branch around the `buildscript` block, and the BOM choice in `buildSrcBuildGradle.gsp`.

The framework's own features move to these channels in step 1: asset-pipeline contributes `assets {}`, compile-static contributes `grails {}`, Spock and Geb contribute the test task configuration (`tasks.withType(Test).configureEach`), asciidoctor and jrebel contribute their blocks, and `GrailsBase` contributes the BOM. The central template owns structure only. Stable rendering reduces unrelated diffs; it does not guarantee that edits to a user's Gradle script merge cleanly merely because a block still exists.

The supported contribution API does not allow a pack to change framework BOM selection, reorder framework plugins, change the Java toolchain or delete another feature's contributions. Free-form Groovy fragments cannot enforce those semantic restrictions statically; they remain trusted author code and require generated-build tests. Prefer structured channels and provide targeted lint diagnostics for recognized patterns, not a promise to understand arbitrary Gradle code. Detect file-path collisions across all features, presets and framework outputs before rendering to the destination.

<a id="metadata"></a>
### 2.3 Plugin metadata: catalog plus pack

**Where a pack lives, and the Gradle plugin that publishes it.** Inside the plugin's main jar under `META-INF/grails-forge/`. Authoring and publishing are automated by the Grails Gradle plugins, the same way `grails-plugin-cli` automates `-cli` companions today: a plugin author keeps the pack in `src/main/forge/` (`feature.yml` plus `templates/`), and the `grails-plugin` Gradle plugin, when that directory exists, does the rest:

- validates `feature.yml` against the descriptor schema (unknown keys, predicates, compatibility ranges, parameter types, output paths and references). Validate framework references against the declared generator version; resolve external references from declared pack inputs or a test catalog, not just the bundled public index, so private packs remain authorable offline;
- check-compiles GSP sources against the declared template API in an isolated tool JVM. Gradle's embedded Groovy must not load Forge's application Groovy/GSP stack. Compiler dependencies are task tooling, not dependencies of the plugin's runtime publication;
- packages the directory into `META-INF/grails-forge/` of the main jar and stamps `Grails-Forge-Pack: <name>`. The manifest is a detection hint inside the jar, not a separate descriptor or a guarantee of a partial network download;
- registers an opt-in `forgePackTest` task that invokes Forge in an isolated JVM, generates supported application-type/option fixtures, and builds them using their wrapper. Task inputs include generator coordinates, source digests, options and fixture repositories; configuration-cache reuse must work. A preset-only publication uses the same JAR layout without implying an application runtime dependency;
- with `grails-publish` applied, needs nothing more: the pack travels with the plugin's existing publication.

Implemented so far (step 1): the packaging only. `GrailsPluginGradlePlugin.configureForgePack` registers `copyForgePack`, which stages `feature.yml` and the files under `templates` of `src/main/forge` (nothing else of that directory, and no empty directories) as `META-INF/grails-forge/` on the output of the main source set, in a directory of its own so the GSP templates are not dropped by the exclusion that keeps views out of `processResources`; the jar's manifest gets `Grails-Forge-Pack` with the `name` of the descriptor, read as YAML, and a descriptor that is not YAML or whose name is not one a pack can have fails the build. So does a pack Forge could not read from the jar: files without a `feature.yml`, a link under `templates`, and a file Gradle leaves out of every copy (`.gitignore`, `.DS_Store`, backup files), which is found by comparing what was staged with what is there. Gradle looks at neither those files nor links as inputs, and with the configuration cache reused nothing else would see one that is added, so `copyForgePack` is never up to date: the pack is staged and checked in every build. The descriptor is at most 256 KiB here as in Forge, and the name is read with every value as written, so `yes` and `on` are names as they are for Forge. The descriptor is read as an input of the configuration, so adding or renaming a pack is seen with the configuration cache on, and any edit of the descriptor discards the cache entry. The pack is on the runtime classpath of the plugin as a resource, as its templates are. Not done: validation of the descriptor and check-compilation of the templates in a tool JVM, and `forgePackTest`. Forge reads what this packages: `PackLoader.fromJar` takes `META-INF/grails-forge/feature.yml` and the files the descriptor names under `META-INF/grails-forge/templates/` from a jar, with the limits of a directory pack; the manifest attribute is not looked at and a signature is not verified. The jar is read into memory, at most 64 MiB of it, and goes through the one reader of jars described under "Implemented: direct coordinates" below, which takes a jar only in one form and checks every entry of it: there is no second way of reading a jar, and a jar given as a file is refused for what a jar taken by coordinates is refused for. Both loaders stop after 50 problems and once the pack is over its 16 MiB, and count a file each time the descriptor names it. The create commands take such a jar with the hidden `--feature-pack-jar`. Finding the jar from the coordinates of a plugin, and recording its checksum, is in the paragraph "Implemented: direct coordinates" below.

Implemented: the check of a pack by its author. `GrailsPluginGradlePlugin.configureForgePackCheck` registers `checkForgePack` (`CheckForgePackTask`), which `check` depends on and `jar` and `assemble` do not: the jar is built without Forge being resolved, and `check` and `build` fail for a pack Forge could not use. The task starts `org.apache.grails.forge.core.pack.PackCheck` with `ExecOperations.javaexec`, in a JVM of the project's toolchain whose class path is the `grailsForgePackTooling` configuration and nothing else, so none of Forge's Groovy or GSP is loaded into Gradle; a launch of a JVM and not the Worker API, because the action of a worker is a class of this plugin, which is Groovy written against Gradle's API, and would have to be loaded in the worker's JVM beside Forge's Groovy. The configuration is resolvable only, is left out of the BOM the Grails plugin gives the other configurations, and by default has `org.apache.grails.forge:grails-forge-core` in the `grailsVersion` of the project; a build that declares a dependency on it replaces the default. The default is added only when `src/main/forge` exists, which is read as an input of the configuration, so a plugin without a pack resolves nothing and skips the task, and a pack added later is seen with the configuration cache on. The task is told the directory, reads the problems from the standard output, one on a line, in bounds and without control characters, and takes exit 0 for a pack without problems, 1 with at least one line for problems and anything else for a check that failed. Its inputs are the tooling class path, the launcher and a digest of the pack that a `ValueSource` (`ForgePackStateValueSource`) computes at execution in every build: the descriptor and every entry under `templates` by path and kind, a file with its content and its number of names, a link with its target, links not followed. Gradle's own file inputs leave out `.gitignore` and the like and do not tell a link from a file, which is why `copyForgePack` is never up to date; with the digest `checkForgePack` is up to date for an unchanged pack and runs again for a file Gradle does not look at or a file replaced by a link to it, with the configuration cache reused. It is not in the build cache. `PackCheck.check(Path)` reads the directory with `PackLoader.fromDirectory` and gives its problems as they are; then it gives a problem for a `compatibility` range that leaves out the Grails or the Forge that checks, as `PackFeatures` would refuse the pack; then it compiles each template once per sample and renders it with `PackPages`, with the limit of a file, and gives the first failure of a template with the sample it failed for. The samples are `PackModel.samples(descriptor)`: models built without a `GeneratorContext` and so without Spring, of an application `com.example.shop` whose features are the pack's and those it requires, as many as it takes for each application type of the descriptor and each value of `options.data`, `options.servlet` and `options.reloading` to be in one (four today), not every combination; `PackModel.of` and the samples are built by one method, so they have the same names. `versionsOfForge` is not in a sample, since a pack that is not of Forge cannot name it. Nothing is written. `PackCheck.main` sends what a template writes to the standard output to the standard error, so the problems are all the task reads. Tested: `PackCheckSpec` in `grails-forge-core`, which also starts `main` in a JVM that has only the runtime class path of that module (written to a file by the task `writePackCheckClasspath`); and `GrailsPluginForgePackCheckSpec` in the Gradle plugins, with TestKit and the configuration cache on, against a stand-in for `PackCheck` in an included build of the test project, because `grails-forge` includes `grails-gradle` and the reverse would be a cycle. Not done: a test of the Gradle task against the Forge artifact as it is published (its POM and module metadata are what the default dependency resolves, and no automated test resolves them); what a template does only for an application the samples are not like, such as one with a feature the pack does not require, which is not run; a limit on the time a template can take, since a template that never ends holds the task until the build's own timeout; validation of references to features (`requires`, `conflicts`) against the features of the framework or a catalog, and the check `PackFeatures` makes of a name or exclusive group of the framework, both of which need the feature registry and so a Spring context; predicates and parameters, which the descriptor does not have; and `forgePackTest`.

Implemented, after a review of the check: `grailsForgePackTooling` is left out of everything the Grails plugins do to the versions of every configuration of a project, which before was true of the BOM platform only: the forcing of `org.apache.groovy` by `groovy.version` (`GrailsGradlePlugin.configureGroovy`), the exclusion of `slf4j-simple`, and the strict constraints of `BomPropertyOverridesPlugin`, all through `GrailsGradlePlugin.isToolClasspathOfItsOwn`. With `-Pgroovy.version` the tooling had resolved that Groovy in place of Forge's. `PackCheck.run` writes one line for a pack without problems, `feature pack checked: templates=<n> applications=<n>`, and `checkForgePack` takes exit 0 for a checked pack only with that line and nothing else on the standard output, so a template that ends the JVM (`System.exit(0)`) fails the task and does not pass it; a spec of `grails-forge-core` holds the class name and that line to the constants of `CheckForgePackTask`, which the Forge build has on its build class path. The samples of `PackModel.samples` now go through the values of `options.jdk` too (21, 25, 26; still four samples). The task takes the files of the tooling configuration leniently and its resolution result beside them, and fails in its action, with a message that names the pack check, the Forge of the Grails version and the `grailsForgePackTooling` override, for a module that was not found; before, Gradle's own resolution error came before the task ran. It adds to what Forge says: that this Forge needs a later Java than the toolchain, for an `UnsupportedClassVersionError`, and that what was resolved is a Forge older than the plugin expects, or not Forge, when the class is not there. `PublishToMavenRepository` tasks depend on `checkForgePack` where `maven-publish` is applied, so publishing to a repository checks the pack; `publishToMavenLocal`, `jar` and `assemble` do not, and a plugin without a pack still resolves nothing. Not done, and not possible in this task because each needs the application a pack is used for, so they are made by `PackFeature.apply` and `PackFeatures` when one is generated: a dependency the pack gives a version that the application has in another; the lookups of `versionOfForge` and `artifactOfForge`, and `versionsOfForge` in a template; a build property or a setting another feature sets differently; a destination with the real names of the project in its placeholders; the 16 MiB the files a pack writes can have together after rendering; a file another feature writes; two packs of one name; and a pack with the name or the exclusive group of a feature of the framework. Not done either: a descriptor with a problem hides the problems of the templates, since the descriptor names them and the parser gives none for one it refuses; a file of the tooling that is resolved as a module and cannot be downloaded is left out of the class path without a word of the task, and Forge then fails for the class it misses; a publish task of another type than `PublishToMavenRepository` does not check the pack, and one to a repository in a local directory does.

Implemented, after a second review of the check: a class path of Forge that is not whole no longer runs. `ForgeToolingProblems` is computed with the configuration, from its resolution result and from the failures of its lenient artifact view, and the task fails before it starts anything: for what the configuration declares and no repository has (Forge itself, with the reason, the places searched and the `grailsForgePackTooling` override, as before); for modules that what was found needs and that were not found ("Forge … was found, and N modules it needs were not", ten named and the rest counted, with no advice to name another Forge); and for a file of a module that was found which could not be fetched ("<file> of <module> could not be fetched"), which before was left out without a word, so that a missing jar gave the wrong hint, a `NoClassDefFoundError`, or a pass. A build whose tooling came out with any of these reads a value that is never the same twice (`NeverTheSameValueSource`) while it is configured, so its configuration cache entry is not used again and the next build looks in the repositories again; before, the stored entry repeated the failure after the repository had been put right. The task tells exit 0 with the summary line and other lines beside it ("other lines were written to the standard output") from exit 0 without the line, gives exit 1 without a problem line a hint as exit 0 has, and gives the hints for a later Java and for a missing class only for the lines the launcher of Java writes, at the start of a line and for the class the task starts, when Forge wrote nothing to the standard output. Forge is started with `slf4j.internal.verbosity=ERROR`, and the three lines of an SLF4J without a provider are not echoed. `BomPropertyOverridesPlugin` no longer knows a configuration of Grails: its extension has `excludedConfigurations` (`excludeConfiguration 'name'`), which get no override and whose platforms are not detected, and `GrailsPluginGradlePlugin` names `grailsForgePackTooling` there. Behaviour to know: every `PublishToMavenRepository` task of a project with the `grails-plugin` plugin and `maven-publish` depends on `checkForgePack`, a `file:` repository included, so a plugin with a pack cannot be published even to a directory unless a Forge resolves, while `publishToMavenLocal` of the same pack succeeds unchecked; and the summary line guards against accidents, not against a template that writes the line through the file descriptor and ends the JVM, which is trusted code by design. Not done: a module of the tooling that a repository refused for another reason than not having it is reported with the first line Gradle gives, in the form Gradle gives it.

Implemented: `forgePackTest`. `GrailsPluginGradlePlugin.configureForgePackTest` registers the task (`ForgePackTestTask`) lazily for every plugin project; nothing depends on it, so `check`, `build`, `assemble` and the publish tasks do not run it, and a plugin without `src/main/forge` skips it and resolves no Forge. It runs in two halves. First it starts `org.apache.grails.forge.core.pack.PackFixtures` the way `checkForgePack` starts `PackCheck`: both tasks now extend `ForgePackToolTask`, which holds what they share (the pack directory and its digest from `ForgePackStateValueSource`, the class path of `grailsForgePackTooling` with `ForgeToolingProblems`, the launcher of the toolchain, the launch with `ExecOperations.javaexec`, the refusal of a class path that is not whole, the hints for a later Java and a missing class, and the reading of the output in bounds), and `GrailsPluginGradlePlugin.configureForgePackTool` wires those for both. `PackFixtures.main` takes the directory of the pack, a directory to generate in and optionally a file that lists fixtures, a fixture on a line as its name, its type of application and then any of `data=`, `servlet=`, `reloading=`, `jdk=` and `features=`; without the file it makes one fixture for each type of application of the descriptor, named after the type, so the default is read from the descriptor by Forge when the task runs and not by Gradle when the build is configured. It reads the pack once with `PackLoader.fromDirectory`, starts a plain Spring context of `ForgeCoreConfiguration`, and for each fixture creates the context of an application `com.example.shop` with the feature of the pack selected, through `ProjectGenerator.createGeneratorContext` with the pack, with options made as the create commands make them (an option that is given is a choice), and writes it with `FileSystemOutputHandler` into the directory of the fixture's name. An application that has no settings file, which is every application without the feature `gradle-settings-file`, gets a `settings.gradle` that names it and says it was written for the fixture: a fixture is below the build of the plugin, and Gradle refuses to build a directory below a settings file that does not include it. It writes one line for each fixture to the standard output, `generated <name>` or `refused <name>: <why>` (a fixture of a type the pack is not for, and any `IllegalArgumentException` of the generator: a feature there is not, an option the pack conflicts with, a template that fails), or `pack: <problem>` for each problem of a pack that cannot be read, and then always `feature pack fixtures: generated=<n> refused=<n>`; it ends with 0 when every fixture was generated, 1 when one was refused or the pack has problems, and 2 when it could not run (arguments, a list that cannot be read, more than 32 fixtures or 64 KiB of list, a directory of a fixture that is there already), with the reason on the standard error. A line is one line, at most 2000 characters, without control characters and without the markup sequence of the command line, and a name that is echoed goes through `FeatureSelectionException.shown`. What a template or the generator writes to the standard output goes to the standard error. The repositories of the generated builds are those of `GRAILS_REPO_URL`, which the task sets for that JVM from its own input and takes away when it has none, so the variable of the environment of the build is not used. The task takes the run for one that generated the fixtures only for exit 0, the summary as the last line, counts that agree with the lines, and no other line; a refused fixture or a problem of the pack fails it before anything is built, and anything else is said as a Forge that could not test, with the hints `checkForgePack` gives. Then it builds each generated application with the wrapper it was generated with (`gradlew`, or `gradlew.bat` through `cmd /c`), with `ExecOperations.exec`, in the directory of the application, with the tasks of the configuration (default `assemble`: it compiles and packages, and runs no test of the application), `--console=plain`, `--gradle-user-home` of the build of the plugin so that what is downloaded is downloaded once, `--offline` when asked, further arguments, and `JAVA_HOME` of the toolchain. The Gradle daemon is not turned off: a generated application sets `org.gradle.jvmargs`, so `--no-daemon` would start a single-use daemon for each fixture anyway, and with the daemon the fixtures of one run, and the next run, share one. The project cache of a fixture is its own `.gradle` directory. All output of a build goes to `build/forge-pack-test/<fixture>/build.log`, the applications are in `build/forge-pack-fixtures/<fixture>`, and `build/forge-pack-test/report.txt` says what became of each. A fixture that cannot be built does not stop the others; the task then fails with, for each, the tasks Gradle marked as failed (five at most), where the application and the log are, and what Gradle said went wrong (the lines from `* What went wrong:` to `* Try:`) or else the end of the log, 30 lines of 300 characters at most, without control characters. Its inputs are the tooling class path, the launcher, the digest of the pack, the fixtures, the tasks, the arguments, the repositories and whether it is offline; its output is the report directory; it is up to date for a pack that passed and is as it was, with the configuration cache reused, and is not in the build cache. Configuration: `grails { forgePackTest { } }` (`GrailsForgePackTestOptions` on `GrailsExtension`) with `fixture(name) { applicationType; options; features }`, `tasks`, `arguments`, `repositories` (default: the Maven repositories of the build script of the plugin by address, the local Maven repository and Maven Central by their aliases, a `file:` repository as its path; no credentials and no content filters), `mavenLocal` and `offline`, which `--offline-fixtures` on the command line also sets. A fixture Forge could not read from its line (a name, a value with a space) fails the build when it is configured. Tested: `PackFixturesSpec` in `grails-forge-core` generates real applications of every type, with options and features, refusals, the problems of a pack, every argument error, and starts `main` in a JVM that has the class path of the module's jar (its runtime class path and its compiled pages, which `writePackCheckClasspath` now adds because the generator renders from them and the jar holds them) with `GRAILS_REPO_URL` set; a spec there holds the class name and the four line patterns to the constants of `ForgePackTestTask`. `GrailsPluginForgePackTestSpec` in the Gradle plugins runs the task with TestKit and the configuration cache on against a stand-in `PackFixtures` in the included build of the test project, whose fixtures are directories with a script of the name of the wrapper, so the launch, the arguments, the environment, the logs, the report, the up-to-date check and the failures are tested and the build of a real application is not. Run once by hand, not in a test: the Forge of this commit published to a scratch Maven repository, the task run on a copy of the test project's pack, four applications generated by the real Forge from the published class path, `web` and `rest-api` (also with `data=mongodb`) built by their wrappers with `assemble` against the Apache snapshot repository, the second run up to date with the configuration cache reused, and the run with `--offline-fixtures` built from the Gradle user home. Not done: `web-plugin` and `plugin` fixtures of that run failed in `:generateI18nDescriptor` of the generated plugin itself ("ships message bundles outside its own namespace"), which is the generated plugin and not the pack, so a pack for plugins cannot pass `forgePackTest` with the default task until that is put right; the generated applications needed `gradle/classic-framework.init.gradle` passed with `arguments` to build against the snapshot; no automated test runs the task against the published Forge or builds a real application; offline from locked inputs, which would need the fixtures to be built with dependency locking or verification metadata that the task writes on an online run and requires on an offline one, and a wrapper distribution that is already there, where today `--offline` only works for what the Gradle user home happens to hold; repositories declared in `settings.gradle`, credentials and content filters of repositories, which are not passed to the fixtures; a limit on the time of a fixture build other than the `timeout` of the task; fixtures built in parallel; the tests of the applications, which run only when `tasks` names them; a preset-only publication, since presets do not exist; and Windows, where the `gradlew.bat` path is written and not run by a test.

Implemented, after a review of `forgePackTest`: the repositories the applications get by default are taken from the build script for what they are (`ForgePackFixtureRepositories`). Maven Central is the repository with the address of Maven Central and the local repository is the one of Gradle's class for it, whatever their names; before, a repository whose name started with `MavenRepo` or `MavenLocal` was replaced by one of the two. A repository whose address has a user or a password is left out, and so is one whose address is neither `https` nor a directory, which the generator would refuse for every fixture in the name of `GRAILS_REPO_URL`; the task warns of each, each time it runs, the first by its host only. Repositories the build names in `forgePackTest { repositories }` are given as they are, a password included, which Forge writes into the build files of the applications and their baseline under `.grails`. The repositories are no longer an input of the task, which Gradle keeps as text in the execution history: a SHA-256 of them is, and they travel as an internal property (in the configuration cache entry, which Gradle encrypts). What the task prints, the failures it shows of Forge and of a log, and `report.txt` have the user and the password of any address taken out; the log of a fixture, which its own Gradle writes, has not. A fixture is built by a process whose output goes to the log file directly, with no pipe to the build, so a process the wrapper leaves behind no longer holds the task; it has `fixtureTimeout` (30 minutes by default) to be done in, after which the wrapper and every process below it are destroyed and the fixture is one that could not be built, the others still being built. The wrapper is started by itself with its arguments one by one, on Windows as the batch file and no longer through `cmd /c`, which loses the quoting of two paths with spaces (`ForgePackTestTask.wrapperCommand`). A build of the plugin that is run with `--offline` builds its fixtures with `--offline`. Tested: rows in `GrailsPluginForgePackTestSpec` for repositories with the names of the two, with a password by default and when named (the password is searched for in every file of the project and found only in what Forge wrote), not `https`, a build that does not end, a process left behind, `--offline`, and `JAVA_HOME` as the home of the toolchain; `ForgePackTestCommandSpec` for the command, also as for Windows, and for what is shown of an address; the stand-in makes the four default fixtures Forge makes; `PackFixturesSpec` now holds the exit codes, the start of each line, and the most lines Forge can write (32 fixtures or 50 problems, and one) to what the task reads (60 lines). Not done: the applications are not an output of the task, so deleting `build/forge-pack-fixtures` leaves the task up to date, which the guide says; the Windows command is constructed and tested as a list and not run; a repository that is named with a protocol the generator refuses is still refused by the generator in its words; a password with a slash in it is not recognized in a line that is shown; what a stopped build had started and detached from itself (a process no longer below the wrapper) is not found.

V1 uses the main JAR only. It never probes for a `forge` classifier. Future classifier support must extend artifact identity and record the checksum of that exact classifier artifact. `grails create-feature-pack` scaffolding is deferred; the 9.0 authoring guide supplies a complete working source layout and fixture instead.

**Presets.** A preset is a pack with `kind: preset`, using the same format, template API, compatibility checks, distribution and locking. It names one application type, supplies option and parameter defaults, requests features and may add files through the normal contribution model. For example:

```yaml
format: 1
kind: preset
name: acme-service
templateApi: 1
compatibility:
  grails: '[9.0.0,10.0.0)'
  forge: '[9.0.0,10.0.0)'
applicationType: rest-api
options: { data: hibernate7, servlet: tomcat, jdk: 21 }
features: [views-json, database-migration]
```

V1 permits one preset per project and no preset inheritance. Explicit CLI options override preset defaults, which override framework defaults; an incompatible application-type command fails. Requested features and mandatory requirements follow section 2.2. A preset cannot overwrite an existing framework output or replace the build template; it uses the shared contribution channels and collision rules. Full custom-profile capabilities such as inherited skeleton replacement and multi-project skeleton layouts are not claimed as automatic equivalents. Document those migration gaps and provide a manual conversion path before removing profile support. Preset schema, authoring, a published synthetic company-preset fixture and migration coverage are step-2/step-3 delivery gates, not deferred work.

**Catalog.** The catalog already exists. `apache/grails-plugins-metadata` is the data source of the plugin page: one YAML file per plugin at `grails-plugins/<group path>/<artifactId>.yml`, submitted by pull request and reviewed by the Grails team.

```
name: Cache
desc: Provides AST transformations for caching method calls
coords: org.apache.grails:grails-cache
owner: Apache Software Foundation
vcs: https://github.com/apache/grails-core
docs: https://grails.apache.org/docs/latest/guide/cache.html
maven-repo: https://repo1.maven.org/maven2
labels: [cache]
licenses: [Apache-2.0]
versions:                     # maintained by the indexer, newest first
  - version: 8.0.0
    date: 2026-10-04T18:46:18Z
    grailsVersion: 8.0.0-SNAPSHOT > *
```

Every eight hours `index-plugins.groovy` reads each plugin's `maven-metadata.xml`, downloads every new version's jar by its exact URL, opens `META-INF/grails-plugin.xml` inside it to read `grailsVersion`, rewrites the YAML, and regenerates `grails-plugins-index.json` (a JSON array of those maps) through a pull request. The website's `PluginsTask` fetches that file at build time. As of 2026-10-13 the index has 256 entries (157 under `org.grails.plugins`, 29 under `org.apache.grails`, 12 under `io.github.gpc`, 9 under `cloud.wondrify`), 40 marked `deprecated`, and 61 with at least one version whose `grailsVersion` starts with 7 or 8.

The plan extends this pipeline instead of adding a second catalog:

1. **The indexer detects packs.** Read `META-INF/grails-forge/feature.yml` and record its format, kind, canonical feature ID, application types, template API and compatibility declarations, together with the main JAR's SHA-256 and exact artifact coordinates. Pack-only preset artifacts are indexed from their descriptor even without a runtime `grails-plugin.xml`. Extend the existing catalog schema with an explicit version, keeping ordinary plugin entries usable for `plugin search`.
2. **The CLI reads the index.** `labels` map to categories, `desc` and `docs` supply display metadata, and deprecated entries are hidden unless requested. Filter against both target Grails and executing Forge compatibility; use the newest compatible released external pack only for a new, unlocked selection. Refresh from `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/grails-plugins-metadata/main/grails-plugins-index.json`, with a release-pinned copy bundled for offline discovery. Record the index content digest used for resolution. `forge-web` neither refreshes this index nor exposes external plugin search. Existing locks are never re-resolved merely because the index changed.
3. **Official versions remain BOM-aligned.** Bundled official features use the matching generator release. When selecting a separately distributed BOM-managed pack, resolve its exact artifact version from the target BOM's bundled version metadata, not from the catalog's newest entry. Omit the runtime dependency version in generated builds only when the chosen BOM actually manages it; still record the exact pack artifact and digest in the lock. Fail if the needed BOM version mapping is unavailable rather than silently choosing a newer pack.
4. **Explicit coordinates remain the escape hatch** for a plugin that is not in the index: `grails create-app shop --features com.acme:acme-audit-grails:2.3.0`. Users can add private indexes and repositories in `~/.grails/forge.yml`.

Weaknesses of the index that the plan has to live with or fix: `grailsVersion` is whatever the plugin descriptor says (grails-cache 8.0.0 reports `8.0.0-SNAPSHOT > *`), `docs` is missing on 134 entries, `labels` are free-form, there is no checksum today, and the older `sync-latest-version` action still refers to a `grails-plugins.json` file that no longer exists.

**Fetching without a build.** Fetch the main JAR by exact Maven-layout URL without resolving its runtime dependency graph. Follow descriptor requirements as a pack graph, separately from Maven transitive dependencies. Hash the complete downloaded artifact before extracting only `META-INF/grails-forge/**`; never put the plugin's runtime JAR on the template classpath. A checksum mismatch is a failure, not a reason to try a different artifact.

Explicit repository mappings in `~/.grails/forge.yml` take precedence, then configured `GRAILS_REPO_URL` overrides, catalog repository overrides and Maven Central. Repository IDs, group/content filters and exclusive mappings prevent a private coordinate from being sent to an unrelated repository. Honor per-version catalog coordinates and repositories. Credentials stay in user configuration, are scoped to their repository and are not forwarded across origins. Repository access used to fetch a pack is distinct from repositories required by the generated build; emit only declared, credential-free build repository configuration.

Use HTTPS for remote repositories; explicitly configured local `file:` repositories and loopback test servers support authoring fixtures. No catalog entry may enable those local exceptions. A small `settings.xml` reader supports documented proxy/server fields; do not imply full Maven mirror, profile or encrypted-password support. Unsupported required settings produce an actionable error. Do not reintroduce Maven Resolver.

`--offline` makes no network requests and requires all locked artifacts or reproducible local inputs to be available. `--refresh` refreshes discovery metadata and retries retrieval of the same locked identity; it does not upgrade a lock. Explicitly requested snapshots resolve to a timestamped artifact and digest, both recorded. Cache artifacts by repository identity plus exact coordinates and content digest, with atomic writes and locking; GAV alone is insufficient for mutable snapshots or two repositories serving different bytes. Catalog metadata has a 24-hour TTL. Local-directory inputs are fingerprinted; shared automatic replay requires a project-relative checked-in source directory or a published artifact, not a machine-specific absolute path.

**Trust and filesystem contract.** External packs are CLI-only trusted code. Catalog-backed packs require the indexed checksum; direct coordinates without one display their provenance and observed digest, which is then locked (integrity on subsequent use, not proof of authorship). Before extraction and generator writes, reject absolute/traversing paths, symlink escapes, duplicate normalized destinations and excessive entry count/expanded size. Apply equivalent checks after token substitution and to local directories. New-project generation never overwrites an existing file, including in-place generation. These guards protect the extraction/output mechanisms; arbitrary GSP code is not confined by them, and dry run executes that code. Hosted Forge never fetches or compiles an external pack on request. Document these precise guarantees and limitations in `THREAT_MODEL.md`.

Implemented: direct coordinates in the create commands of the Forge CLI. An entry of `--features` with a colon is the coordinates of a plugin, `group:artifact:version`, with the parts held to the characters a descriptor allows for coordinates and the version to one version (no range, no `+`, no `latest.*`, `LATEST` or `RELEASE`; a `-SNAPSHOT` version is allowed); anything else with a colon is a mistake in the command line (exit code 2). `PackArtifacts`, in `grails-forge-cli` so that `grails-forge-web`, which depends on `grails-forge-core` alone, cannot reach it, takes the main jar by its Maven-layout address with the HTTP client of the JDK (no resolver and no new dependency), asking for the file as it is (`Accept-Encoding: identity`; an encoded answer is refused), with no POM, no classifier and no dependency of the plugin asked for. It reads the jar into memory, hashing the bytes as they come, checks the pin and the checksum of the repository against that digest, and reads the pack from the same array with `PackLoader.fromJarBytes`: the jar is written to no file, so there is nothing between the digest and the pack that could be changed, and nothing to delete or to sweep. The JDK reads a jar by its directory only from a file, so there is a reader for bytes, `PackJar`, and it is the only reader of jars: `PackLoader.fromJar(Path)` reads the file into memory, under the same limit of 64 MiB, and hands the bytes to it. The format lets one file be two jars, since a jar can be read from its end by its directory or from its start entry by entry, and a reader that does only one of the two takes whatever the other would not have shown; the first reader of bytes did, and took another pack than the JDK from a jar with a second directory in the comment of its end record, from one whose end record counted fewer entries than its directory held, from one with a ZIP64 end record, and took jars the JDK refuses. `PackJar` therefore takes a jar only in the one form on which every reader has to agree, and refuses anything else with a message that says what is wrong and has nothing of the jar in it but numbers. The form: the jar is its entries from the first byte, one directly after the other, then the directory, then the end record of 22 bytes, with which the file ends, so there is no comment and no byte in front, between or after that belongs to nothing (a jar after prepended bytes, such as an executable jar with its launch script, was read before and is refused now: bytes no entry covers are where a second jar is put); the end record names one disk, gives one count twice, and its offset and size are exactly where the entries end and the end record begins; nothing is ZIP64 (no count of 65535, no field of 0xFFFFFFFF, no ZIP64 extra field, and no mark of an end record inside the directory, where a reader looks for the ZIP64 one); the directory is exactly the entries counted, in the order the entries have in the file; every entry, of the pack or not, is stored or deflated, needs no more than version 2.0, has no flag but the deflate options, the data descriptor and UTF-8, a name that is strict UTF-8 (flagged so when it is not ASCII) without control characters or backslashes, relative, with no empty step, `.` or `..`, no comment, extra fields that are whole and of six kinds only, those that carry the mark of a jar, times and owners (0xCAFE, 0x5455, 0x000A, 0x5855, 0x7855, 0x7875: an extra field can be a second name for the entry, 0x7075, which Python and the zip tools take for its name and the JDK does not, so kinds are admitted and not excluded); no name twice, and no file where another entry has a directory (found by sorting the names with the slash first, without making a string of any of them); the header in front of each entry repeats the directory (flags, method, name, and CRC-32 and sizes; with a data descriptor these are in the descriptor, which has to have its signature, and in the header they are all zero, as `ZipOutputStream` and so the `jar` tool and `jarsigner` write a deflated entry, or all there as well, as the archiver of Maven writes it; both are accepted because the jars of those tools have one of the two, and Gradle writes no descriptor; a stored entry cannot have a descriptor, as the JDK cannot read it from a stream); and the content of every entry is inflated and checked: a deflated entry ends exactly at its compressed length, has its stated length and its CRC-32. Read is bounded by what was given: fewer than 65535 entries, 4 MiB of names together, 256 MiB of content together, checked against what the directory states before anything is inflated and against what is inflated after; nothing is kept but the array and seven numbers for each entry. `PackLoaderJarBytesSpec` builds every refused form in code, among them the jar that was two packs, has both entrances refuse each alike, reads the jars the JDK streams, the `jar` tool and a tool with sizes known in advance write, and holds 1,500 seeded variations to the property that a jar that is taken is read with the same names and bytes by `ZipFile` and by `ZipInputStream`. A jar of 64 MiB is in memory once while it is read; memory that runs out there is reported as that, about this computer. Not done: the names of entries are compared as bytes, so two names that differ only in case, or in the normal form of their characters, are two entries, as they are to the JDK; the time stamps and the attributes of an entry, which name no content, are not compared between the header and the directory; a jar in the ZIP64 form (which Gradle writes for every jar of a task with `zip64 = true`, however small), with a comment or with a launch script in front is not read at all, whoever wrote it. The feature that is selected is the one of the pack, by the name of the pack; the same coordinates twice are one selection. Two plugins with one pack name are refused as two directories are, as a mistake in the command line that asked for both (exit code 2); a pack of a repository that can be used with nothing (the name of a framework feature, an exclusive group of the framework, versions it is not for) is a failure of what was fetched (exit code 1, no usage text), told from the other by the type of the refusal: `PackFeatures` throws `PackRefusedException`, which carries the packs and whether it is the pack itself that cannot be used or the combination that was given. The repositories are those of the repeatable `--feature-pack-repository` and no others when it is given; without it, those of `GRAILS_REPO_URL` in its order and then Maven Central. `repo.grails.org` and the snapshot repositories of a generated build are not asked. Of what `GradleRepository` reads in the variable this takes `mavenLocal()` (the directory of `maven.repo.local` or `~/.m2/repository`) and `mavenCentral()` in exactly those spellings, an `https` address, and a directory by its absolute path or `file:` address. A path that is not absolute is not asked: for the generator and Gradle it is one of the generated application, and here it would be one of the directory the command runs in, where a directory of that name would supply the pack. An entry a pack cannot be taken from is left out with a warning and is no error: one that is not `https` (which the generator refuses too), one with a user or password (credentials are not sent, and the entry is not used without them), one with a query or fragment, a `file:` address with a host, a path that is not absolute, a host or port no request can be made to. The warnings name the entries by their number, put the entries that are left out for one reason on one line, and are at most 5 lines and one that counts the rest. An address is `https`, or `file:` for a directory, in ASCII, without user, query or fragment and without an at sign anywhere, with a port from 1 to 65535; `http` is refused. A host is a name or an address, and which is not left to a resolver: an address is four numbers from 0 to 255 with dots and without leading zeros, or one in brackets; a host that is one number, numbers and dots that are not that, or a name whose last part is a number or begins with `0x`, is refused for the option and for every redirect. No address is repeated in a message as it was given (`GradleRepository.shownAddress`): one that parses with a host is shown as its scheme, host, port and path, without user, query or fragment, and not at all when its path has an at sign; of any other only a scheme of a few known ones and its length are said. The generator's own two messages about an entry of `GRAILS_REPO_URL` follow the same rule and no longer carry the exception that repeats the entry. An address in the error line is cut at 200 characters. At most 5 redirects are followed; each address is checked before it is asked to be `https` and to have such a host and port. Unless the repository is itself one of them, a redirect is refused to `localhost`, `*.localhost`, `localhost.localdomain`, `ip6-localhost`, `ip6-loopback`, and to an address that is loopback, link-local, private (10/8, 172.16/12, 192.168/16, fc00::/7, fec0::/10), this-network (0/8), shared (100.64/10), for protocols or measuring (192.0.0/24, 198.18/15), multicast or reserved (224/3 and up, ff00::/8), or that carries such an IPv4 address (IPv4-mapped and IPv4-compatible, 64:ff9b::/96, 2002::/16). The address is made of what is written; no name is looked up. A jar has at most 64 MiB, the `maven-metadata.xml` of a snapshot 256 KiB and a checksum file 1 KiB, each counted as it is read. The connection has 15 seconds; a request without an answer and an answer without its next bytes have 60 seconds, and one file 5 minutes. The last two are kept by a watchdog that closes the answer and interrupts the read, since the request timeout of the client ends when the headers arrive; they hold for the jar, the metadata and the checksums, and the watchdog of a file is ended in a `finally`, whatever ends the file, so it interrupts nothing afterwards. A repository that does not have the jar (no file, or HTTP 404 or 410) is passed over; every other failure ends the search, HTTP 401 and 403 for the jar among them, of which the message says that the repository refuses access and that the search stops there so that the coordinates are sent to no other repository. Every such failure ends the search without the next repository being asked, and the error is one line with the coordinates, each repository asked with its reason (not found, timeout, TLS failure, no connection, HTTP status, checksum mismatch, size, a redirect that is refused, or the kind of exception the client threw, never its text) and a pointer to `--feature-pack-repository`; exit code 1, nothing created, no stack trace. Nothing a repository answers leaves `resolve` in another way than as `PackArtifactException`: it catches every `Throwable`, a `StackOverflowError` included, and lets through only the other errors of the virtual machine. A failure of a request for the metadata or a checksum names that file, whatever the failure is. The jar is checked against the `.sha256` of the repository, or the `.sha1` when there is no `.sha256`, and refused on a mismatch or when that file is not a checksum (a byte order mark before it is allowed); for the checksum file, HTTP 401 and 403 mean what 404 and 410 mean, that none is published, and any other failure of that request ends the search and names the file. `--feature-pack-sha256 group:artifact:version=<hex>` (repeatable, either case) refuses a jar with another SHA-256 before it is opened, and is a mistake in the command line when it names coordinates that are not selected. The line that names the pack is `Feature pack <name> from <coordinates> (<repository>), sha256 <hex>`, followed by whether the SHA-256 was the one asked for and whether a checksum of the repository was matched or the jar was not checked, then the summary; `--list-features` prints the first two before the list, for each pack it took from a repository. A pack whose `dependencies` do not have the `group:artifact` it was fetched by gets a warning, and so does one that declares it only in another version than the one it was fetched as (the warning says which version the application gets); a declaration without a version gets none, and the dependency is never added or changed for the pack. The warnings are printed after the application is generated, so one that is refused is not warned of as if it had been made, and a refusal of generation that names no feature of a pack is followed by the coordinates and repositories of the packs the application was to be made with. A jar that holds no usable pack is a failure of what was fetched: exit code 1, a first line with the coordinates and the repository, then every problem of the descriptor on a line of its own (at most the 50 the parser reports), without the usage text. A refusal of the selection (`FeatureSelectionException`) is a mistake in the command line wherever it comes from (exit code 2, with the usage text), also when a pack of a repository causes it, such as one that requires a feature there is not: the user can name that feature or the plugin that has it. Its message then says after the refusal which coordinates and repository each feature it names was taken by. `CreateCommand.call` prints any other `IllegalArgumentException` of context creation or generation as its message with exit code 1, and the stack trace only with `--stacktrace`. What was typed is shown in messages by one rule, `FeatureSelectionException.shown`. A snapshot is the build its `maven-metadata.xml` names for the main jar: of the `snapshotVersion` entries with extension `jar` and no classifier the one with the latest time and then the highest number, else the `snapshot` timestamp and build number. The file is read with a streaming parser (StAX, document types and entity references refused) and no tree is built of it: at most 8 levels and 20,000 elements, of the nine elements that are read only the text directly in them and at most 400 characters of it, so nothing recurses on what the file nests. Its root has to be `metadata`, so the page a server answers with instead is a failure that names the file. A `groupId`, `artifactId` or `version` in it has to be the one asked for; a build has to be exactly the version with `yyyyMMdd.HHmmss-<number>` in the place of `SNAPSHOT`, the time being a day and a time of day there are, or the version itself. A repository without that file, or whose file names no build, gives the jar under the name of the version. `Pack.getArtifact()` (`PackArtifact`: name, coordinates, repository, file name, SHA-256, kind of checksum matched, whether pinned) carries this, and `grails-forge-cli.yml` records name, coordinates, repository, file name and SHA-256 under the key `featurePacks`, which `CodeGenConfig` ignores. The repository is written only when `PackArtifact.getRecordableRepository()` gives it, an `https` address without a user: `grails-forge-cli.yml` has no `repository` line for a pack of a directory, and the lock of the project state takes its `origin` from the same method, so no generated file says where on the computer a pack was taken from. Not done: the catalog and names from it, `~/.grails/forge.yml` with repository mappings, content filters, credentials, the `settings.xml` reader and proxies beyond the JVM's own, loopback `http` test servers, `--offline` and `--refresh`, the artifact cache (every use downloads), a lock that is checked later, signature verification, pack requirements followed to other artifacts, a refusal of redirects to names that resolve to private addresses (a public name that resolves to one is followed, since no name is looked up before the client connects), and the same options on `feature add`.

Implemented: names from a catalog, in the create commands of the Forge CLI (item 2 of the list above, as a reader; the index that is read is that of another repository). An entry of `--features` that is neither a feature of the framework for the application type (hidden ones included), nor the name of a pack that was given by directory, jar or coordinates, and that has the form of a feature name, is looked up in the feature catalogs; so is `group:artifact#name`, the full name of the feature of one plugin. `PackCatalog` and `PackCatalogs` are in `grails-forge-cli` beside `PackArtifacts`, so `grails-forge-web` cannot reach them, and nothing of `grails-forge-core` selects by a catalog. A catalog gives coordinates and a digest and nothing else: the pack is then taken by `PackArtifacts.resolve` as for coordinates that were typed, with the SHA-256 of the catalog as the digest the jar has to have (a jar with another one is refused before it is opened, which is the "catalog-backed packs require the indexed checksum" of the trust contract), and the pack of the jar has to have the name the catalog gave. The repositories are those of coordinates; the `maven-repo` a catalog names for the plugin or for one pack is asked after those of `GRAILS_REPO_URL` and before Maven Central, has to be `https` and not an address of this computer or its network (the rule of a redirect; `https://repo1.maven.org/maven2` is read as Maven Central), and is not asked when `--feature-pack-repository` is given. The lines that name the pack say after the first: `the coordinates are those the feature catalog <address> (sha256 <hex>) has for the feature <name>` and `the jar has the SHA-256 the catalog records for it`. The catalog format as it first was (it is now the index's own array with a `pack` member, see the last paragraph on the catalog; the index does not publish packs yet, and the change to its indexer is drafted, not merged): a JSON object with `format` (1; any other is refused) and `plugins`, an array of the maps the index has today; of a plugin `coords` (`group:artifact`), `versions`, and optionally `name`, `desc`, `docs`, `maven-repo`, `deprecated` (`true`, `false` or a text); of a version `version` and optionally `pack`; of a `pack` its `format` (1), `kind` (`feature` or `preset`), `name` (the feature name), `templateApi`, `compatibility.grails` and `compatibility.forge` (ranges as `PackVersionRange` reads them), `sha256` of the main jar, and optionally `applicationTypes`, `coords` (`group:artifact:version`, the exact artifact when it is not the plugin's) and `maven-repo`. Fields that are not named are not read; a plugin without a `pack` in any version is not looked at; an array as the top level is the index of today and is read as a catalog without packs, of which the messages say so. The document is untrusted: at most 16 MiB, counted as it is read; UTF-8 or refused; read by a JSON reader of its own (no new dependency) with at most 8 levels, 1,000,000 values, 65,536 characters in a text, a key at most once in an object and nothing after the document; at most 20,000 plugins and 1,000 versions of a plugin. What is wrong with the document refuses the catalog. A plugin with a pack that has anything wrong with it (coordinates, version, name, digest, range, application type, repository, a type that is not the one expected, a version or the plugin twice) is left out, with one line of at most 300 characters for each of the first 20, and the others are still found: the index is written by many hands, and one bad entry does not take the catalog from every user. Texts of the catalog reach a message only through `FeatureSelectionException.shown`, and `desc` and `name` as one line of at most 200 and 100 characters without control characters or the markup sequence. Version choice: of the versions of a plugin that have a feature pack of the name, the latest release (numbers and dots only, compared as numbers) whose pack is of format 1 and this template API, for the application type, and whose ranges have the Grails and the Forge of `VersionInfo`; a snapshot, a milestone and every version of a deprecated plugin are not chosen for a name, and the refusal names the coordinates by which they are used, which is the explicit form (coordinates read no catalog). A name the framework has is the framework's feature, and no catalog is read for it; when a catalog was read for another name of the same command and has a plugin under the framework's name, a line says that the feature of Grails is the one used. A name that more than one plugin has is refused with the full names to choose from (exit code 2); a name whose plugins cannot be chosen is refused with the reason for each (exit code 2); a name no catalog has is left to the selection, which refuses it as a feature that does not exist (exit code 2), followed by where it was looked for and how many plugins were left out. A catalog that cannot be read or is no catalog is exit code 1, one line that says which names were looked for, which catalog and why, and nothing is created; an address of `--feature-catalog` that is not one is exit code 2. Where the catalogs come from: `--feature-catalog` (repeatable; `https`, or `file:` for a file, by the address rules of a repository; `none` for no catalog), else the `GRAILS_FORGE_CATALOG` environment variable (addresses separated by semicolons, or `none`), else `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/apache/grails-plugins-metadata/main/grails-plugins-index.json`; with several, the first named speaks for a plugin more than one has. A catalog is read only when a name is looked up, once for a command, through `PackArtifacts.file`, which is the transport of the jar (the same client, limits by argument, watchdog, redirect and address rules; `PackArtifacts.repository` and the new `PackArtifacts.catalog` share one check of an address). A command with only framework features, directories and coordinates makes no request for a catalog, and neither does `--list-features`; `--list-catalog-features` reads the catalogs and lists each feature that can be selected by its name with the coordinates that would be taken, and counts what is not offered, what has a name of the framework, and what was left out with its reason. Copies: a catalog of an `https` address is kept in `~/.grails/forge/catalogs/<sha256 of the address>.json`, the bytes as read, written to a temporary file beside it and moved into place, the directory `rwx------` and the file `rw-------` where the file system has such permissions; a copy is read under the limits of a catalog and without following a link, and one that is no catalog is not used. A copy younger than 24 hours is used without a request; `--refresh` asks anew and does not fall back; a catalog that cannot be read is answered by an older copy with a warning that gives its time. A `file:` catalog is read every time. `--offline` (on the create commands): no request is made; a catalog is its copy of any age or a file, and a jar is taken only from a repository that is a directory, an `https` repository being passed over as "not asked". State: `PackArtifact` carries the catalog and the SHA-256 of its bytes (`withCatalog`), and a feature of the lock has `catalogSha256` and, for an `https` catalog, `catalog`, after the coordinates, version and `sha256` it had; the reader takes a lock without them as before, refuses a `catalog` that is not a public `https` address, a `catalog` without `catalogSha256` and a `catalogSha256` without coordinates, version and digest, and `StateLimits.LOCK_LINES` counts eight lines for a feature. The manifest is unchanged: the requested feature has the coordinates and the version, so what was chosen is not chosen again. The specs of the CLI run with `GRAILS_FORGE_CATALOG=none`, so that a spec that asks for a feature there is not asks nothing of the network. Tested: `CreateAppFeatureCatalogSpec`, through the command with catalogs that are files, repositories that are directories and a transport that answers from a map and records every request; `ProjectStateSpec` for the lock; two rows of `ForgeApiIntegrationSpec` for the hosted Forge, where such a name does not exist. Not done: item 1 (the indexer and the extended index: until it is published, the default catalog has no pack and a name looked up in it does not exist, after one request a day); item 3, the version of a BOM-managed pack from the BOM's version metadata (the latest compatible release is chosen for every plugin, also for one the Grails BOM manages); the release-pinned copy of the index inside Forge (there is no index with packs to pin); `~/.grails/forge.yml` with private indexes, repository mappings, content filters and credentials (private catalogs are named by the option or the variable, and a catalog that needs credentials cannot be read); `labels` as categories, and `docs` in any output; presets (a `pack` of kind `preset` is read and never selected); pack requirements resolved through the catalog; a lock of the copy against two commands writing it at once (the move is atomic, the last writer wins); the artifact cache, so `--offline` finds a jar only in a directory; `--offline` and `--refresh` as options of every command; a re-resolution guard beyond the manifest, since nothing reads the lock back to generate yet; the same on `feature add`; and a test over TLS of the catalog itself (the transport is the one `HttpsPackArtifactsSpec` covers for a jar).

Implemented, after a review of the catalog. The catalog nobody named (the plugin index, read when neither `--feature-catalog` nor `GRAILS_FORGE_CATALOG` names one) cannot make a name worse off than it was: when it cannot be read or is no catalog, `PackCatalogs.read` gives no catalog and `getUnread()` the reason, the names go to the selection as they would without a catalog, and the refusal is the one of before, `The requested feature does not exist: <name>`, exit code 2 with the usage, followed by a line of its own: `The feature catalog <address> could not be read: <reason>, so <names> was not looked for there`. A catalog that was named and cannot be read is exit code 1 as before, since it was asked for; so is `--list-catalog-features` of the catalog nobody named, which asked for the list. The catalog nobody named is read through `PackArtifacts.file(address, limit, name, within)` with 5 seconds (`PackCatalogs.DEFAULT_WITHIN`) for the whole file in the place of the 60 seconds and 5 minutes of a jar, kept by the same watchdog, which also ends a request that has no answer yet. A failure to read it is remembered: an empty file `<sha256 of the address>.unread` beside the copy, written as a copy is (same directory, `rw-------`), whose time is the time of the failure; for 24 hours the catalog is not asked for and the reason is `the last attempt, at <time>, failed, and it is not asked for again within a day; --refresh asks for it`; `--refresh` ignores the marker, a catalog that is read deletes it, and `--offline` neither reads nor writes it. With `--offline` and no copy the outcome is the same refusal, the reason being that nothing is asked. Whether the plugin index is read at all when nothing is named is one constant, `PackCatalogs.DEFAULT_ON` (true): with false, `PackCatalogs.addresses` gives no catalog unless one is named, no name is looked up, no request is made and no file is written, which is the command as it was before catalogs, and everything a named catalog does is unchanged. Item 3, as far as Forge has the metadata: `ContextFactory.getCoordinateResolver()` gives the create commands the `CoordinateResolver` the features and `versionOfForge` look versions up in, and `PackCatalogs.choose` takes a function from `group:artifact` to the version Forge has for it. For a plugin whose artifact the resolver knows under the same group, the pack that is taken is the one of exactly that version (the plugin's own `group:artifact:version`, a release or not), and no other: without one in the catalog the name is refused (exit code 2) with `Forge has <group:artifact> in version <X> for the applications it generates, and the catalog has no pack for <X> (it has one for ...); another version is used only by its coordinates, such as <coordinates>`, and a pack of that version that this Forge does not read, or that is not for the application, is refused likewise. `--list-catalog-features` lists the version that would be taken. The guard of the specs: `PackArtifacts`' own transport throws an `AssertionError` that names the address for a request to a host that is not this computer when the system property `grails.forge.cli.noNetwork` is `true`, which the test task of `grails-forge-cli` sets beside `GRAILS_FORGE_CATALOG=none`; the error is let through everything that reads an answer, and through the command. `CreateAppFeatureCatalogSpec` runs the default path through the command with the transport answered by the spec (not found, timeout, TLS failure, HTTP 500, no catalog, a transport that does not return, the marker and its day, `--refresh`, `--offline`, a catalog that is read, the index of today), a named catalog that fails, the version of Forge with a catalog entry for `org.apache.grails:grails-web-console`, and the guard itself. Not done: the resolver knows the artifacts of Forge's own `pom.xml` (seven today), not the Grails BOM: a plugin the Grails BOM manages and that file does not name is still taken in its latest compatible release, and the BOM is not among what the CLI has. A copy of a catalog in `~/.grails/forge/catalogs/` is read back as a catalog is, as untrusted: a copy someone else wrote there can do what a hostile catalog at the address can (name a plugin, a version, a repository on the internet and a digest for a name, whose jar is then read as a pack) and nothing more, and it is the user's own directory, which whoever can write to can do more with elsewhere; the copy is not signed or bound to the user, and a marker written there only keeps the plugin index from being asked for a day. Two commands that write a copy or a marker at once race, each write being whole: the last writer wins. The time a named catalog can take is that of a jar.

Implemented, after a second review of the catalog; where this differs from the two paragraphs above, this is what the code does. No catalog is read unless one is named: `PackCatalogs.PLUGIN_INDEX_UNNAMED` is false, and the create commands ask `readsPluginIndexUnnamed()`, a protected method that returns it; no option, environment variable or system property turns it on, and the specs turn it on by overriding the method. With it off and nothing named, a name that is not known is refused as before catalogs, with no request and no file under `~/.grails`. `PackCatalog` reads less and keeps less. The limits are 4 MiB, 8 levels, 200,000 values, 5,000 plugins, 200 versions of a plugin, 4,096 characters of a text that is read, 200 of a key, 64 of a number, 120 of `group:artifact` and 60 of a version, set from the index that is published (0.64 MiB, 257 plugins) with several times its size as headroom. The reader follows a pattern of the members a catalog has: what the pattern does not name is checked to be JSON, counted, and not kept, a text that is not read is not built, a plugin is converted and dropped as it is read, and of a plugin without a pack only its `group:artifact` is kept. A number is checked against the JSON grammar by hand and no number is made of it unless it is a whole one of at most nine digits (`format`, `templateApi`); `BigDecimal` is not used. Nothing but the reader's own refusal leaves `PackCatalog.parse`: any `RuntimeException`, `StackOverflowError` or `OutOfMemoryError` becomes `it is not a catalog: it could not be read (<kind>)`. Measured: a document of 4.19 MiB with 1,391 plugins and 16,692 packs is read in a 64 MiB heap with 28 MiB in use afterwards (before: 384 MiB for 11.6 MiB), and a spec reads that document and one of 4 MiB of unread texts in a forked JVM with `-Xmx128m`. A pack is the main jar of the plugin it is listed under in the version it is listed under: the `coords` member of a `pack` is no longer read, since V1 has no companion artifact, so a catalog cannot make one plugin's name fetch another's artifact, and the version Forge has for a plugin is held to the coordinates that are fetched. The catalog's `maven-repo` is asked last, after `GRAILS_REPO_URL` and Maven Central, wherever Maven Central is in that list. Before any repository is asked, a line says `The feature <name> is taken as <coordinates>, which the feature catalog <address> (sha256 <hex>) names for it, with the SHA-256 <hex>. Asking <repositories>`; there is no prompt. The line after the origin now reads `the SHA-256 is the one that same catalog gives for the jar: the jar is the one the catalog means, which nobody else has checked`. A name the framework has for any application type is never looked up. Which catalog speaks for a plugin is one rule in one place (`PackCatalogs.listed`), used by selection, by the shadowing note and by `--list-catalog-features`: the first catalog that lists the `group:artifact` at all, with a pack, without one, or with an entry that was left out. A name, its full name and the coordinates of its plugin in one command are one fetch and one selected feature. Messages made of catalog text are bounded: lists name three and count the rest, a message of `choose` has at most 1,000 characters, and coordinates cannot be longer than 181. Copies are read and written only when `.grails`, `forge` and `catalogs` under the home are, as far as they exist, directories and no links, owned by the owner of the home, and not writable by group or others; otherwise one warning says that no copy is read or kept. `catalog*.tmp` files older than an hour are removed when a copy is written. `--offline` and the fallback to an older copy say the time and the age of the copy. The guard of the specs is no property: `PackArtifacts.beforeEachRequest` takes a callback from code, and a Spock global extension in the test sources of `grails-forge-cli` installs one that throws `PackArtifacts.RequestRefused`, an `AssertionError` nothing catches, for a host that is not this computer. Tested in `CreateAppFeatureCatalogSpec` (152 with `HttpsPackArtifactsSpec`, which now holds the time of a catalog against the real client over TLS on loopback: no answer, an answer that stops, an answer that drips) and `NoFeatureCatalogSpec` in `grails-forge-web`, which shows the catalog and artifact classes are not on the hosted Forge's class path. Not done, and why the plugin index is not read by default: a catalog is a full trust root (it chooses the coordinates, can name its own repository and supplies its own SHA-256), so with a default catalog a mistyped feature name could fetch and run a third-party pack without the user having asked for any catalog. The default stays off until the index publishes packs, entries are reviewed before they are published, and selection by name asks for confirmation; none of the three exists. Also not done: confirmation before a named catalog's pack is used (the lines are printed, the command goes on); the Grails BOM as the source of managed versions (Forge has versions for seven artifacts); duplicate keys are refused in every object, read or not, which costs a set of keys per object while it is read; `--list-features` with an unknown name reads a named catalog, as creating would.

Implemented: the plugin index as the catalog, and the BOM of the application; where this differs from the three paragraphs above, this is what the code does. There is one catalog format, the index's own: `grails-plugins-index.json` as `apache/grails-plugins-metadata` publishes it, a JSON array of plugins. The object with `format` and `plugins` that was read before is refused (`the document is not an array of plugins, as the plugin index is`): the plugin page and the other consumers of the index read an array, so the indexer will not wrap it. What is read of a plugin: `coords`, `versions`, `name`, `desc` (an empty one, which the index has, is none), `docs`, `maven-repo`, `deprecated` (a text is the reason, which is shown; `true` deprecates without one; `false`, the text `false` and an empty text do not). Of a version: `version`, `coords` and `maven-repo` of its own, `grailsVersion`, `artifactAvailable`, and `pack`.
