The `pack` member is the smallest thing the indexer can compute from a jar without running anything, what `META-INF/grails-forge/feature.yml` says and the digest of the jar: `format` (1), `kind` (`feature` or `preset`), `name`, `templateApi`, `compatibility.grails`, `compatibility.forge`, `sha256`, and `applicationTypes` when the descriptor declares them; nothing the index already has is in it, and a `coords` or `maven-repo` in it is not read. The artifact of a version is the version's own `group:artifact`, which the index has for 1,370 of its 5,081 versions because plugins were published under other coordinates over time (1,295 of them in another group), or else the plugin's, in that version; the repository the catalog names is the version's own or else the plugin's. This is the power to make a plugin's name fetch another artifact, which an earlier review had removed from the `pack` member, so it is never used silently: the line before anything is fetched says `The feature <name> is taken as <fetched coordinates>, the coordinates the catalog gives for version <v> of the plugin <entry>, which the feature catalog ... names for it, with the SHA-256 .... Asking <repositories of the user>, and then <repository>, the repository the catalog names`; `--list-catalog-features` shows `<fetched coordinates> of <entry>`; the lock and the manifest record the fetched coordinates, as `PackArtifact` has them; the full name `group:artifact#name` and the rule of which catalog speaks are keyed on the entry's `coords`; and the rule of the BOM is applied to the fetched coordinates. `grailsVersion` is the range of the plugin's descriptor, `lower > upper`: read with `>` or `>=`, any spacing, `*` for the upper version or for a part of a version, a qualifier after the lower version (which is the release it leads to), and an upper version, which is included; these are all the forms the index has (`7.0.0 > *` 2,608 times, `7.0.0  > *`, `2.4 >= *`, `3.1.* > *`, `5.3.0 > 6.2.3`). A pack is for a Grails that both `grailsVersion`, when the version has one, and `compatibility.grails` have; neither is taken over the other, and the refusal names both. A version with a pack whose `grailsVersion` is not such a range is left out by itself, with a line and a count of versions beside that of plugins, and the other versions of the plugin are found. A version with `artifactAvailable: false` is not offered. No order of the versions is relied on: the index lists them newest first, and the latest release is found by comparing. Limits, from the index of 2026-11-20 (638,899 bytes, 257 plugins, 5,081 versions, 303 of one plugin and more than 100 of seven, 26,167 values, texts of at most 678 characters, coordinates of at most 58 and versions of at most 16): 4 MiB, 5,000 plugins, 1,000 versions of a plugin, 200,000 values, 4,096 characters of a text that is read, 120 of `group:artifact`, 60 of a version. Measured: the whole index is read in a 32 MiB heap with 6 MiB in use afterwards; a document of 4.19 MiB with 980 plugins and 11,760 packs, each version with coordinates, a repository and a range of its own, in a 48 MiB heap with 29 MiB in use, and it does not fit in 32 MiB, which is then the one-line refusal. Reading the real index found two things the invented format had hidden: a description that is an empty text, which left the plugin out, and a slip in the reader that kept no member of an object, which no document of the specs had caught because every spec wrote its catalog with the writer of the spec. Tested: `PluginIndexCatalogSpec`, against a part of the real index that is among the test resources (23 plugins of commit `b17759d`, picked for the overrides, the 303-version plugin cut to 210, the forms of `grailsVersion`, `artifactAvailable`, deprecation; see the README beside it), the same with `pack` members, and the whole index when `-PpluginIndex=<file>` names a copy; `CreateAppFeatureCatalogSpec` for the rest.

The version rule is that of the BOM the application gets (item 3). The BOM of Grails has the version of the CLI, so what it manages is known when Forge is built: the task `grailsBomTables` of `grails-forge-core` writes, for `grails-bom` and for `grails-hibernate7-bom`, a resource `META-INF/grails-forge-boms/<artifact>.tsv` of `group:artifact`, a tab and the version, sorted, from the poms the included build generates for publication (`generatePomFileForMavenPublication` of `grails-base-bom`, `grails-bom` and `grails-hibernate7-bom`). A table has what the pom of the BOM manages itself and what `grails-base-bom`, which it imports, manages, each version resolved from the properties of the pom that declares it; nothing is resolved from a repository, so what Spring Boot's BOM and the other imported BOMs manage is not in it. `grails-bom` has 264 artifacts and `grails-hibernate7-bom` 274, 166 of them of `org.apache.grails` groups in each; no artifact is in both at different versions (4 are only in the first, 14 only in the second); each manages 27 of the 257 plugins of the index, all of `org.apache.grails`. `BomVersions` in `grails-forge-core` reads a table; it is data, and the hosted Forge has it without any code of the catalog. `GrailsBase.bomArtifactId(micronaut, hibernate7)` is the one place that says which BOM an application gets, for the generator and for the command. The create command decides the BOM before a catalog is read, from what the command line says: `--data hibernate7` or the feature `gorm-hibernate7`, and the feature `grails-micronaut`. For the artifact that would be fetched, managed by that BOM: the pack of exactly that version, or the refusal `<BOM>, which the application gets, manages <artifact> at <version>, and the catalog has no pack for <version> (it has one for ...); another version is used only by its coordinates, such as ...`; not managed: the latest compatible release. With the Micronaut feature the application gets `grails-micronaut-bom` or `grails-hibernate7-micronaut-bom`, which this build does not make and Forge has no table of (decided 2026-11-20: left so for now; the Micronaut BOMs are published apart from this build and are to become an entry of the plugin index, and their versions are read from there then): a name is then refused before anything is chosen (`A feature is not taken from a feature catalog for an application with <BOM>: this Forge does not have the versions that BOM manages ...`), which is the design's "fail if the needed BOM version mapping is unavailable"; coordinates work as ever. After the features of the application are known, the BOM they give is compared with the one the packs were chosen for, and a difference is refused (exit code 2) and not chosen again. `ContextFactory.getCoordinateResolver()` and the rule of the seven artifacts of Forge's own `pom.xml` are gone. Tested: `BomVersionsSpec` in `grails-forge-core` for the tables, and in `CreateAppFeatureCatalogSpec` each BOM through the command, with an artifact only `grails-hibernate7-bom` manages (which shows the table that is used is the application's), one both manage, one neither manages, the two Micronaut BOMs, and the comparison after selection with a BOM given by the spec.

The indexer (item 1) is code of the other repository and is not changed from here. A change to it is drafted and not merged or proposed: `index-plugins.groovy` adds the `pack` member to a version whose main jar has `META-INF/grails-forge/feature.yml`, reading the descriptor as data (SnakeYAML's `SafeConstructor`, no alias, anchor or tag, 256 KiB and 20 levels as `PackDescriptorParser` has them), copying only the members above with the SHA-256 of the jar it downloads anyway, logging and passing over a jar whose descriptor cannot be used, and leaving versions that are already in a YAML file alone unless it is run with a new `--rescan-packs`. It was run against a repository on the loopback interface with jars made of the pack of the `plugin-forge-pack` test project, and the index it wrote was read by `PackCatalog`. Not done: that change proposed to and merged in the other repository, and a review of entries there that knows what a `pack` entry gives a plugin; tables for the Micronaut BOMs; what a BOM imports from outside this build; a confirmation before a pack that a catalog chose is used; and the rest of the "Not done" of the paragraphs above, which stands.

<a id="project-state"></a>
### 2.4 Project state: `.grails/`

```
.grails/
  project.yml      committed; project intent, type, options and requested features
  forge.lock.yml   committed; exact resolved generation inputs and baseline index digest
  baseline/       committed; pristine generated outputs, never copied from user-edited files
    index.yml     paths, content digests, text/binary kind and executable mode
    files/        generated file tree, excluding .grails state itself
  cache/          ignored; derived command metadata and compiled templates
  transactions/   ignored; the lock, staged updates and backups (the journal of a change is kept outside the project, see 2.6)
  run/            ignored; lifecycle pid, port and stopping markers
  history         ignored; interactive console history
```

```
format: 1
grailsVersion: 9.0.0
applicationType: web
name: shop
defaultPackage: com.acme.shop
options: { jdk: 21, servlet: tomcat, data: hibernate7, reloading: devtools }
generationState: tracked
requestedFeatures:
  - name: gsp
  - name: grails-spring-security
  - name: acme-audit
    coordinates: com.acme:acme-audit-grails
    version: 2.3.0
excludedFeatures: []
parameters: {}
```

The manifest records user intent; the lock records the fully resolved selection, including defaults and requirements. Lock the exact generator release/build identity, target Grails/BOM metadata, template API, normalized options and output OS/line-ending policy, preset and pack artifacts/digests, non-secret parameters and template override digests. `grailsVersion` alone is not a generator identity, especially for snapshots. Do not include credentials, absolute user paths or runtime secrets in any committed generation state.

The baseline stores actual rendered bytes and modes so a fresh clone has the same merge ancestor even when an artifact disappears or the author changes a template. It contains only generator-owned outputs; it is not a backup of arbitrary application files. The storage cost is intentional: some generated source and binary resources are duplicated in Git. The baseline index and lock make tampering, missing files and manual state drift detectable. Generation is deterministic (no current timestamps or ambient machine inputs in managed output); non-repeatable packs cannot participate in automatic `feature add`.

The CLI validates the manifest/lock/baseline as a set. A manual change to generation inputs, the framework version or an override template does not silently update the baseline. V1 `feature add` requires the exact recorded generator identity; it directs the user to that CLI version on mismatch and does not automatically download and execute an old generator. Generator upgrades and changes to existing pack versions are separate migration/reconciliation operations, not side effects of adding a feature. All generated projects, including hosted Forge ZIPs, receive this state for their bundled features.

Implemented: writing and reading the state when an application is generated (`org.apache.grails.forge.core.state`). `DefaultProjectGenerator` renders every file of the application once and before anything is written, builds the state from those bytes, reads the state back from its own bytes with the reader, and only then writes: the application, then `.grails/baseline/files/<path>` with the same bytes, `.grails/.gitignore`, `.grails/.gitattributes`, `.grails/baseline/index.yml`, `.grails/forge.lock.yml` and, last, `.grails/project.yml`, for every output (the directory of a create command, the ZIP of the hosted Forge, the map of a preview). A template that fails and a state the reader would refuse leave nothing behind. The manifest is the commit point: a directory with other files of the state and no manifest was not generated to the end, the reader says so, and generating into it again removes `.grails/baseline` and writes the state anew; a directory with a manifest is refused with what to do (remove `.grails`). Before anything is written to a directory, `.grails`, `.grails/baseline` and `.grails/baseline/files` have to be absent or real directories and the five state files absent or regular files, checked without following a link; no message has an absolute path. The manifest has `format: 1`, `grailsVersion`, `applicationType` (`web`, `rest-api`, `web-plugin`, `plugin`), `name`, `defaultPackage`, `options` (`jdk`, `servlet`, `data`, `reloading`, written as the create commands take them), `generationState: tracked`, `requestedFeatures` (the features asked for by name, by their canonical names, sorted, each `- name:`, and for a feature of a pack that was taken by coordinates its `coordinates` and `version`), `excludedFeatures` (the names given with `--exclude-features`) and `parameters` (always empty). The lock has `format: 1`, `manifest.sha256` (the digest of the bytes of the manifest), `generator.name` (`grails-forge`) and `generator.version`, `grailsVersion`, `templateApi`, `applicationType`, `options`, `lineEndings: preserve` (the files have the line endings of their templates on every operating system), `repositories` when `GRAILS_REPO_URL` put repositories in place of those of Grails (each `kind: remote`, `local`, `maven-local` or `maven-central`, a remote one with its `url` when that is an `https` address without user information, of which the scheme, host, port and path are written and the query and fragment are not: `ProjectLock.recordableAddress`, the one rule, which the `origin` of a pack goes through as well after `PackArtifact.getRecordableRepository()`; a directory, a `file:` address and an address with a password are never written; the first 50 are named and `repositoriesNotNamed` counts the rest), `features` (every feature of the application in the order it is applied, each with `name` and `source`: `framework`, `bundled-pack` or `pack`; one of a pack that was taken by coordinates has `coordinates`, `version`, `sha256` and, when the repository is an `https` address without user information, whatever host that is and in whatever case `https` is written, `origin`), `parameters`, `baselineIndex.sha256` and, as its last two lines, `seal.sha256`, the digest of every byte of the lock before those lines. The lock has no `operatingSystem`: no generated file depends on it, and the hosted Forge takes it from the User-Agent of whoever asks. The index has `format: 1` and `files`, sorted by path, each with `path`, `sha256`, `kind` (`binary` when the template says so, as those of the wrapper scripts do, or when the content is not UTF-8 text or has a NUL, as an icon has that its template does not call binary; `text` otherwise) and `executable`; the copies under `files/` are never executable themselves, and a step of a path that is `.gitignore` or `.gitattributes` in any case, or one of them followed by `.baseline` any number of times, is written with one more `.baseline` after it, so that the baseline has no file Git reads (`BaselineIndex.copyPath`). `.grails/.gitattributes` is `* -text`, so that a Git that converts line endings keeps the bytes of the state; `.grails/.gitignore` negates `/project.yml`, `/forge.lock.yml`, `/.gitattributes`, `/.gitignore`, `/baseline/` and `/baseline/**`, so that a pattern of the application's `.gitignore`, of a global one or of `.git/info/exclude` leaves no file of the state out (checked with Git against global `*.yml`, `*.lock.yml`, `*.jar` and `.grails/*`); a pattern that ignores the directory `.grails` itself cannot be undone from inside it, which the guide says; the reader asks for neither file. `grails-forge-cli.yml` is still written, is no part of the baseline, and says the repository of a pack only when the lock does. All three files are YAML in block style, UTF-8, with line feeds and no time in them, so the same inputs give the same bytes, whatever the operating system argument is; for that `PropertiesTemplate` no longer writes the time of day `java.util.Properties` starts a file with, and ends its lines with a line feed on every platform. A feature that writes below `.grails`, or a file whose path the index cannot name (the rules of a pack's destination, `PackDescriptorParser.isPathBelowRoot`), fails the generation before anything is written. A package with a part that is the name of a device on Windows, and an application of such a name, are refused where the name is parsed (`NameUtils.parse`), as a usage error of the create commands and with status 400 by the hosted create, preview and diff; the hosted ZIP is made before its response is begun, so that every refusal of the generator is a 400 and not a 500 after a 201. `NameUtils.isValidJavaPackage` no longer takes a package that ends with a dot. With `--inplace`, a file that exists is kept as before and the baseline has the file as the generator rendered it: the existing file is to the baseline what an edited file is, and the create command names the kept files whose content, or whose missing executable bit, is not what was generated, twenty of them and a count. `ProjectState.read(Path)` reads the three as a set and `ProjectState.readSelection(Path)` the manifest and the lock without reading the index, which has to be there; `CodeGenConfig` uses the second. Both refuse, with a `ProjectStateException` of one line: no state, a part of it missing (with what to do: when the index and the directory of the copies are there and the manifest or the lock is not, that the files were not committed and an ignore rule may be why; when the manifest and the index are missing, that the generation did not finish; otherwise to restore the file), a format other than 1, a lock that does not end with the digest of itself, a lock whose `manifest.sha256`, `applicationType`, `grailsVersion` or `options` are not the manifest's, a requested feature that is not in the lock or is there with other coordinates or another version, an excluded feature that is in the lock, an index whose digest is not the lock's, a file or a directory on the way that is a symbolic link, text that is not UTF-8, YAML with an alias, an anchor, a tag, a duplicate key or more than six levels, a key it does not read, a value that is not what its key takes, a name that is not one step of a path or a package and name that `NameUtils.parse` does not give back, and an index path that is absolute, has `..`, is below `.grails` or `.git`, or is there twice for a file system that ignores case. The bounds are one set of constants (`StateLimits`) for the writer and the reader: manifest 256 KiB, lock 1 MiB, index 8 MiB, 20,000 files, 1,000 features, a name or version 255 characters, coordinates 401, an address or a path 2,000, a file of the baseline 16 MiB and all of them 256 MiB. They are applied to the text before the YAML library sees it (a line has at most 4,200 characters, and a file at most the lines its entries can take: 6,264 the manifest, 6,364 the lock, 80,016 the index) and to the events of the parser before anything is built (four for a line), because the scanner of the library looks ahead in a line at a cost that grows with the square of its length. What a message repeats of a file has the characters replaced that the pack parser replaces (control, format, surrogate and private-use characters, line and paragraph separators) and is cut. `ProjectState.verifyBaseline(Path)` checks the copies against the index: a missing, changed, oversized or unlisted file is refused. `ProjectState.isAsGenerated(Path, String)` says whether a file of the working tree is what the baseline has: a `text` file is compared with every CR LF taken for LF on both sides, a `binary` file byte by byte; nothing but the specs calls it (`feature add` compares the same way, with the bytes it has read). `grails-forge-cli.yml` (and `grails-cli.yml`) is read as the state is (`EarlierRecord`): at most 256 KiB, 8,192 lines of at most 65,536 characters, without aliases, anchors or tags, every value text, and `CodeGenConfig` holds each value to what it can be (a type of application, a package, a way of reloading, a language, at most 1,000 names of features); a file that is not so gives one warning line and no configuration, so the command line is then in a directory without a project and its help works. A file of more than one YAML document is refused, where it was merged before. A name for a new application is refused where it is parsed when it has a control or format character, a slash or a backslash, or when the application's own name is not a step every file system takes (`PackDescriptorParser.isPathBelowRoot`), with a message that repeats it through `FeatureSelectionException.shown`; `parse(name, false)`, for a project there already is, takes what it took. An entry of `GRAILS_REPO_URL` with a control or format character, a quote or a `$` is refused without being repeated, and `DefaultGradleRepository` escapes the backslash, the quote and every control character of what it writes between the quotes of the build file; before, the entry was written as it was, so that a line break in it put code into `build.gradle`, which was so before there was a state (d6216f7a65 has the same code). Before a directory is written to, a file where the project needs a directory, a directory where it needs a file and a directory that cannot be written to are refused by their paths in the project; a failure of the file system while writing is an `OutputFailedException` that names the file by its path in the project, says what the file system said without its path, and names up to ten of the files written before. The generated `.gitignore` ignores `.grails/cache/`, `.grails/transactions/`, `.grails/run/` and `.grails/history` and nothing else of `.grails`. Every application is generated with a `.gitattributes` at its root, written by the Gradle build feature beside `.gitignore`: `*.bat text eol=crlf`, `gradlew text eol=lf`, `grailsw text eol=lf` and `*.jar binary`. The four wrapper scripts stay binary in the index, and with these rules Git gives every clone the bytes they were generated with: the `.bat` files have CR LF and nothing else, so storing them with LF and checking them out with CR LF gives them back, and the other two have no CR. The rules do not reach the baseline: `.grails/.gitattributes` unsets `text` for everything below `.grails`, and Git converts nothing whose `text` is unset, also where the root file's `eol` still applies; the copy of the root file is `.grails/baseline/files/.gitattributes.baseline`. Checked by hand with Git, for a commit and a clone with `core.autocrlf` false, input and true each (nine combinations): before, the wrapper scripts were not as generated in seven of the nine (`gradlew` and `grailsw` on a clone with `true`, the two `.bat` files after a commit with `input` or `true`); now every file of every clone is as generated by `isAsGenerated`, and every copy of the baseline has the digest of the index. A spec holds the generated bytes to what that relies on, without Git. The code generation commands go by `.grails` when `.grails/project.yml` is there, warn in one line when `grails-forge-cli.yml` names another default package or other features, and go by `grails-forge-cli.yml` when there is no state or it is refused, in which case the reason is a warning; the Forge CLI does not read the record of the current directory for a create command or for `--version`. The preview shows the manifest, the lock and the two files for Git and leaves out `.grails/baseline/**`; `FeatureDiffer` leaves out all of `.grails`, so that its difference can be applied to a project by hand without the state ceasing to read. `BundledPackGoldenSpec` ignores `.grails/**` as it ignores `grails-forge-cli.yml`; its golden files are unchanged.

Not done: besides the code generation commands only `feature add` reads the state (section 2.6): no `feature diff` against the baseline and no baseline adoption (`project init`); `project migrate` writes `generationState: untracked` for an application that has no record (revisions 41 and 42), and establishes no baseline. The digests bind the three files against a change made by hand or by accident; they are no signature, and a tool that rewrites all three reads as generated. The lock has no build identity of the generator beyond its version, so two builds of one snapshot are one generator to it; it has no BOM metadata beyond `grailsVersion`, no preset and no template override digests, and for a pack of a directory or a jar on the command line nothing but that it is a pack. The options of the manifest are the options after the defaults were applied: what the user left out is not told from what the user chose. `--inplace` over existing files records `generationState: tracked` and names the kept files on the console only: the adoption of a file whose content is the generated one is not recorded anywhere, and a kept file that differs is not marked in the state as the design's "adoption is recorded" asks. The root `.gitattributes` has no `* text=auto`: how the text files of an application are stored and checked out is left to the settings of whoever commits and clones, and with `core.autocrlf=true` a clone has them with CR LF, which `isAsGenerated` allows for and a byte comparison would not. The limit of 256 MiB for a baseline is checked by the writer and the verifier and has no spec that reaches it. `MapOutputHandler` still discards binary content, so a preview has no binary files to show. A repository address of an organisation's own host is written to the lock when it is an `https` address, as it is to `build.gradle`. `--help` still reads the record of the current directory, because it lists the code generation commands the project has. A generation that stopped after the index and before the manifest reads as a clone without its manifest does, with the advice for a clone; creating the project again with `--inplace` completes it all the same. Step 5's removal of `grails-forge-cli.yml` and the move of the legacy `CodeGenConfig` parsing into migration are not done.

Implemented, slice a of step 5, which takes the place of what the two paragraphs above say of `grails-forge-cli.yml` being written: the generator writes no `grails-forge-cli.yml` (the template `forge/feature/cli.gsp` is gone) and no `profile` dependency, for every output, the hosted ZIP and the preview among them. The baseline is every file of the application. A feature that writes `grails-forge-cli.yml` or `grails-cli.yml` at the root of the application, in whatever case, fails the generation before anything is written, as one that writes below `.grails` does, because the command line reads a file of that name as the record of a project (`ProjectStateRecorder.check`). Reading is as it was: `EarlierRecord`, `CodeGenConfig` (the state first; the earlier record for a project without one or with one that cannot be used; the warning when a record beside the state names another package or other features), `ConsoleProject`, which still takes a directory with one of the two files for a project. What `grails-forge-cli.yml` had and the state has not is the file name of the jar of a pack that was taken by coordinates (`featurePacks[].artifact`): the lock has its coordinates, version and SHA-256, and for a timestamped snapshot the digest is what tells the build. `GrailsProfiles` is `GrailsProjectSettings` (feature `grails-project-settings`, not shown), which writes `grails.codegen.defaultPackage` and `grails.profile`; `Scope.PROFILE`, `Phase.PROFILE`, `GradleConfiguration.PROFILE` and `Dependency.Builder.profile()` are removed. The lock of an application therefore names `grails-project-settings` where it named `grails-profiles`; the name is one that sorts where the old one did among the names of the features, because features of one order are applied by name and the settings of `application.yml` are written in the order they are applied. A generated application whose state cannot be used has no earlier record to go by any more: its code generation commands are not there until the state is restored, which the commands say in one line. The identity of the generator in the lock is unchanged (`grails-forge` and its version): an application that a build of this snapshot generated before the change is refused by `feature add` with the message there was for it, that it cannot be generated again as it was, naming `build.gradle`, and no released version wrote a state. The spec that held Forge's welcome page, layout, error page, assets and message bundles to the bytes of the profile skeletons under `grails-profiles` is removed, so the templates of Forge are the only source and slice c can delete the skeletons; no other spec of Forge read a file of a profile.

Not done: `FeatureAddition` still has the branch that changed a `grails-forge-cli.yml` together with the state, and `ProjectTransaction` still lets a journal name the file; neither is reached for an application this generator made, and they were left because that package is being changed elsewhere. The legacy parsing in `CodeGenConfig` is where it was; it moves when `project migrate` exists. The `DirectoryScanner` workaround in `settings.gradle` and the launcher in `grails-cli` belong to slices c and d. What this paragraph said was left of `grails.profile` in generated output and in `grails-forge-web` is done by slice g, below.

Implemented, slice g of step 5: nothing of the framework reads `grails.profile`. `GrailsApplicationPostProcessor.setApplicationContext` adds no profile to the environment, `Settings.PROFILE` is removed and the key has no configuration metadata any more, so the active Spring profiles of an application are the ones that were asked for and the name of the Grails environment (`GrailsApp.configureEnvironment`), with the key or without it. What the injection did was measured before it was removed, by running the fixtures below with it: the profile was added when the context was handed to the post processor, which is after the configuration files are read, after the conditions of the configuration classes are evaluated and after the early phase has registered the beans of the plugins, so a `@Profile('web')` bean was not registered, `application-web.yml` and a `spring.config.activate.on-profile: web` document were not read (a document of `application.yml` follows the `spring.profiles.active` system property alone, which is how `YamlPropertySourceLoader` selects), and a plugin that asked the environment in `doWithSpring` did not see it; only the active profiles as code reads them once the application runs had it, at their end. That list is the one behaviour an application can observe to have changed. What the scan had not established about `profiles` of a plugin: the property has not been read since 7.1.0. `DefaultGrailsPlugin.evaluateProfiles`, the one place that read `GrailsPlugin.PROFILES`, was removed by the commit that moved plugin discovery ahead of the context (`3e67c83c16`, in every release from 7.1.0), the field it filled stayed `null`, and `isEnabled(String[])` has answered `true` for every plugin since, at each of its callers (`GrailsApplicationPostProcessor.applyPluginBeanRegistrars`, `GrailsEarlyPluginRegistrationPostProcessor.applyBeanRegistrars`, and `AbstractGrailsPluginManager`/`DefaultGrailsPluginManager` for the runtime configuration, the artefact configuration, the dynamic methods, shutdown, class changes and observers). Had it been read, the injected profile would not have been among the profiles of the early phase of 8.0, which runs before `GrailsApplicationPostProcessor` exists (runtime configuration, artefact configuration, bean registrars), and would have been among those of the later calls. The declaration is therefore retired and not revived: the dead field and the override in `DefaultGrailsPlugin` are removed, `GrailsPlugin.PROFILES` is deprecated for removal, `isEnabled(String[])` stays as the question the plugin manager asks any `GrailsPlugin` with the real active profiles, and `def profiles = ['web']` is removed from the twenty-two plugin classes of the repository that had it (the sixteen of the scan and six plugins under `grails-test-examples`); none gets a condition in its place, because none was disabled by the declaration in any application of 7.1 or later. Forge: `GrailsProjectSettings` writes `grails.codegen.defaultPackage` only, the welcome page has no profile row, `welcome.app.profile` is out of the nineteen message bundles, `templates/plugin.gsp` declares no `profiles`, and `grails-forge-web` has no `grails.profile`. In a plugin the default package is now the only key under `grails`, and the YAML writer puts a key that is alone on its path on one line (`grails.codegen.defaultPackage: ...`); the golden files of the two cache packs changed by that and by nothing else. Repository: the key is removed from 110 `application.yml` files outside Forge, the profile modules and the shell (four of them fixtures of `grails-gradle`), with the row or field that showed it in the welcome pages and JSON views of the test examples, and the tests that used the key as their sample of configuration read another key. `GrailsAppRuntimeProfileSpec` in `grails-core` holds the fixtures this slice was gated on, each without the key and with it: the active profiles and `acceptsProfiles`, `@Profile` beans, a document of `application.yml` and an `application-<profile>.yml` file of a profile, the `environments` block, `spring.profiles.active` given as an argument, as a system property and in `application.yml`, `spring.profiles.include`, a plugin that declares `profiles`, a plugin that asks the environment, and a context `GrailsApp` did not start; thirteen of its twenty-six cases fail with the injection put back.

Not done, slice g: `BuildSettings.PROFILE` and the `grails.profile` system property the Gradle plugin read for the default profile dependency were left to slice b, which removed both with the default dependency; the `profile` dependency lines of example builds (slice e); the files under `grails-profiles` and `grails-shell-cli`, which still have the key and the declaration (slice c deletes them); the pages of the guide that describe profiles (slice f). `LifecycleCommandsSpec` and `LauncherSpec` of the CLI still use a `profile` key as sample input, of a configuration and of an earlier record, and `pack-template-views/31-*.txt` is the copy of an example page it was; none is read as a profile. `plugin.gsp` still takes the `applicationType` model it no longer uses.

Implemented, reading the record back: `project show [--json]` and `project regenerate (--output DIRECTORY [--force] | --diff)` of the Forge CLI (`ProjectGroupCommand`, `ProjectShowCommand`, `ProjectRegenerateCommand`), found in a project as `feature add` is and marked as commands of a project. `project show` reads the manifest and the lock (`ProjectState.readSelection`, so a baseline that was changed does not keep it from showing) and prints the type, name, package, Grails version, generator, `generationState`, the four options, the requested and the excluded features, the repositories of the lock, every feature of the lock in its order with its source and, for one taken by coordinates, its version, SHA-256 and origin, and as its last line the create command: the name of the command line as it was started, `create-app`, `create-restapi`, `create-web-plugin` or `create-plugin` by the type, `<defaultPackage>.<name>`, `--jdk`, `--servlet`, `--data` and `--reloading` each only when the manifest has another value than the create commands take when the option is not given, `--features` with the requested names in the order of the manifest (a feature with coordinates as `group:artifact:version`), `--feature-pack-repository` for each origin the lock has, `--feature-pack-sha256 group:artifact:version=<sha256>` for each such feature, and `--exclude-features`. An argument with a character outside `[A-Za-z0-9_@%+=:,./-]` is put between single quotes, a single quote in it as `'\''`; control and format characters are written as `?`. What the line cannot say is said in a note before it: a pack of a directory or a jar (the lock has `source: pack` and no coordinates), coordinates without an origin, repositories of `GRAILS_REPO_URL`, and a generator version that is not the running one. Every value of the record goes through `FeatureSelectionException.shown`. `--json` is one line: `format` (1), `applicationType`, `name`, `defaultPackage`, `grailsVersion`, `generator` (`name`, `version`), `templateApi`, `generationState`, `options`, `requestedFeatures`, `excludedFeatures`, `repositories`, `repositoriesNotNamed`, `features`, `command` (`name`, `arguments`, `line`) and `notes`, in that order, a member that the record has not left out. `project regenerate` uses the replay of `feature add`, which is now `FeatureAddition.regenerate` (the checks of step 1 and the replay of step 2 of section 2.6, taken out of `plan` unchanged, returning the files as a `RegeneratedProject`), so it refuses what `feature add` refuses with the messages of `feature add`, an application with the feature of an external pack among it, and it ends no interrupted change. `--output` resolves the directory against the current one, refuses one that is a link or no directory, one that is the project or above it, and one that has an entry unless `--force` is given (exit 1, as the code generation commands refuse a file that is there), checks every path as the code generation commands check theirs (no link and no file on the way, the names as they are written), writes every file with its executable flag, and then compares `.grails/project.yml`, `.grails/forge.lock.yml`, `.grails/.gitattributes`, `.grails/.gitignore` and everything below `.grails/baseline` of the two directories byte for byte: the first path, in the order of the paths, that differs or is in one of them only is named and the exit code is 1, with what was written left there. `--diff` writes nothing, to a temporary directory either: the regenerated files are compared in memory with a walk of the project that does not follow links and leaves out `.git` and what the regenerated root `.gitignore` leaves out (its patterns are read by the command: a name, a path from the root, `*`, `?`, `**`, a trailing `/`, `!`; the last match decides and nothing below a directory that is left out is looked at). A file of the baseline index is compared with `ProjectState.isAsGenerated`, another file byte for byte; the lines are `added`, `removed` or `changed` and the path, sorted by path, and the exit code is 1 when there is one.

Not done, of reading the record back: the options that equal the defaults are not on the command line, and the manifest does not say whether an option was given, so the line is the same application only because a selection that is accepted with an option given is accepted to the same result without it when the option has the default value; the round trip is tested for the options and features of the spec and not proved for every selection. The command line is for a POSIX shell; there is no quoting for `cmd.exe` or PowerShell. `project regenerate` does not regenerate an application with an external pack (the replay takes no packs), nor one of another generator version; `project show` is what there is for those. The executable flag is not compared by `--diff`. A pattern of `.gitignore` with a character class or a backslash is not applied. The messages of the refusals speak of adding a feature.

Implemented, for an application that is created as a project of an existing Gradle build (`--subproject`; section 2.5, "Multi-project builds"): `options` of the manifest and of the lock have a fifth key, `layout: subproject`, written only for such an application and read as optional with that one value; the files of the root of a build are not written and so are in no baseline.

Keep `grails.codegen.defaultPackage` in `application.yml` because runtime code reads it. In 9.0, `grails.profile` no longer drives runtime Spring-profile injection and new projects omit it; `.grails/project.yml` is tooling metadata, not runtime configuration. The 8.1 runtime and generator retain their existing keys and profile dependency.

**Migration to 9.0.** `grails project migrate --to 9.0 --dry-run` performs file-only preflight and displays a migration plan; it must work even when the old `profile` declaration prevents the new Gradle plugin from configuring. Applying that plan uses the same staging, conflict and recovery mechanism as section 2.6. Supported inputs and limits are explicit:

- Read `grails-forge-cli.yml` or legacy `grails-cli.yml`, known built-in profile names, declared framework version and recognizable build dependencies as evidence. A build-script scan is not evaluation of arbitrary Groovy and cannot establish a complete feature set. Report missing/ambiguous options, custom profile coordinates, convention-plugin indirection and multi-project ownership; require explicit mappings or manual edits instead of guessing.
- Remove a recognized profile dependency and obsolete marker only for an explicit 9.0 migration after the replacement metadata is staged. Do not modify an application that remains on 8.1. Version bumps, unrelated dependency upgrades and custom skeleton conversion remain guided upgrade work, not implicit actions of this command.
- Inventory the effective old Spring profile, including implicit `web`. Profile-conditional beans and configuration may depend on it. Produce guidance for deliberate Spring-native activation or removal; do not silently discard it or claim that adding `spring.profiles.include` is timing-equivalent to the old postprocessor. Fixtures must cover `@Profile`, profile-specific configuration and pre-existing explicit profiles. Ambiguous/dynamic activation needs a manual migration decision before completion.
- Legacy metadata does not prove a pristine Forge baseline. Mark migrated projects `generationState: untracked`; lifecycle commands and codegen work, and feature diffs are advisory, but automatic `feature add` is unavailable. `project init` establishes a 9.0 generation baseline from explicitly chosen type/options/features/preset and a review of the full generated-versus-current diff. It does not pretend current edited files are pristine generator output. Adoption records its origin and establishes the inputs for future three-way merges without replacing application source.
- Custom profile authors convert supported defaults, dependencies, configuration and added files into a preset plus feature packs, and commands into the command extension API. Unsupported inherited file replacement/multi-project layouts receive a documented manual recipe. Test both automatic built-in cases and refusal/diagnostics for unsupported cases.

<a id="console"></a>
### 2.5 The console after profiles

The catalog combines the following sources. Built-in names are reserved; extension collisions produce deterministic diagnostics with origin information instead of depending on classpath iteration order. Namespaced extension IDs allow explicit selection where short names collide. Retained script overrides require an explicit supported override declaration rather than silently shadowing a built-in.

1. **Built-in lifecycle commands**, moved from profile scripts into Picocli commands over the existing Tooling API services: `run-app`/`stop-app` (retain HTTPS/port/debug options and process handling), `test-app`, `compile`, `clean`, `assemble`/`war`/`package`, `console`, `shell`, `dependency-report`, `run-command`, `run-script`, `stats`, `bug-report`, and plugin-aware `publish-plugin`/`package-plugin` task adapters. Preserve test-app's unit/integration selection, filters and continuous/cancellation behavior rather than mapping it to `integrationTest` alone.
2. **Gradle passthrough**: `grails gradle <args>` with completion from `ReadGradleTasks` (exists). Fix: arguments are passed as one string today (`GradleCommand.groovy:62-65`).
3. **Plugin `ApplicationCommand`s** from `-cli` companions through `ApplicationContextCommandFactory` and `GradleTaskCommandAdapter` (exists, profile-free apart from the `ProfileCommand` interface). Fix the two known gaps: legacy Grails 7 commands are listed but have no named Gradle task (`GrailsCliGradlePlugin.groovy:411-414`), so route them to `runCommand`; app-local `grails-app/commands` are invisible because only `grailsCliClasspath` jars are scanned (`:450-480`).
4. **Artefact codegen**, one implementation in Forge: retain `create-controller`, `create-service`, `create-domain-class`, `create-taglib`, `create-interceptor`, `create-job` and `add-property`; add `create-unit-test`, `create-integration-test`, `create-functional-test`, `create-restful-controller`, `create-domain-resource`, `create-command` and `create-script`. Pack artefacts register dynamically from selected, locked packs. Template lookup is app `src/main/templates/<path>`, then the owning pack, then Forge's built-in template. An override is explicit and fingerprinted; it is not permission for unrelated packs to overwrite one another. Extend `install-templates` to copy these templates into the application.
5. **Script commands** (`src/main/scripts/*.groovy`, `META-INF/commands/*`, `GroovyScriptCommand`): retain the documented, profile-free DSL in 9.0 with deprecation, and remove it no earlier than the next major. Replace command chaining through `profile.hasCommand` with catalog dispatch and profile-template lookup with the new lookup order. Re-home the necessary script API types so the removed `grails-shell-cli` artifact is not a transitive requirement. Scripts that directly use `Profile` or custom profile inheritance need source migration; do not promise binary compatibility for those internals. Convert the inventory's script-only commands: Geb `create-functional-test`, Quartz `create-job`, `s2-create-acl-domains`, `s2ui-create-challenge-questions`, `s2ui-override`, dbmigration `dbm-create-changelog` and `dbm-changelog-to-groovy`, plus `sql-tail` if its script implementation is present on the implementation branch. Track explicit names, not the earlier inconsistent command count.
6. **Catalog services**: help, completion, abbreviation expansion (`rA` for `run-app`), "did you mean", and the legacy-command hint move from `AbstractProfile.groovy:361-499` into a profile-free `CommandCatalog`.

Project detection walks upward to the nearest `.grails/project.yml`, legacy marker or `grails-app`, stopping at a filesystem/repository boundary. The manifest identifies the owning Gradle project in multi-project builds; never silently execute a sibling project's task ("Multi-project builds" at the end of the implemented slices says how). Legacy markers permit migration diagnostics, not automatic adoption of an invented baseline.

**Gradle passthrough, as implemented (step 4, slice 1).** Implemented: the Forge CLI has a `gradle` command (`GradleCommand`, registered in the `subcommands` of `ForgeCommand`, so it is listed outside a project too and the record of the project is not read for it). Every argument after `gradle` is passed to Gradle as the element of the argument vector it was: a picocli parameter preprocessor takes the rest of the command line before any option is read, and the options every other command has (`-h`, `-V`, `-x`, `-v`) are removed from this command's model, so `-x`, `-v`, `--stacktrace`, `--help` and `--` are Gradle's. The command runs the Gradle wrapper script of the project as a child process (`gradlew`, through `sh` when it is not executable; `gradlew.bat` on Windows) in the project directory, with the standard input of the CLI. Standard output and error are the CLI's own, inherited by the child (`ProcessBuilder.inheritIO`), when `System.out` and `System.err` are still the streams `Application.main` started with, so Gradle sees the terminal there is (colour, progress) and nothing is transcoded; when they were replaced, as a spec does to read them, the bytes Gradle writes are copied to them as they come, and only on that path the copying is waited for at most 10 seconds after Gradle has ended, for a process that holds the pipe. The project is found by `org.apache.grails.forge.cli.console.ConsoleProject`: the nearest directory from the current one upwards with a `grails-app` directory, `.grails/project.yml`, `grails-forge-cli.yml` or `grails-cli.yml`; the wrapper is the nearest one from the project directory upwards; neither search goes above a directory that has `.git`, or more than 32 levels up, and the message says which of the two ended it, or that there was no directory further up. Trust, after git's `safe.directory`: the current directory is the project when it is one, whoever owns it, because the user chose to run there. A project found above the current directory is used only when its directory and each of `grails-app`, `.grails`, `.grails/project.yml`, `.grails/forge.lock.yml`, `grails-forge-cli.yml` and `grails-cli.yml` that exists in it are owned by the user who runs the command; otherwise no project is used, for any command (the code generation commands are then not there, `feature add`, `stats` and `bug-report` refuse, `help --json` has `projectDirectory: null`, `complete: false` and the reason as a diagnostic), and the one line names the path that is not the user's, its owner, and the directory to run the command in instead. The wrapper is a regular file, or a link whose target is inside the directory the link is in. A wrapper in the project directory is run as it is when the project is the current directory; when the project was found above, the wrapper (and the target of a link) is owned by the user too. A wrapper above the project is run only from a directory that is owned by the user and has `settings.gradle` or `settings.gradle.kts`, and is itself owned by the user, also when the project is the current directory. The search stops at the first project and the first wrapper it finds: one that is refused is not passed over for one further up. "The user who runs the command" is the owner of a file the process creates (a temporary file, created and deleted once per run and only when something above the current directory is looked at; the owner of `user.home` when no temporary file can be created): that is the effective user on POSIX, root under `sudo` (so root does not run from below it what another user owns; `SUDO_UID` is not consulted), and on Windows the user, or the Administrators group for an elevated process, which then takes for its own what that group owns and not what the unelevated user created. Where the owner of a file cannot be read, nothing above the current directory is used. The check is a predicate (`ConsoleProject.setOwnedByUser`) that specs replace to simulate another user's files. Because Gradle runs in the project directory, an unqualified task name selects the tasks of that project in a multi-project build. Exit codes: 0 when Gradle ends with 0; 1 with one line after Gradle's own report when it ends with anything else, when there is no project or none that is trusted, when there is no wrapper or none that is run, or when the wrapper cannot be started (no fallback to a Gradle on the `PATH`; on Windows also when the path of the wrapper or of the project has one of `"%&|<>^` or a control character, and when the JVM refuses the command line with an `IllegalArgumentException`); 2 with the usage text when no argument is given, and on Windows when an argument is empty, contains one of `"%&|<>^` or a control character, or contains a space and ends with a backslash, none of which a batch file receives unchanged (the argument is echoed through `FeatureSelectionException.shown`). When the thread running the command is interrupted, or the JVM of the CLI shuts down, every process the wrapper started is destroyed on every platform: the descendants of the child (`Process.descendants()`), deepest first, then the child, and after 10 seconds by force whatever of them is still alive. A Gradle daemon that was running before is no descendant and is not killed: it cancels the build when its client is gone; a daemon this run started is a descendant and ends with it. A run that was stopped says `Gradle was stopped before it finished, because the command was cancelled` whatever exit code the child ended with, also from the shutdown hook. In the interactive shell the interrupt of the terminal (Ctrl-C) while a command runs interrupts the thread of the command (`InteractiveShell.execute` registers a JLine handler for `INT` for the time of the command), which stops Gradle as above and returns to the prompt; at the prompt it leaves the shell, as before. Covered by `GradleCommandSpec` through `ForgeCli.execute`, with the wrapper of a generated application around a build that resolves nothing, and for trust, links, cancellation and bytes with wrappers that are scripts of a few lines.

Not done (as it stood after slice 1; see "How Gradle is run: a hybrid, and why" below for what holds now): the Tooling API is not used. `org.gradle:gradle-tooling-api` is a dependency of `grails-shell-cli` only, its version is a property of the root `gradle.properties` and not a BOM constraint, and it is not on the class path of `grails-forge-cli`; the execution target `gradle` of this section is therefore a wrapper process for now, without a cancellation token. There is no task-name completion (`ReadGradleTasks` stays in the shell). The shutdown hook, the inherited output (a spec replaces the streams, so it always takes the copying path) and everything specific to Windows (the batch-file invocation, ending the processes below the command interpreter, ownership by the Administrators group) are not exercised by a spec: the refusals on Windows are tested by setting `os.name`, the rest was not run on Windows. Ownership by another user is simulated through the predicate; with the real file system a spec only holds that what it created is the user's and that `/` and `/bin/sh` are not. The trust rule looks at owners, not at permissions: a project of the user's own in a directory others can write to is used, and what the wrapper script itself runs (`gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar`, the distribution its properties name) is not checked. There is no setting like `safe.directory` to trust a project of another owner from below it; the way to use one is to run the command in its directory. The interrupt handler of the interactive shell is exercised on a JLine terminal that is none (`dumb`), by raising the signal, not through a terminal. picocli expands an argument of the form `@file` before the command sees it, as for every command of the CLI. The code generation commands still read the current directory only and do not use `ConsoleProject` (they use it since slice 2, below). The shell's `gradle` command is unchanged: it joins the arguments that do not start with `-` with spaces and gives Gradle that one string as a single argument (`grails-shell-cli/.../GradleCommand.groovy:60-66`, `DefaultCommandLine.remainingArgsToString`), so two task names become the name of one task that does not exist, and arguments that start with `-` are parsed as options of the shell and do not reach Gradle.

Implemented, after a second review; where this paragraph and the one above differ, this one holds. Trust of what is above the current directory looks at owners, modes and link counts: the directory that holds a marker or a wrapper is owned by the user and, on POSIX, not writable by others (`.grails`, `grails-app`, `gradle` and `gradle/wrapper` likewise); a group that can write is not looked at, as git looks at the owner only; each file that is checked is owned by the user and has a link count of 1 where `unix:nlink` is there, because a hard link has the owner of the file it is another name of, which for root is root; beside the wrapper itself, `settings.gradle(.kts)` of a root above the project and `gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar` and `gradle-wrapper.properties` are checked like the markers. A wrapper that is a link is run when its real path is inside the directory the link is in, at any depth (`gradlew -> gradle/real`), and the guide says so. Access control lists are not read and nothing is pinned between the check and the run; the guide says that too, and that a project in the current directory is used whoever owns it. The user is established by `org.apache.grails.forge.cli.console.ProcessUser` from the process and from no temporary file or home directory, in this order: the user id of the process (`com.sun.security.auth.module.UnixSystem.getUid`, by reflection, where files have `unix:uid`), on Linux the `unix:uid` of `/proc/self`, then `ProcessHandle.current().info().user()` compared with the name of the owner of a file (what Windows has); when none answers, the refusal says that the user could not be established and which sources were asked. The way out is the environment variable `GRAILS_SAFE_DIRECTORY` (`ConsoleProject.SAFE_DIRECTORY`): absolute paths separated by the path separator, each compared by real path with the directory of the project or of the wrapper, no patterns, the single value `*` for every directory; a directory that is named is used whatever its owner, modes and link counts are. Every trust refusal names it. An unknown command below a project that is not used has a second line with the reason (`CommandCatalog.unknown`). Cancellation: the stopped line is said once, by the shutdown hook or by the thread of the command, whichever comes first, and before the wait for a process that ignores the signal; an exit code of 128 plus a signal number (and Windows' code for Ctrl-C) is waited on for up to a second for the JVM's own shutdown or the interrupt to become known, so a signal the terminal sent to Gradle and the CLI alike is reported as stopped, and a wrapper that another process ended is reported with its exit code. In the interactive shell the interrupt cancels a command only while `GradleRunner.isRunning()`; during any other command it ends the JVM with 130 (`InteractiveShell.interruptedOutsideGradle`), which is what it did before commands could be cancelled, since generation has no point at which it can stop and leave its files whole. `bug-report` applies the link-count rule to `build.gradle` too, and `.env.*` and `id_*` names are left out whatever they end with (only `truststore*`, `credentials*` and `secrets*` are exempt for source files). Catalog copies (`PackCatalogs`): "yours" is `ProcessUser`, not the owner of the home; the warning and the `--offline` failure say which directory is not used and why, with `chmod go-w` where the mode is the reason; a plugin with more than 200 versions none of whose first 201 has a pack is a problem line; an `OutOfMemoryError` while reading is "too large for the memory this command has"; `"deprecated": "false"` does not deprecate. The files slice 4 keeps in `.grails/run` and its record are the user's when `ProcessUser` owns them or the project is named in `GRAILS_SAFE_DIRECTORY` (`ConsoleProject.isOwnedByUser(Path, ConsoleProject)`), and the process a record names is the user's by `ProcessUser.isOfThisUser`; the catalog file of slice 5b is read only for the project `ConsoleProject.find` gives, so for none that is not trusted and for one that is opted in. `GradleRunner.start`, which `run-app --detach` uses, finds the wrapper by the same check as `run`; `isRunning()` counts only a build that is waited for, so a detached one is not cancelled by the interrupt of the shell. Specs: hard links, a directory everybody can write to and one its group can, the opt-in and the order of the identity sources are exercised on the real file system without the owner predicate; `ForkedCli` runs `Application.main` in a JVM of its own for the inherited output, the shutdown hook and for holding that a program nobody named a catalog to asks no computer for one; `NoFeatureCatalogSpec` of the hosted Forge takes the class names from the sources of the CLI.

Not done: a directory that a group can write to is trusted, also where the group is one the user shares with others and not the user's private one (umask `002` with a group for each user is common, and refusing it refused too much); the members of that group can put a project or a wrapper there. This holds for what is above the current directory only: the directory of the catalog copies is the CLI's own and is still used only when neither its group nor others can write to it. `UnixSystem.getUid` is the real user id, not the effective one; they differ only for a set-uid launcher. On Windows only the owner is compared, by name. The `OutOfMemoryError` message, the interrupt of a real terminal and a signal sent to the process group are not held by a spec (the last two were run by hand with the review's `sig.py`).

**Catalog for the commands the CLI has, as implemented (step 4, slice 2).** Implemented: `org.apache.grails.forge.cli.console.CommandCatalog` is built from the picocli model of the command line that `ForgeCli.createCommandLine` puts together, per invocation and in the interactive shell per command, so it holds what is registered and nothing has to be declared twice. `ForgeCli.execute` gives it the command line before picocli reads it (`CommandCatalog.expand`). Short forms follow the shell's rule, by calling the shell's `ScriptNameResolver.resolvesTo` from `grails-bootstrap`: as many parts as the name has words, each the start of its word, parts told apart by upper case letters and hyphens (`cA`, `creA`, `creat-app`, `CA` are `create-app`; `ca` and `c` are not). Differences from the shell, all deliberate: a short form that fits several commands is refused with exit code 2 and the candidates (`AbstractProfile.handleCommand` runs the first that fits); what is tried as a short form has only letters and hyphens and at most 64 characters (the shell's rule takes the letters out of any text, so a path would match); hidden commands have no short form. The name or alias of a command is that command before any short form is tried. Only the positions where a command is expected are looked at: the arguments after the options of the root that take no value (also written together, `-xv`), and after a command that has commands and no parameters of its own (`feature`) the next argument; the walk ends at `--`, at an option the command does not have, at an argument file that exists (`@file`), and at the first command that has parameters or no commands, so the arguments of `create-app` and the raw vector of `gradle` are never rewritten. An argument at such a position that is neither a command nor a short form is refused there, exit code 2 with the usage text: `There is no command X`, plus `Did you mean: a or b or c?` from picocli's own similarity ranking of the visible commands, which is asked with what was typed and is not asked at all for an empty argument or for one that is not shown as it was typed (shortened, or with a character replaced). When an option of the root that asks for the help or the version came before it (`--help bogus`, `-V bogus`, `-xvh bogus`), nothing is refused and the command line goes to picocli as it is, which answers with the help or the version. This replaces picocli's `Unmatched argument at index 0` for an unknown command, which repeats the argument with its control characters; what was typed is repeated through `FeatureSelectionException.shown`. `help [COMMAND...]` (`HelpCommand`, registered in `ForgeCommand`) prints the usage text picocli prints for `--help` of the root, of a command or of a command of a command, resolves names and short forms through the catalog, and refuses an unknown or ambiguous one in the same two ways; `help gradle` is the only way to the usage text of `gradle`, whose `--help` is Gradle's. `help --json` writes the document below. Project commands in a subdirectory: `CodeGenConfig.load` reads the record of `ConsoleProject.directoryFor(current directory)` (the project the directory is in by the bounds of slice 1, or the directory itself), the configuration remembers that directory, and `CodeGenCommand.getProjectDirectory()` is where the code generation commands write and where `add-property` looks for the domain class; `feature add` takes its project from the same lookup. The lookup stops at the nearest project: a project without a record inside a project with one has no code generation commands. Because a short form is resolved among the commands of the project too, the record is read before the command is known; what reading it says (a record that cannot be used) is held back and printed only when the command that was meant has to do with the project, and for `help --json` it becomes the diagnostics of the document; `--json` is that option only before a `--`, so `help -- --json` is the help of a command named `--json`, which there is not, and what was held back is printed. `complete` is `false` when a record that is there could not be used (`CodeGenConfig` tells whoever it reports to through `CodeGenConfig.RecordListener`) or when a project above the current directory is not trusted; a warning about a record that is used, as that `grails-forge-cli.yml` and `.grails` do not say the same, is a diagnostic of a document that is complete. The usage width of 100 columns is set when every command has been added to the command line and again by `ForgeCli.execute`, because picocli gives it only to the commands there are when it is set: the commands added after the root (the code generation commands, the built-ins of slice 3, what a caller adds) had picocli's 80. Completion in the interactive shell: `InteractiveShell` completes from a command line that `ForgeCli` puts together again after every command of the shell (`InteractiveShell.refresh`), so the commands of a directory that a command made a project, or of a feature that was added, are completed without leaving the shell; before, the completer kept the commands of the moment the shell started, while dispatch already used a new command line per command. Covered by `CommandCatalogSpec` through `ForgeCli.execute`, with applications generated by `create-app` and not built; the completer and its refresh are exercised directly, not through a terminal.

What a command does to be in the catalog: be a picocli command that is either named in the `subcommands` of `ForgeCommand` (or of a command registered there, as `add` is in `FeatureCommand`), or a `CodeGenCommand` listed in `ForgeCli.CODE_GEN_COMMANDS`, or added to the `CommandLine` that `ForgeCli.createCommandLine` returns before `ForgeCli.execute(CommandLine, String...)` is called with it. Nothing else: short forms, `help`, `help --json`, the generated completion script and the man pages read the model. Three things are the command's own choice. It implements the marker `org.apache.grails.forge.cli.console.ProjectCommand` when it cannot be used outside a project (`CodeGenCommand` does, so its subclasses do), which is what `"project"` in the document says and changes nothing else. It finds its project with `ConsoleProject.find` or `directoryFor`, never with the current directory alone. And when it is named in `ForgeCommand` and its availability depends on the project, it is listed everywhere and refuses outside a project itself, as `gradle` does; only `CODE_GEN_COMMANDS` are left out of the command line where they do not apply. A name whose short form collides with another command's is allowed and makes that short form ambiguous for both: with the artefact commands of slice 6, `cC` is `create-controller` and `create-command` and `cS` is `create-service` and `create-script` in every project, and both are refused. The artefact commands of slice 6, `install-templates` and the lookup of a project's templates under `src/main/templates` go through `CodeGenCommand.outputHandler()`, which is on the project directory, so they too read and write at the top of the project from any directory of it.

**`help --json`, format 1.** One JSON document on one line of standard output; nothing else is written to standard output, and warnings about the project record are in `diagnostics` instead of being printed. Members can be added without a new format number, so a reader ignores what it does not know; the number changes only when a reader of the earlier format would misread the document.

```
{ "format": 1,
  "complete": true,                    false when commands of the directory could not be found
  "diagnostics": ["one line each"],    why not, and warnings that leave the document complete
  "projectDirectory": "/abs/path",     the project the current directory is in, or null
  "commands": [
    { "name": "create-controller",     what is typed; unique among the commands of one parent
      "aliases": [],
      "description": "one line of plain text, without markup",
      "project": true,                 cannot be used outside a project
      "origin": "built-in",            where the command comes from
      "options": [ { "names": ["-f", "--force"], "arity": "0", "label": null, "required": false, "description": "..." } ],
      "parameters": [ { "label": "CONTROLLER-NAME", "arity": "1", "description": "..." } ],
      "commands": [ ... ]              the commands of the command, same shape
    } ] }
```

`arity` is text: `"N"`, or `"MIN..MAX"` with `*` for no upper bound. `label` is `null` for an option without a value. Hidden commands, options and parameters are left out. Today every command is `built-in` and `complete` is `false` only when a record of the project cannot be used or the project above the current directory is not trusted (section "Gradle passthrough"); `diagnostics` can have lines when `complete` is `true`. The document is meant to be the catalog file of slice 5 as well: the build side (5a) writes a file with `format`, `complete`, `diagnostics` and `commands`, each command with `name`, `description`, `origin` and, as far as the descriptor knows them, `aliases`, `options` and `parameters` (absent means not known, an empty list means none); the CLI side (5b) appends those commands to `commands`, sets `project` to `true`, turns a missing or stale file into a diagnostic, and reports a name collision as a diagnostic with both origins. Two members are reserved for that and are not written by `help --json` yet (the catalog file of slice 5a writes both, and a third, `registration`): `origin` values other than `built-in` (the coordinates `group:artifact:version` of the artifact a command comes from, or `application` for `grails-app/commands`), and `target`, one of the execution targets of this section (`console`, `standalone`, `application`, `gradle`, `session`), absent for a command the CLI runs itself. A canonical command ID for selecting between colliding names is not part of format 1; it can be added as a member.

Not done: there was no legacy-command hint in this slice; the table of old commands is slice 9's and is there now (below). Abbreviations of options are not offered. An unknown option is still reported by picocli, which repeats it as it was typed. Completion outside the interactive shell: the generated completion script (`buildCompletion`) and the man pages list `help`, and like before only the commands named in `ForgeCommand`, since they are generated at build time from the model without a project; the code generation commands are not in the script, and a script that asked the CLI for the commands of the current directory would start the application context for every completion (about a second), so it waits for the start without the context of slice 8. Startup is unchanged: every invocation, `help` and `help --json` included, starts the Forge application context first; the catalog itself needs none (it reads the picocli model), which is what slice 8 can build on. `help --json` describes the options from the model without interpolating anything a pack contributes at run time. Paths the code generation commands report stay relative to the project directory, also when the command was given below it. The shell (`AbstractProfile` dispatch, its `HelpCommand`) is unchanged.

**Gradle-target built-ins, as implemented (step 4, slice 3).** Implemented: `org.apache.grails.forge.cli.console.GradleRunner` is the one place that runs Gradle for a command: it finds the wrapper of a `ConsoleProject`, builds the command line from a list of arguments, gives Gradle the output of the CLI or copies its bytes where the output was replaced, turns the exit code of Gradle into 0 or 1 with one line, refuses on Windows an argument a batch file does not receive unchanged, and ends Gradle when the command is cancelled. It is the code of slice 1's `GradleCommand` moved, and `GradleCommand` uses it; what it does is described with slice 1 above. Over it the CLI has these commands, one picocli class each in the same package, all taking their own options and then everything after `--` for Gradle unchanged (a parameter preprocessor removes `--` and what follows before picocli reads the command line):

| Command | Gradle arguments | Options of the command | In the shell |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `compile` | `classes` | none | `compile.yml` |
| `clean` | `clean` | none | `clean.yml` |
| `assemble`, aliases `war` and `package` | `[clean] assemble -Dgrails.env=<env>`, production by default | `--clean`/`-clean`, `-Dname=value` | `assemble.groovy` with its synonyms, and `package.yml` |
| `dependency-report [CONFIGURATION]` | `dependencies [--configuration CONFIGURATION] -Dgrails.env=<env>`, development by default | `-Dname=value` | `dependency-report.groovy` |
| `test-app [TEST...]` | `testApp [--phase P]... [--tests T]... [--rerun-tests] [--debug-jvm] [--continuous] -Dgrails.env=<env>`, test by default: the one task of the Grails Gradle plugin that runs every `Test` task of the project (revision 43) | `--unit`, `--integration` and a switch of the name of every other test phase (`--functional` for `functionalTest`), `--clean`, `--continuous`, `--debug-jvm`, `--tests T`, `-Dname=value` | `test-app.groovy` |
| `package-plugin` | `jar` | none | `plugin/commands/package-plugin.yml` |
| `publish-plugin` | `publish` | none | `plugin/commands/publish-plugin.yml` |

On every one of them `--stacktrace` adds `--stacktrace -Dgrails.full.stacktrace=true` (the shell's `GradleInvoker`) and `--verbose` adds `--debug` (the shell's `GradleStep`, which maps `-verbose` to `-d`). `-Dgrails.env=NAME` replaces the default environment and the other `-D` options follow it, each as one argument. `-Dname` is the property without a value and `-D name=value` is accepted; a `-D` that stands alone and is followed by nothing or by something that is not `name=value` (`test-app -D BookSpec`, which picocli would read as the property `BookSpec` and run every test), and a `-D` without a name (`-D=x`), are usage mistakes with exit code 2, checked by the preprocessor before picocli reads the command line. The first line of the description of every command is a sentence of its own, because the list of commands of the root shows that line and no other. After a successful build `assemble` says where the archive is and for which environment, and `test-app` says `Tests PASSED`, as the shell does; a failed build ends with 1 and the one line of the runner. Two commands need no Gradle: `stats` counts the files and lines of code by the kind of class with the shell's table, categories and line-counting rule, reading only the eleven source directories, no links, at most 100000 files, and not the lines of a file over 16 MiB; a source directory that is reached through a link at any step of its path (`src -> /elsewhere`), or whose real path is outside the real path of the project, is not counted and is named in a warning (`ConsoleProject.isLinked`, which the search for the class of a plugin uses too); `bug-report` writes `build/<directory name>-bug-report-<ddMMyyyy>.zip` with the shell's content (`grails-app/**`, `src/**`, `build.gradle`), through a temporary file in `build`. `install` is a hidden command that runs nothing, names `gradle publishToMavenLocal` and ends with 1. Registration is one call in `ForgeCli.createCommandLine`, `ConsoleCommands.addTo(commandLine, readsProject)`: every command but the two plugin adapters is added in every directory and ends with 1 and the message of `gradle` outside a project; `package-plugin` and `publish-plugin` are added when the project of the current directory has a file whose name ends with `GrailsPlugin.groovy` under `src/main/groovy` (a bounded walk without links), so elsewhere they are unknown commands (exit code 2), as the code generation commands are outside a project. Covered by `ConsoleCommandsSpec` through `ForgeCli.execute`: the fixture of `GradleCommandSpec` with tasks of the real names that print the task requests, system properties and switches of the start parameter, so each case asserts the whole Gradle invocation.

Changed on purpose against the shell, each with a line in the guide: `test-app --continuous` is Gradle's continuous build (runs the tests at once, watches their real inputs, ends on cancellation) and not the shell's `DirectoryWatcher`, which runs nothing at the start and ends at once outside the interactive shell; `test-app --integration` in a project without `src/integration-test` is refused with 1, where the shell calls Gradle with no task; `assemble --clean` runs `clean`, where the shell declares the flag and ignores it; test names and the `--configuration` value are separate arguments, where the shell joins them with spaces and splits again; an option a command does not have is a usage mistake (2) and reaches Gradle only after `--`, where `GradleStep` passes any unknown flag on with one dash; `bug-report` follows no link (the Ant fileset of the shell follows them), leaves out version-control and editor files as Ant's default excludes do, leaves out files that are keys or lists of secrets by their name, compared without regard to case (`.env`, `.env.*`, `*.env`, `*.jks`, `*.p12`, `*.pfx`, `*.pem`, `*.key`, `*.keystore`, `*.p8`, `*.ppk`, `*.kdbx`, `*.der`, `*.asc`, `*.gpg`, `.netrc`, `.npmrc`, `.pypirc`, `cacerts`, `id_rsa*`, `id_dsa*`, `id_ecdsa*`, `id_ed25519*`, `truststore*`, `credentials*`, `secrets*`, and each of these followed by `.bak`, `.old` or `~`, also several of them; the names that are given by how they start do not hold for `.groovy`, `.java`, `.gsp`, `.gson` and `.kt` files, so `CredentialsController.groovy` is in the ZIP), leaves out every directory named `build` or `.gradle` below `grails-app` and `src` at any depth, leaves out a file that has more than one name (a hard link count above one, read as `unix:nlink` where the file system has that attribute; not detected elsewhere), leaves out a file whose path in the ZIP would have a control character and warns with the number of them and the first, does not replace an existing ZIP without `--force`, refuses a `build` that is a link or a file, and writes nothing above 10000 files or 256 MiB. `grails-app/conf` is in the ZIP as it is, as in the shell: before the lines that say what was created, a warning on a line of its own names each `grails-app/conf/application*.yml`, `.yaml`, `.groovy` or `.properties` file that went into the ZIP as where passwords usually are, and the guide says the same.
