Not done: the environment in front of the command (`grails prod war`) and the abbreviations are dispatch and belong to slice 2; `-Dgrails.env=NAME` after the command is the form that works now. `test-app`'s shell synonym `test` is not registered (in the shell `test` in first place is the environment). `-plain-output` is not mapped: Gradle chooses its console by the terminal it sees, `-- --console=plain` asks for the plain one, and `--plain-output` is slice 8's. No completion of test names (`TestsCompleter`) or of configurations: slice 2 owns completion. The commands are not yet entries of a `CommandCatalog` with an execution target, and `GradleRunner` is still a wrapper process, synchronous only: the asynchronous runner and the Tooling API decision are slice 4's. Plugin `ApplicationCommand`s are slices 5a and 5b. The profile scripts and YAML of the same names stay until step 5. No end-to-end run against a generated application (slice 9). A directory named `build` that is a package of the application (`src/main/groovy/example/build`) is left out of a bug report with the others; the command says how many directories it left out.

Implemented, after a review: `GrailsPicocliFactory` asks the application context only for a class that is not an interface, not abstract, not a type of the JDK (loaded by the bootstrap class loader), and is annotated `@CommandLine.Command` (the commands that are beans: those named in `ForgeCommand`, and `FeatureAddCommand`), implements `IVersionProvider` (`GrailsVersionProvider`), or is an `Iterable` that is no `Map` (the completion candidates `WebAvailableFeatures`, `RestApiAvailableFeatures`, `PluginAvailableFeatures`, `WebPluginAvailableFeatures`, beans that need the features injected); a class of these kinds that is no bean, and everything else (the converters, the preprocessors, the collection or map of an option, whatever type its field has), comes from `CommandLine.defaultFactory()`. Before, it asked the context for every type, so a field typed `java.util.Map` received the `grailsConfigProperties` bean, into which the values of the option were put and stayed for the next command line of the interactive shell, and `Collection` and `Queue` fields received collections of Spring. The `-D` option is a plain `Map<String, String>` again. `ConsoleCommandsSpec` holds that nothing of a command line reaches the system properties or a bean, with `-D` and with a command that has `Map`, `Collection`, `Queue` and `Properties` options.

**Application lifecycle and session commands, as implemented (step 4, slice 4).** (The choice of runner this paragraph records was a shortcut and is superseded: see "How Gradle is run: a hybrid, and why" below.) Decided for this slice: the runner stays a wrapper process, and the Gradle Tooling API is not added to the Forge CLI (it is not managed by the Grails BOM and not on the class path of `grails-forge-cli`). What that costs: there is no cancellation token and no build events, so a build is cancelled by ending the Gradle client, and a Gradle daemon that was running before cancels the build when it notices that its client is gone (measured by hand on Gradle 9: the process `bootRun` started ended about a second after the client was ended; no spec holds it); readiness is asked of the port and not of the build; every run pays the start of a Gradle client JVM; and the process `run-app` records is the Gradle client, not the JVM of the application, which with a daemon is a child of the daemon.

Implemented, all in `org.apache.grails.forge.cli.console` of `grails-forge-cli`, registered through `ConsoleCommands.addTo` and project commands like the commands of slice 3 (they end with 1 outside a project and in a project above the current directory that is not the user's):

| Command | Gradle arguments | Options of the command | In the shell |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `run-app`, alias `run` | `bootRun [--debug-jvm] [--quiet] -Dgrails.env=<env> [-Dgrails.server.port=PORT] [-Dgrails.server.address=HOST] [-Pgrails.gorm.queryTail.enabled] [-Pname=value]...`, development by default | `--port`, `--host`, `--https`, `--debug-jvm`, `--query-tail` (each also with one dash), `-Dname=value`, `-Pname=value`, `--detach`, `--foreground`, `--ready-timeout SECONDS` | `run-app.groovy` |
| `stop-app`, alias `stop` | none | none | `stop-app.groovy` |
| `console` | `console [--debug-jvm] [--quiet] -Dgrails.env=<env>` | `--debug-jvm`, `-Dname=value` | `console.groovy` |
| `shell`, alias `sh` | `shell [--debug-jvm] [--quiet] -Dgrails.env=<env>` | `--debug-jvm`, `-Dname=value` | `shell.groovy`, which runs nothing and names `gradle shell -q` |
| `run-script FILE...` | `runScript -Pargs=<files> -Dgrails.env=<env>` | `-Dname=value` | `run-script.groovy` |
| `run-command NAME [ARG...]` | `runCommand --args-file=.grails/run/command-args-N.json -Dgrails.env=<env>` | `-Dname=value`, before `NAME` | `run-command.groovy` |

`--quiet` is left out with `--verbose`, which for these commands does not add `--debug` (`GradleTaskCommand.verboseArguments`, a hook the commands of slice 3 leave as it was); `--stacktrace` and what follows `--` are as in slice 3, except that `run-command` has no `--`. `run-app --https` adds `-Dgrails.server.port=8443` when no port is given or configured, and `-Dgrails.server.ssl.key-store=<project>/build/ssl/keystore` with the two passwords of the shell (`123456`) unless `-Dgrails.server.ssl.key-store` was given; the key store is created when it is not there, by running the `keytool` of `java.home` (the shell calls the class of `keytool`), and a failure to create it is a warning, as in the shell.

*The runner.* `GradleRunner` has two additions. `run(project, arguments, started)` tells a callback the process as soon as it is started. `start(project, arguments, log)` is the asynchronous one: it starts the wrapper and returns its `ProcessHandle` without waiting; the build reads nothing, and what it writes is added to the file. On POSIX it is started through `/bin/sh` with job control (`set -m`), so it is in a process group of its own and the interrupt of the terminal of the CLI is not its, and with the hang-up signal ignored; a shell that has no job control without a terminal (dash) starts a background command with the interrupt ignored instead. It is not a session of its own: the JVM cannot call `setsid`, and macOS has no such command. On Windows the batch file is started as it is with its output redirected. `GradleRunner.stop(ProcessHandle, seconds)` is the tree stop of slice 1 made callable: the descendants deepest first, then the process, by force after the time, and it says whether all have ended.

*`.grails/run`.* `RunDirectory` is the directory `.grails/run` of a project. It is used only when `.grails` and `.grails/run` are directories and not links and `.grails/run` is owned by the user (`ConsoleProject.isOwnedByUser`); it is created with permissions for its owner only, files in it likewise (where the file system has POSIX permissions), a file is created after what had its name was removed, so nothing is written through a link, and the directory gets a `.gitignore` with `*`. The `.grails/.gitignore` the generator writes is unchanged: it only takes files back in, and changing it would change the baseline of every generated project. In the directory: `app.json` (the record), `app.log` (the output of a build that was left running, truncated by each `run-app`), `app.stopping` (written by `stop-app` for a `run-app` that stays with the application, which then says `Application stopped` and ends with 0, as the stop marker of the shell does), and `command-args-N.json`.

*`run-app`.* It stays with the application unless `--detach` is given or the command runs in the interactive shell (`ConsoleCommands.setInteractive`, which `ForgeCli.interactive` sets while the shell runs) without `--foreground`: the shell's `run-app` stays in the foreground outside the interactive mode and returns to the prompt in it. Staying: `GradleRunner.run`, with the record written when the process is started and removed when it ends; cancelling ends the tree as for every Gradle command. Not staying: nothing is started when something answers on the port already; then `GradleRunner.start`, the record, and a wait until the application answers, the process ends, the time is over (`--ready-timeout`, 300 seconds by default, 1 to 86400) or the command is cancelled; in the last three cases what was started is ended, the record removed, the last 20 lines of the log shown and the exit code 1. Answering is asked of `127.0.0.1` and `::1` only (`ApplicationPort`): a connection, then `HEAD /`, and an answer with the `Grails-Startup-Phase` header other than `READY` is none yet, as in the shell's `ServerInteraction`; no name is looked up, so `--detach` is refused (2) for a `--host` that is not `localhost`, the text of a loopback address or of the address for all, and ends with 1 when the configuration names such an address. The port is `--port`, `-Dgrails.server.port`, the `server.port` of `grails-app/conf/application.yml` (plain values, all documents, then `environments.<env>`; read with SnakeYAML's `SafeConstructor`, at most 1 MiB, not through a link), 8443 with `--https`, or 8080. An application started in the interactive shell without `--detach` is ended by a shutdown hook of the CLI (`RunAppCommand.stopWhatTheShellStarted`), as the shell ends its own. A second `run-app` while the recorded process runs starts nothing and ends with 1.

*The record and `stop-app`.* `app.json` is `{"format":1,"pid":N,"started":<epoch milliseconds of ProcessHandle.Info.startInstant>,"port":N,"detached":true|false,"command":[the arguments of Gradle]}`, written beside itself and moved into place. The command line is in it, with every `-D` value the user gave, which is why the file is the owner's alone. `RunningApplication.find` believes none of it: the file is a regular file owned by the user, at most 64 KiB, JSON of format 1 with a process number from 2 to 2^32-1 and a port from 0 to 65535 that are whole numbers; the process is alive, began at the recorded millisecond, is not the CLI or one of the processes above it, has the user of the CLI, and, where `ProcessHandle.Info.arguments` is there (not on Windows), has `bootRun` among its arguments and the directory of the Gradle wrapper of this project in its command line. `stop-app` then ends the tree of that process (30 seconds, then by force), waits up to 30 seconds until nothing answers on the recorded port, and removes the record. Everything else ends with 1 and one line: no record (`No application that run-app started is running for this project`, the shell's `Application not running.`); a `.grails/run` that is a link or not the user's (nothing read or removed); a record that cannot be read, or of a process that has ended or is not the recorded one (removed); and a process that is the recorded one by number, instant and user but not by its command line, which is not ended and whose record is kept, with the advice to end it by hand, so that a wrapper script this check does not know does not leave an application that cannot be stopped unnoticed. It never looks for what listens on a port.

*Sessions.* `console` and `shell` are the Gradle tasks with `--quiet`; the child has the terminal because `GradleRunner` gives it the streams of the process (slice 1). `run-script` writes the one text the task splits (`CommandLineParser.translateCommandline`, at spaces outside quotes) so that it is split into the files that were given: a file with a space or a quote is put between quotes of the kind it does not have, piece by piece where it has both. Every file name is carried that way but an empty one, which is refused; a file that is not there ends the command with 1 before Gradle is started. `run-command` uses the transport of slice 5a: `CommandArgumentsFile` (a class of its own, for slice 5b to use as well; it needs `RunDirectory`) writes `[NAME, ARG...]` as ASCII JSON to `.grails/run/command-args-N.json`, N the first of 0 to 15 that is free, for the owner only, gives `--args-file=` the path in the project, and deletes the file when Gradle has ended; a file older than a day is one a command left behind and is replaced. The name is stable so that the configuration cache entry of the command line is reused, as slice 5a measured. A parameter preprocessor takes everything after `NAME` before picocli reads it. What the task would refuse is refused first, with exit code 2 and without repeating an argument: U+0000, half a surrogate pair, more than 4096 arguments, more than 1 MiB.

Short forms: `rA`, `sA`, `rS`, `rC` are `run-app`, `stop-app`, `run-script`, `run-command`. New ambiguities: `s` and `st` were `stats` and now also fit `stop-app` (its alias `stop`) and, for `s`, `shell`; `co` was `compile` and now also fits `console`; `c`, ambiguous before, has `console` too. Covered by `LifecycleCommandsSpec` through `ForgeCli.execute`: a wrapper of two lines around a program of one Java file that writes down its arguments and answers on a loopback port, so each case asserts the argument vector, and starting, waiting, the record, stopping, cancelling, hostile records and links are run without Gradle; one feature runs the wrapper of a generated application around a build whose `bootRun` listens on the port, and `runCommand` with a task option.

Not done: nothing in `grails-gradle`. The cut assigned "the PID writer and `bootRun` configuration for the new location" to it; the record here is written by the CLI and is of the Gradle client, so no build change was needed, `GrailsGradlePlugin` still has the application write `build/run-app.pid`, and the two `stop-app` commands do not stop each other's applications. The IDE contract of section 2.5 ("the same configured lifecycle-state location under `.grails/run/`") is therefore not there yet: `gradlew bootRun` from an IDE writes no record. `runAppOptions` (slice 5). `runScript` has no `--args-file`; the quoting above is its transport, and on Windows a file name with a double quote is refused by the rule for batch files. Script arguments: the task takes files only. The environment in front of the command and completion, as for slice 3. `run-app` has no positional argument for Gradle (the shell passes its remaining arguments on as task names). The interactive shell: the flag and the shutdown hook are exercised by a spec that sets the flag and calls what the hook calls, not through a terminal or the end of a JVM; `console` in the interactive shell stays with the console, where the shell's returns to the prompt. A pre-existing daemon that ends the application was probed by hand, not by a spec; when it does not, `stop-app` says after 30 seconds that the port still answers. The readiness check knows `application.yml` only: a port from `application.groovy`, a properties file, `SERVER_PORT`, a placeholder or a profile-specific document is not seen, and `--port` says it. A record is trusted by owner, instant and command line, not by a secret: a process of the user that begins in the recorded millisecond with the recorded number and a command line that has the wrapper directory and `bootRun` would be ended. Between the check and the end of the process there is no lock. `--detach` gives a process group, not a session, and the application keeps the controlling terminal. Windows: the code paths are written (direct start with redirected output, `keytool.exe`, no command-line check) and nothing of this slice was run there; `LifecycleCommandsSpec` is skipped on Windows as a whole. Not run against a generated application (slice 9); the profile scripts, `RunningApplicationProcess` and `ServerInteraction` stay until step 5.

Implemented, after a review of slice 4; where this paragraph and the ones above differ, this one holds.

*Whose record, whose stop request.* A `run-app` that stayed with its application removes the record only while it names the process that run started, by number and instant, and `stop-app` removes the record it read only while it still names that process (`RunningApplication.forget`): before, a `run-app` whose Gradle had ended removed whatever record was there, also the one a `run-app` started since had written, which then ran unrecorded. `app.stopping` holds the number and the instant of the process it is for (`askToStop`), is read and removed only by the `run-app` that stayed with that process (`wasAskedToStop`), and is not removed when a `run-app` starts: `stop-app` followed at once by a new `run-app` leaves the first one its request, so the first ends with `Application stopped` and 0. The record no longer has the arguments of Gradle (`command`): nothing read them, and they hold every `-D` value; a record is the five members `format`, `pid`, `started`, `port`, `detached`, read with `BoundedJson` (depth 2, 16 values), and a process number is at most 2^31-1.

*The command line of the recorded process.* It is the Gradle of the project only in one of two forms, found by equality of files (`Files.isSameFile`) and not by text: an argument that is the wrapper script of the project with `bootRun` among the arguments after it, as while a shell runs the script; or an argument that is `gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar` beside the wrapper, directly after `-jar`, or directly after `-classpath`, `-cp` or `--class-path` with `org.gradle.wrapper.GradleWrapperMain` after it, and `bootRun` after that. Before, `bootRun` anywhere and any argument that contained the directory of the wrapper were enough, which a process that only mentioned a path of the project met, and a project whose path starts with another's.

*`.grails/run`.* A directory that its group or others can write to is refused by every command that uses it, with the `chmod` that mends it. `GRAILS_SAFE_DIRECTORY` lifts the question of the owner for the directory and the record, as for the rest of the project, and not this one.

*Ending the CLI while it waits.* From the start of the build until the application answers, `run-app` that does not stay has a shutdown hook: a signal to the CLI (Ctrl-C outside the interactive shell, `SIGTERM`), which interrupts no thread, ends the tree that was started, removes the record and says `The application was stopped before it answered, because the command was ended`; the exit code is the signal's (130, 143). Before, the build and the record were left. The hook is removed when the application answers: from then on the application is not the command's to end.

*The port.* What the command waits on is what the application is told, in the order the application listens, checked in this repository and in Spring Boot: `--port` and `-Dgrails.server.port` (the Grails Gradle plugin hands every `grails.`-prefixed system property of the build to the application without the prefix, `GrailsGradlePlugin.configureForkSettings`, so these are the system property `server.port` there, which is above the environment and the configuration files); then `SERVER_PORT` of the environment (the application inherits the environment of the build, and Spring Boot binds it to `server.port`); then `application.yml`, where the block of the environment the application runs for comes before the top level, whichever document either is in; then 8443 with `--https`, or 8080. The address likewise (`--host`, `-Dgrails.server.address`, `SERVER_ADDRESS`, `server.address`). Two things the review asked to honour are not honoured, because the application is not told them: `-Dserver.port` is a system property of the build only, which the plugin does not hand on, so with `--detach` it is refused (2) unless `--port` or `-Dgrails.server.port` is given too, and it is passed on untouched when the command stays; and `GRAILS_SERVER_PORT` is bound by Spring Boot to `grails.server.port`, a key nothing in the application reads, so it is ignored. Nothing is guessed: a value that names the port and is not plainly one (not `[1-9][0-9]{0,4}` up to 65535: a placeholder, a fraction, a leading zero, a list) and a file that cannot be read end the command with 1, `The port and the address the application answers on could not be determined`, the reason in the command's own words, and nothing started; when the command stays with the application none of this is asked. `application.yml` is read with duplicate keys refused, at most 20 aliases, nesting of at most 32, at most 1 MiB, the safe constructor, and not through a link; whatever the loader throws is not repeated, so nothing of the file reaches the terminal (before, the loader logged a duplicate key with the key as it was written).

*What an answer is.* An HTTP status line was received (`ApplicationPort.isReady`); a connection that is accepted and says nothing, or something else, is none. When plain HTTP gets no status line the request is made once more over TLS with any certificate taken (loopback only), which is what `--https` and a configured `server.ssl` need. An answer counts only while the process that was started is alive, which is asked again after the answer. When the time is over, the message names the port that was asked and `--port`, says that the build was still running and was stopped, and says when the port took connections without an answer.

*`stop-app` and a daemon.* Its help and the guide no longer say that the application is among the processes it ends: with a Gradle daemon that was there before, the application is a child of the daemon. What ends it then is the daemon cancelling the build when its client is gone, and `stop-app` reports success only when nothing takes connections on the recorded port any more (up to 30 seconds, otherwise 1 with a line that says so). A spec holds both with a wrapper whose application is not below it.

*Arguments on disk.* `CommandArgumentsFile` writes UTF-8 with only `"`, `\` and control characters escaped, which the reader of the task (`StrictJson` in `grails-gradle-model`) takes, so the 1 MiB that both sides hold the file to is the size of the arguments and not of their escapes. Beside `command-args-N.json` is `command-args-N.pid` with the number and the instant of the CLI that wrote it; the next command that writes a file first deletes those whose process is gone (or, without a readable `.pid`, that are older than a minute), which replaces the rule of a day, and a shutdown hook deletes the file when the CLI is ended while Gradle runs. `build/ssl/keystore` is made `rw-------`, also one that is there, and a `build/ssl` the command creates `rwx------`.

*Usage.* The five commands with `-D` have a synopsis of their own (`customSynopsis`) that says `[-DNAME=VALUE]...`, since picocli prints `[-D=NAME=VALUE]...` for a map option and `-D=name=value` is refused; the list of options below it, which the command shares with those of slice 3, still shows `-D=NAME=VALUE`. The options the shell spells with one dash (`-port`, `-host`, `-https`, `-debug-jvm`, `-query-tail`) are taken and hidden: usage and `help --json` show the forms with two. The aliases `run`, `stop` and `sh` are removed: with `run`, the short form `r` started the application. `s` now fits `stats` and `shell`, `co` fits `compile` and `console`, and `st` is `stats` again.

Not done: that the port which answers belongs to the application and not to a program that took it after the first look is not established; the command asks that the process it started is alive and no more, since with a daemon the application is not a process the command knows. `application.groovy`, `application.properties`, documents for a Spring profile and `runtime.groovy` are not read, and a port set there is waited for on the wrong port unless `--port` is given. The signal case is held by a spec with the CLI as a forked program and `SIGTERM`; Ctrl-C on a terminal, where the signal reaches the build as well, was not run. A file of arguments of a CLI that was killed stays until the next `run-command` in that project. Windows as before: not run.

**Build side of plugin and application commands, as implemented (step 4, slice 5a).** Implemented, in `grails-core` (`src/cli`, the `grails-core-cli` artifact) and `grails-gradle`; nothing in `grails-forge-cli`.

*Descriptors.* `CommandFactoriesTransformation`, the compiler transformation that registers a compiled command in `META-INF/grails-cli.factories`, now also writes one file per registered command into the same output directory: `META-INF/grails-commands/<class name>.json` (`CommandDescriptorWriter`; path, limits and the writer of the JSON are `org.apache.grails.gradle.model.cli.CommandDescriptor` in `grails-gradle-model`, which both the compiler side and the Gradle side use). One file per class, so compiling one class again writes one file and nothing is merged or parsed in the compiler. Descriptor format 1 is one JSON object: `format` (1), `class`, `name`, optionally `description`, and `target` (`application`). Only what the source says as a constant is written: the name is the one the class name gives unless the class or a class it extends declares `name` or `getName()`, and then it is read when that is a property with a string constant or a method that only returns one; a name declared in another way gets no descriptor (and an earlier one is deleted); a description declared in another way is left out; without a declaration the description is the name, as `ApplicationCommand` has it. The method the trait weaves into a class is told from a declaration by its `TraitBridge` annotation, so the name from another trait or from a class compiled before is "not known". This was chosen over a build task that scans class files: it is in the compiler that already writes the registration, so it works for a companion (`src/cli/groovy`), for `grails-app/commands` of an application and for a build that is not Gradle, it reads the source and not a bytecode pattern of a constructor, and it needs no ASM in the Gradle plugin. A command the transformation does not see (Java, or registered by hand, as `ConfigReportCommand` is in `grails-core-cli` itself) has a descriptor when the file is put into the resources by hand; `grails-core-cli` has that one file. The writer applies the rules of the reader (`CommandDescriptor.describe` and `CommandDescriptor.read`, in one class): a command whose name is not the name of a command (the class `Command`, whose name is empty; a declared `Say Hello`) gets no descriptor, an anonymous class gets none, and a description that is not one line that can be shown (two lines, a tab, 600 characters) is left out of the descriptor; a property that is not `final` is not read when a constructor or an initializer block of its class assigns to it. Covered by `CommandDescriptorWriterSpec` through the compiler.

*Catalog file.* `GrailsCliGradlePlugin` registers the task `grailsCommandCatalog` (`GrailsCommandCatalogTask`) in every project it is applied to that has the `java` plugin, so in applications and plugins. It writes `build/grails/command-catalog.json` (`GrailsCommandCatalogTask.CATALOG_LOCATION` below the build directory): one line of ASCII JSON, format 1 of `help --json` with the members the build side knows:

```
{ "format": 1,
  "complete": true,                    false exactly when diagnostics is not empty
  "diagnostics": ["one line each"],    printable ASCII, sorted, at most 64 and a line that says how many more
  "commands": [                        sorted by name, names unique
    { "name": "hello-world",
      "aliases": [],                   an application command has one name
      "description": "one line",       "" when not known
      "origin": "org.example:hello-plugin-cli:1.2.3",   or "application"
      "target": "application",
      "registration": "descriptor"     or "factories" or "legacy"
    } ] }
```

There is no `projectDirectory`, `project`, `options` or `parameters` (absent: not known; `ApplicationCommand` declares none). `registration` is new: `descriptor` for a command with a descriptor; `factories` for a command that is registered without one (a companion built before 9.0, Java, a name that is worked out), whose name is the one its class name gives and whose description is empty; `legacy` for a `grails.dev.commands.ApplicationCommand` of a Grails 7 plugin, read from `META-INF/grails.factories` and listed the same way. Legacy commands are listed only when the bridge is on the class path: an element whose `grails-cli.factories` registers `LegacyApplicationCommandProvider` and which has the entry of that class (the entry is looked for, not read), which is what `legacyCommandSupport = true` brings. That any element with the bridge turns the listing on is intended: the bridge is what runs these commands, wherever it comes from; an element that only names it does not; without it they are a diagnostic per origin. `origin` is `group:artifact:version` of the resolved module, for a project of the build the first capability of the resolved variant (so the companion of a sibling project is `group:name-cli:version`), `file:<name>` for a file dependency, and `application` for the output of the project. What is read is the class path `runCommand` runs with, in its order: the classes and resources directories of the main source set (so the compiled `grails-app/commands`; not what else a build adds to the output of the source set, such as compiled views), `runtimeClasspath`, `grailsCliClasspath`; from each jar or directory only the two factories files and, for a registered class, its descriptor. No class is loaded and nothing is executed. A class can be registered by more than one element: the compiler copies a registration written by hand (`src/main/resources/META-INF/grails-cli.factories`) into the classes directory and leaves the descriptor written beside it in the resources, and a jar can register a class another jar describes. The descriptor of a class is therefore looked for in every element that registers the class, the classes and resources directories of the project being one element; the first element in class path order that has a descriptor supplies it and is the `origin`; a class without a descriptor anywhere has the origin of the first element that registers it. Two descriptors of one class whose bytes differ are not chosen between: the command is left out, with a diagnostic that names the origins. An element is a directory or any regular file, which is opened as an archive whatever its name is, as a JVM does (`lib.JAR`, `commands.zip`); one that cannot be opened is a diagnostic when its name ends in `.jar` or `.zip` in any case, and otherwise holds no commands for a JVM either. Everything from a dependency is bounded and checked: a factories file is at most 256 KiB, an element registers at most 256 commands, the catalog has at most 2048, a registered class name is ASCII Java syntax of at most 255 characters, a descriptor is at most 16 KiB of UTF-8 JSON, read by a strict reader of the grammar (`org.apache.grails.gradle.model.cli.StrictJson`: one value and nothing after it, the escapes of RFC 8259 only, four hexadecimal digits after `\u`, a surrogate only as half of a pair, no control character in a string, no name twice in an object; whatever is wrong is a refusal and never another exception), nested at most 4 deep, with `format` 1 and `class` equal to the registered class, a name is `[a-z][a-z0-9]*(-[a-z0-9]+)*` of at most 64 characters, a description is at most 512 characters without control, format, line-separator, private-use or unassigned characters, a `target` other than `application` is refused (named when it is one of the five, never echoed otherwise). What fails is left out and named in `diagnostics` by class and origin, without repeating the offending text. A name that two classes have is listed for none of them, with one diagnostic. A diagnostic is at most 600 characters and names at most three classes or origins and counts the rest; an origin is shown with at most 120 characters, printable ASCII without `@` and `$`. The descriptions are those of the descriptors, checked to be one line without control or format characters of at most 512 characters and not otherwise: `@|...|@`, `${...}` and anything else a command line gives a meaning to are listed as they are. The catalog is data, and whoever shows a name, a description, an origin or a diagnostic treats it as text of a stranger. Collisions with the names the CLI has itself are not found here: the build does not know them (slice 5b). The task is cacheable and up to date while its inputs are: it declares only the registration and descriptor files of the project output (a change to another class does not run it), the two class paths, and the origins. Covered by `CommandCatalogSpec` (TestKit, artifacts written into a file repository by the specification, no class in any jar).

*Argument vector.* `ApplicationContextCommandTask`, the type of `runCommand` and of the task of each command, has two options. `--arg=<value>`, repeated, gives one argument per occurrence as it is. `--args-file=<path>` names a UTF-8 file with a JSON array of strings (relative paths in the project directory; at most 1 MiB and 4096 arguments; read with the strict reader; refused: anything that is not such an array, half a surrogate pair among it, and an argument with U+0000; the content is never repeated in a message; the task does not delete the file). What the file holds does not become arguments of the JVM of the command: the task checks the file and starts that JVM with `[command,] <application class> --grails-args-file=<absolute path>`, and `GrailsApplicationContextCommandRunner.main` reads the file first (`CommandArgumentsFile.expand`, the same code the task checks it with) and puts its arguments in front of the application class. So the arguments are in no process listing and in no line Gradle logs with `--info`, and the size of the file is not held to what a command line carries. A runner that does not know the argument (a `grails-console` before 9.0 with this Gradle plugin) takes it for the application class and ends with `Application class --grails-args-file=... not found`: it does not run the command without its arguments. `-Pargs="..."` is unchanged, split by `CommandLineParser.translateCommandline` as before. More than one of the three in a build is refused. Line breaks are carried. What Gradle itself does was measured on 9.9.1: it refuses an empty option value (`--arg=`), so an empty argument needs the file; and the values of task options are part of the key of a configuration cache entry, as project properties read during configuration are, so only the file form reuses the entry when the arguments change. For `--arg` and `-Pargs` the runner is as it was: `main` takes the vector, and `ExecutionContext.commandLine.rawArguments` is that vector with the name of the command first, while `remainingArgs` and the options are what `CommandLineParser.parse` reads from it (trimmed, empty arguments dropped, surrounding double quotes removed, a dash starts an option that takes the next argument). Covered by `CommandArgumentsSpec` (TestKit, a stand-in for the runner that prints the bytes of what it was started with) and a feature of `CommandLineParserSpec`.

*What slice 5b can rely on.* (1) The file is `<project directory>/build/grails/command-catalog.json`; it exists only after `grailsCommandCatalog` ran, and nothing runs that task implicitly. (2) It is ASCII, one line, the document above; unknown members are to be ignored. (3) Every command in it is run as `gradle runCommand --arg=<name> --arg=<argument>...` or `gradle runCommand --args-file=<file>` with `[name, argument...]` in the file, in the project directory; `runCommand` exists in every project that has the catalog task. The file form is the one for a program: nothing of the arguments is on a command line (Windows batch quoting, length, process listings) and the configuration cache entry is reused. (4) `runCommand` ends as Gradle does: 0 when the command returned `true`; a failed build (the wrapper exits with 1) when the command returned `false` or threw, when the application context could not start, when there is no command by that name (the runner prints `Command not found for name: X`), when the project has no application class, when the arguments are refused, or when the build itself fails. Gradle does not pass the exit value of the command on. This is what the runner and `JavaExec` do by their code; the specs of this slice run a stand-in for the runner and do not exercise it. (5) The task fails, and leaves the earlier file as it was, when the class paths cannot be resolved or the project does not compile. (6) Every text of the file (`name`, `description`, `origin`, each line of `diagnostics`) is data of a dependency: names are held to the pattern of a command name, the others to one line of bounded length without control characters and to nothing else, so the CLI shows them through its own sanitising and never as picocli markup or as a template.

Changed by the review of the slice, without a change to what 5b relies on (the file, its members, the two ways to invoke and the exit behaviour are as stated): the lookup of descriptors, the strict reader in place of `JsonSlurper` for the descriptor and the file of arguments (which made a task fail on a descriptor that ends inside an escape, and read `["a"]["b"]` as `["a"]`), the file of arguments reaching the runner as a path, the bounds of diagnostics, the writer that writes nothing its reader refuses, archives by content and not by name, the bridge by its class. `CommandCatalogBuilder` moved to `grails-gradle-model` beside `CommandDescriptor`, so that a spec of `grails-core` puts a catalog together from what the real compiler leaves. The reader was compared with Python's `json` on 500,138 texts made by changing JSON at random (the 200,045 of the review among them), for both shapes, without a disagreement; `strict-json-corpus.tsv` in the tests of `grails-gradle-model` is 1,293 such texts with Python's verdicts, and `StrictJsonSpec` holds the reader to them and runs 20,000 seeded changes of its own. A project that has a task named `grailsCommandCatalog` before the plugin is applied keeps it and is warned that no catalog is written.

Not done: the catalog has no `options`, `parameters` or canonical command ID, and a descriptor has no members for them. The descriptors of classes deleted without a clean build stay in the output directory under incremental Groovy compilation (`PruneGeneratedFactories` prunes the registration only); they are ignored, because a descriptor is read only for a registered class, but they are in the jar. The file says nothing that lets a reader tell that it is stale: 5b has to decide that (for example from the modification times of the build files and of `grails-app/commands`). Nothing runs `grailsCommandCatalog` after a compilation; a broken application build means no new catalog. Application commands still get no task of their own, and legacy commands none either: both are reached through `runCommand`, which is the route section 2.5 item 3 asks for. `runScript` did not get the two options. The descriptor's `target` is always `application`: there is no API yet by which a command declares another. `ApplicationContextCommandFactory` and `GradleTaskCommandAdapter` in the shell are unchanged and still send `-Pargs`. Not run on Windows: the hop from the Gradle task to the JVM of the command is `JavaExec`, whose quoting of an argument with a double quote on Windows was not verified. The per-command tasks are still registered by reading the jars of `grailsCliClasspath` during configuration, which makes those jars configuration inputs.

**CLI side of plugin and application commands, as implemented (step 4, slice 5b).** Implemented, in `grails-forge-cli` only (`org.apache.grails.forge.cli.console`: `ApplicationCommandFile`, `ApplicationCommands`, `ApplicationCommandCommand`, `BoundedJson`). When a command line is put together for a command that has to do with the project (`ForgeCli.createCommandLine`, after every command of the CLI itself is there), the file `build/grails/command-catalog.json` of the project `ConsoleProject` finds and trusts is read, and each command in it is added as a picocli command under its name and its other names. No Gradle is run for it, by any command. *Reading.* The file is untrusted. `build`, `build/grails` and the file are looked at without following links: a link at any of the three is refused, what is not a regular file (a pipe, a directory) is refused without being opened, the real path has to be the one below the real path of the project, the size is at most 4 MiB before and while reading, and every byte is ASCII. The JSON reader is `BoundedJson`, written for this: the reader inside `PackCatalog` is built around the plugin catalog (its patterns of members and its callback per plugin) and could not be shared without moving that along; the discipline is the same (strict grammar, depth at most 8, at most 100000 values, texts at most 4096 characters, numbers at most 32) and an object with a key twice is refused. Then every member is held to what the task of slice 5a writes: `format` 1, `complete` a boolean, `diagnostics` at most 65 lines of at most 2048 characters of printable ASCII, at most 2048 commands, a name `[a-z][a-z0-9]*(-[a-z0-9]+)*` of at most 64 characters, `aliases` absent or at most 16 such names, `description` absent or one line of at most 512 characters by the rule of `CommandDescriptor.isDescription`, `origin` `application`, `file:<name>` or `group:artifact:version` with parts `[A-Za-z0-9_][A-Za-z0-9_.+-]*` and at most 200 characters, `target` `application`, `registration` one of the three, no name or other name twice. Members that are not known are ignored. Anything else makes the whole file unused: the project has no application commands and one line says what is wrong, with the number of the command and nothing of the content. Reading never throws. *Listing.* The root help has a section `Commands of the application and its plugins:` after the commands of the CLI, each with its description and its origin in parentheses, then what there is to say (below), broken between words only. `help NAME` shows the usage (`NAME [ARGS...]`, the description, the origin, that it runs in the application through `runCommand`). The description is shown as text: `@|` is written `@ |`, `%` is not a format, and variables are not interpolated for these commands. `help --json` appends the commands to `commands` with `name`, `aliases`, `description`, `project: true`, `origin`, `target: "application"`, `registration` and `commands: []`, without `options` and `parameters` (not known). Short forms and their ambiguity rule are those of every command, since the commands are in the picocli model; so is completion in the interactive shell. *Absence and staleness.* No file: `complete: false` and one diagnostic that names `gradle grailsCommandCatalog`; the root help says the same in the section; a name typed for a command that is none is refused as before with that line added to the message (also for `help NAME`). A file that is not used: the same with the reason. Stale is defined cheaply: the file is older than one of `build.gradle`, `build.gradle.kts`, `settings.gradle`, `settings.gradle.kts`, `gradle.properties`, the directory `grails-app/commands` or the directory `build/classes/groovy/main/META-INF/grails-commands` (the modification time of each, nothing is walked). A stale file is used, with `complete: false` and a diagnostic that names what changed and `gradle grailsCommandCatalog --rerun`, because the task is up to date when its inputs did not change and then does not write the file again. `complete: false` in the file gives `complete: false`, and the lines of the file are diagnostics prefixed with the name of the task. With a file that is current nothing is added to the refusal of an unknown command. Outside a project, or below a project that is not trusted, nothing is read and nothing is said. *Collisions.* The commands the command line has when the file is read are the built-ins. A command of the file is not added when its name or one of its other names is a command of the CLI, another name of one (`war`), or resolves as a short form among the commands of the CLI, to one (`stat` for `stats`) or to several (`c`); the check is made against the built-ins only, so commands of the file do not shadow each other this way. The built-in wins. Each such command is one diagnostic of `help --json` (which stays `complete: true`) and one line of the root help, naming the name, what has it with the origin `built-in`, the command and its origin, and how it is still run; and when that name is typed, the built-in runs after one `| Warning` line on standard error (not for `help --json`). The way named is `gradle runCommand --arg=NAME`, which works today, or `run-command NAME` when the command line has a command of that name: `run-command` is slice 4's and is not added here. Known case: `install-templates` of the scaffolding plugin. *Running.* `ApplicationCommandCommand` has no options: a preprocessor gives it everything after the name, as `gradle` has it. It writes the vector `[name, argument...]` as a JSON array into `arguments.json` in a new directory under the temporary directory of the system (directory `rwx------`, file `rw-------` where the file system has such permissions), runs `GradleRunner` with `runCommand --args-file=<absolute path>`, and deletes file and directory when Gradle has ended, failed or been cancelled, and from a shutdown hook when the JVM is ended. Exit and output are those of the Gradle built-ins. What the task would refuse is refused before Gradle is started, with exit code 2 and without repeating the argument: U+0000, half a surrogate pair, more than 4096 elements, more than 1 MiB. Covered by `ApplicationCommandsSpec` through `ForgeCli.execute`, with files written by hand; where a command runs, the fixture of `ConsoleCommandsSpec` with a `runCommand` task that prints the bytes of each argument, the permissions and the path of the file. No Grails application is built. `CommandCatalogSpec` and `ConsoleCommandsSpec` were changed where a project without the file is now `complete: false` and where the list of commands is followed by the new section.

Not done: no canonical command ID or qualified form (`origin:name`): a command that is not there for a command of the CLI is reached through `run-command`. `complete` stays `true` for a collision; that is a choice, the other reading is defensible. Staleness is modification times: of five build files, and of the directories and command files below `grails-app/commands` and `build/classes/groovy/main/META-INF/grails-commands`. A plugin version changed in a version catalog, a parent build or `buildSrc` is not noticed, a description that changed is noticed only once the class was compiled again, and an edit of a build file that changes no command reports stale until `--rerun`. Between looking at the file and opening it, it can be replaced (by a pipe, on which opening blocks): only someone who can write to `build` of the project can do that. The owner of `build` and of the file is not checked beyond the rule for the project. A hard link is read like a file. An application command named like a command that does not read the project (`gradle`, `create-app`) is reported by `help` only, because the file is not read for those. The warning for a typed name is printed for `help` too when a command of the build is called `help`. The stand-in task of the spec takes `--args-file`; the real `ApplicationContextCommandTask` was run in the end-to-end run of slice 9, by `run-command` with a file of `.grails/run` and by the name of a command with the temporary file that command had then; nothing was run on Windows. picocli expands `@file` before the command sees its arguments, as everywhere. Leaves for step 5: `ApplicationContextCommandFactory`, `GradleTaskCommandAdapter`.

Implemented, after a review of slices 4 to 9 and the end-to-end run of slice 9 (2026-11-20); where this paragraph and the ones above differ, this one holds. *What the catalog reader accepts.* The reader and the writer disagreed on where a command comes from: `CommandCatalogBuilder.shown` writes the coordinates of an artifact or `file:` and the name of a file with every character outside printable ASCII, every space, `@` and `$` replaced by `?`, cut to 120 characters, and the reader wanted `group:artifact:version` in a narrower alphabet, so a jar named `lib (1).jar`, a hidden file, a root project with a space in its name or an empty group took every application command away. `ApplicationCommandFile.isOrigin` now is that rule and nothing more (1 to 120 characters, each printable ASCII and none a space, `@` or `$`): an origin is only shown. The two definitions are in two builds, and `ApplicationCommandsSpec` holds them together by reading what the real `CommandCatalogBuilder` writes for 26 origins, hostile ones among them (`grails-gradle-model` is a test dependency of `grails-forge-cli` for this, without what it brings). A command of the file that is not as the task writes one (name, other names, description, origin, target, registration, a name a command before it has) is left out alone and the others are used: `help` and `help --json` name the first three by their place in the file and count the rest, and `complete` is `false`. What is wrong with the file itself (a link, not a file, too large, not JSON within the bounds, not `format` 1, `complete`, `diagnostics` or `commands` of another type) still leaves the whole file unused. `format` is the number written `1`: `1.0` and `1e0` are refused, because `BoundedJson` gives a number written with digits alone as a `Long` and any other as a `BigDecimal` (`RunningApplication` reads its record by the same rule). The file is at most 256 KiB and its first 512 commands are used, with a diagnostic for the rest; a catalog of the earlier limits (4 MiB, 2,048 commands) cost about two seconds on every command line. *Staleness.* The directories `grails-app/commands` and `build/classes/groovy/main/META-INF/grails-commands` are walked (no link followed, at most 4,096 entries and 24 levels, more is taken for changed): a directory at any depth, a `.groovy` file below the first and a `.json` file below the second that is newer than the catalog makes it stale, so a command that `create-command` writes into the directory of its package is noticed, as one that was deleted or written again. After `create-command` succeeds one line says to list the commands again. *Short forms.* A short form is looked for among the commands of the CLI first and is one of an application or script command only when it fits none of those (`CommandCatalog.shortFor`): a catalog with `hlep`, `complie` and `create-application` no longer makes `h`, `comp` and `cA` stand for two commands. *What runs.* Before Gradle is started, `ApplicationCommandCommand` writes one line to standard error, `| Running the command NAME of ORIGIN, with the Gradle task runCommand` (for a script `the script NAME of src/main/scripts, with the Gradle task runScriptCommand`), also with `--plain-output`: a name mistyped into the name of a command of a plugin is seen before it runs. *Arguments.* There is one writer of the file of arguments, slice 4's `CommandArgumentsFile` (`.grails/run/command-args-N.json`, owner-only, the first free of sixteen names, a `.pid` file beside it, swept when its writer is gone); `run-command`, the application commands and the scripts use it, and the private temporary directory of slice 5b is gone. The option value is therefore the same from run to run, and a build with the configuration cache reuses its entry when the arguments change: measured with the stand-in build of the spec (`Reusing configuration cache` on a second run with other arguments) and, for the real task, by `CommandArgumentsSpec` in `grails-gradle`. The refusals of arguments are those of `CommandArgumentsFile` for all three. `.grails/run/.gitignore` is created owner-only like the other files there. *Help and errors.* The list of the application commands in the help is laid out by the CLI and broken between words only, so coordinates stay whole. A mistake in the use of a command is answered by `UsageHelp.answer`: what is wrong, one line `Usage: ...` (the synopsis when it is one line, otherwise `NAME [OPTIONS] ...`, or the first line of a synopsis a command wrote itself) and `Run 'CLI help NAME' for the options` (`for the commands` at the root), with exit code 2; the whole help, 67 lines after an unknown command, is shown by `help` and `--help` only, and the list of application commands is in none of this. A command that is given an option it does not have and not what it needs says `NAME has no option --x`, where picocli said that the parameter is missing. *One wording outside a project.* Every command that works on a project refuses with exit code 1 and `NAME is a command of a Grails project; there is none in DIR or above it` (with `in the same repository`, or the 32 directories that are looked at, where the search ended there); below a project that is not used it is the reason, then `NAME is a command of a Grails project, and was not run`. The commands that create classes and `install-templates` are registered where the record of no project was read as well, hidden, through `ProjectOnlyCommand`, so they are no longer `There is no command` with exit code 2; `help` outside a project says in its last line that a project has more commands; `package-plugin` and `publish-plugin` are listed outside a project and left out in a project that is no plugin. `feature add` uses the same line outside a project and keeps its own for a project without the state of this generator. The lines of `FormerCommands` for these names are now reached in a project of another kind only (a REST command in a web application, `create-job` without Quartz, the plugin commands in an application). *Options alike.* `-D` is an option of every command that is a build (`GradleTaskCommand`), `compile`, `clean`, `package-plugin` and `publish-plugin` included, and is shown as it is typed, `-DNAME=VALUE`, in the usage line and among the options (`UsageHelp`, a help factory that takes picocli's `=` out for this option). `-v` and `-x` mean the same in front of a command and after its name (`BaseCommand.verbose` and `showStacktrace` read the command line as well). `--verbose` is `--info` for the Gradle built-ins, not `--debug`, which is asked for with `-- --debug`; for `run-app`, `console` and `shell` it leaves `--quiet` out, as before. Messages name the command line by the name it was started under (`add-property` said `grails`). Descriptions: `Creates a Grails plugin`, `Creates a Grails plugin with a web layer`, `Creates a REST API application`, `Creates an interceptor`, and `create-webapp` says that it is another name of `create-app`; a description of one line has no full stop. `help gradle` says that `gradle --help` is the help of Gradle. *Build side.* In `CommandCatalogBuilder.resolve` a class the application registers is the application's: its own descriptor, or the name its class name gives when it has none, holds, and a descriptor for that class in a jar that comes later on the class path is named in a diagnostic and not used; before, such a jar renamed the command of the application without a word. Two dependencies that describe one class differently still leave it out. Covered by `CommandCatalogBuilderSpec` in `grails-gradle-model`. *`test-app` with a filter.* `test-app BookControllerSpec` gives the filter to `test` and to `integrationTest`, and Gradle fails the task that has no match ("No tests found for given includes"). The `Test` task of Gradle 9.9.1 takes `--tests`, `--debug-jvm` and `--test-dry-run` on the command line and nothing for `failOnNoMatchingTests`, which is a property of the filter in the build script (read from `AbstractTestTask` and `Test` in the distribution of the wrapper); no flag was invented, and no init script is written for it. The behaviour is kept, and when the build fails after tests were named for both tasks, one line `| Note ...` says that this is what Gradle does and to use `--unit` or `--integration`; which task failed is in what Gradle wrote and is not read from there.

Not done, after that review: a filtered `test-app` over both tasks still ends with 1 when one of them has no match. `--plain-output` does not suppress the line that says what is run. The synopsis line of a mistake is not the usage of a parent command with its commands. One-line descriptions of options still end with or without a full stop as they did. The first 512 commands of a catalog are used although the task writes up to 2,048: a project with more has the rest as a diagnostic. A descriptor in a later jar for a class of another *jar* that has none still names that class (only the application comes first). The hidden stand-ins have no short forms and are not in `help --json` outside a project. Known and outside the CLI: the Gradle task `shell` of a generated application fails when the context starts, with `groovy.lang.GroovyRuntimeException: exception during bean introspection`, caused by `java.lang.TypeNotPresentException: Type org.jline.shell.CommandGroup not present` (`java.lang.ClassNotFoundException: org.jline.shell.CommandGroup`), and ends with 0; the CLI then says nothing, neither success nor failure, and ends with 0 as Gradle did.

Implemented, after a second review of the command line; where this paragraph and the ones above differ, this one holds. *One set of limits for the catalog.* The writer and the reader had different numbers (the task wrote up to 2,048 commands in a file of any size, the CLI read 512 commands and 256 KiB), so the task could write a file the CLI refused whole. `CommandCatalogBuilder` now writes at most `MAX_COMMANDS` = 512 commands, the first by name whatever the order of the class path is, in at most `MAX_BYTES` = 512 KiB (`toJson` lists fewer commands until the document is within them, which descriptions written as escapes need), and says in one diagnostic how many it left out; it still looks at up to `MAX_CLASSES` = 2,048 registered classes. `ApplicationCommandFile` reads 512 KiB and 512 commands. The numbers are in two builds; `ApplicationCommandsSpec` holds them equal (bytes, commands, origin, description, name, diagnostics) and reads through `help --json` the largest file the real writer writes. *Where a command comes from.* An origin is shown as text in the help of a command as in the list: `help NAME` put it into the description picocli formats, where `%n` in the name of a jar was a line break and `%s` an error; it is escaped there as the description is. A file cannot give `built-in`, or `application` in other letters, as an origin: those are the words of the CLI. *What is wrong with a command line.* `UsageHelp.answer` says a mistake in one line of at most 600 characters without control characters, format characters or `@|`, whatever was typed: the messages of picocli repeat an argument with its line breaks and escape sequences. An option a command does not have and an argument it does not take are worded by the CLI (`compile has no option --x`, `stats takes no argument now`, `feature add has no option --bogus`), with the command named by its path. The usage line of a command with a usage of several lines is those lines as one. The refusal of an unknown command (`UsageHelp.Lines`) keeps its several lines, each made by the CLI. *The directory of a run.* Two commands that start at once in a project without `.grails/run` both run: a directory or a `.gitignore` that another command made in the meantime is not a failure (`RunDirectory.existing`, `createNew`). A name for a file of arguments is held only by a process that runs, as the same user, and began at the instant the `.pid` file names: a file that names no instant, or the process of another user, as the files of a copied or cloned project do, holds none, so sixteen such files no longer refuse every command. *What runs, and with what.* `run-command NAME` says the line `| Running the command NAME of ORIGIN, with the Gradle task runCommand` that the name of a command says, and for a name the build has not listed that it has not. A command run by its name gives Gradle the arguments `run-command` gives it for the same command (`runCommand`, `--args-file=…`, `-Dgrails.env=development`), so the two are one entry of the configuration cache. `-x` (`--stacktrace`) in front of a command of the application or of a script is `--stacktrace -Dgrails.full.stacktrace=true` for the build, as for the commands that are a build; after the name it is the command's own argument. Typing what is short for a command of the CLI that a command of the build is also named says, as the full name does, that the command of the build is not there. Covered by `ApplicationCommandsSpec`, `ScriptCommandsSpec`, `CommandCatalogSpec`, `ConsoleCommandsSpec` and `LifecycleCommandsSpec` through `ForgeCli.execute`, and by `CommandCatalogBuilderSpec` in `grails-gradle-model`.

Not done, after the second review: a project with more than 512 commands has the rest as a diagnostic only, in the task and in the CLI. `--plain-output` still does not suppress the line that says what is run. What tells the task that runs a script to print the full stack trace of the script is the system property alone; the script host reads nothing else. Nothing of this was run on Windows.

**Artefact commands and project templates, as implemented (step 4, slice 6).** Implemented: the Forge CLI has `create-unit-test`, `create-integration-test`, `create-functional-test`, `create-restful-controller`, `create-domain-resource`, `create-command`, `create-script` and `install-templates`, registered in the list of code generation commands of `ForgeCli` like the six before them, so they are there where the current directory has a record of a project. As with the profiles, `create-unit-test`, `create-command`, `create-script` and `install-templates` are commands of every project, `create-integration-test` of every type but `plugin`, and `create-functional-test`, `create-restful-controller` and `create-domain-resource` of a REST API application. Each takes one name, as the shell's did, and writes to the paths the profile commands wrote to: `src/test/groovy/<package>/<Name>Spec.groovy`; `src/integration-test/groovy/<package>/<Name>Spec.groovy` (both test commands); `grails-app/controllers/<package>/<Name>Controller.groovy`; `grails-app/domain/<package>/<Name>.groovy` with `src/test/groovy/<package>/<Name>Spec.groovy`; `grails-app/commands/<package>/<Name>Command.groovy`; `src/main/scripts/<lower-case-with-hyphens>.groovy`. The name is held to the name of a new class by `org.apache.grails.forge.cli.artefact.ArtefactName` before any path is built: no `/`, `\` or `:`, no character that is not shown, at most 200 characters, its letters in the composed form of Unicode (NFC), the class an identifier and the package a name of Java (`SourceVersion`, so keywords and empty parts are refused, `..` with them), no last part `groovy` or `java` in lower case after a dot (`Book.groovy` is the name of a file; `shop.Groovy` is a class), a first letter that has a capital of one letter (not `ß`), a hyphen only between two words, no part of the package and no name of a file as it is written that is a device name of Windows (`create-domain-class con` is refused for `Con.groovy`, `create-controller con` writes `ConController.groovy`) or that `PackDescriptorParser.isPathBelowRoot` does not take; the name of a script is `[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9]*(-[A-Za-z0-9]+)*`, for which the conversion to the name of the file (`NameUtils.getScriptName`) is defined. A refusal is a `ParameterException` (exit code 2, one line and the usage) that echoes the name through `FeatureSelectionException.shown`. All thirteen commands hold a name to this; the six earlier ones only do not take a name without what they add to it (`create-controller BookController` is `BookControllerController`, as before).

Writing, for all thirteen commands and `install-templates` (`CodeGenCommand.write`, `ProjectFiles`, `ProjectOutputHandler`): a command writes all of its files or none. Before the first file is written every target is checked: each step of the path is at most 255 bytes in UTF-8; every directory from the project down to the file that is there is a directory, not a link (wherever it leads, to another directory of the project as well: nothing needs a linked source directory, and a rule without exceptions is the one that can be checked), not a file, and is listed by its parent under the name that is asked for, compared in the composed form of Unicode, which is what tells `EXAMPLE` from `example` on a file system that takes one for the other and takes a name the file system lists in a form of its own for the name it is; the directory the first new entry goes into can be written to; and where the file goes there is no directory, empty or not. A file or a link in the place of the file is "already there": without `-f`/`--force` the command names each such file and ends with 1, with it the file is replaced and a link is removed, not written through. Then every text is rendered, and only then the files are written, each through a `ProjectPlace`: the directories from the project down to the file are opened one from the other and none through a link, what is there under the name of the file is moved aside under the name `.<name>.grails-aside-<hex>` in its directory (a place knows whether there was something and whether it was moved, and a rollback removes only what this run wrote where nothing was: a file that could not be moved aside is not touched), and the file is written under a third name (`CREATE_NEW`, not through a link) that takes its place in one step. When a write fails after all, what was written is removed, what was moved aside has its name again, so it is what it was whatever its size, its mode and its other names, and the directories the command made are removed; the message then ends with "The project is as it was before the command", or says for each file that could not be taken back what it is now and under which name what it was is. What was true of a path when it was checked is enforced when it is written: on Linux the Java runtime opens a directory relative to the one above it and works by name in the open directory (`SecureDirectoryStream`), so a directory that is made a link in between is not followed; on macOS and Windows the runtime has no such way, and there every directory on the way is compared, right before each step, with the one that was opened (not a link, and the same file key), which leaves the instant of the step itself. A directory that is missing is made by its path on every platform, so a swap at that instant can leave an empty directory where the link leads. A command that is killed can leave a file aside, which is then all there is of what the file was: every later write of that file is refused in the check, with both names, until the user has given the file its name back or removed it. It is not restored automatically, also when the file itself is absent, because the command cannot tell a file that was moved aside and never replaced from one whose replacement the user has deleted since, and one rule for both cases cannot lose content. What such a command left of the file it was writing (`.<name>.grails-new-<hex>`) is removed by the next write of that file. Every refusal and failure is one line, `Cannot write <path in the project>: <what is wrong>`, without a path of the computer. This rule is the one of these commands only. `ProjectOutputHandler` is a subclass in the CLI of `FileSystemOutputHandler`, which is unchanged: the commands that create an application (`create-app` and its siblings, in a new directory or in place) and `feature add` keep its check, which is that the path is below the directory as text, and follow the layout the directory has. `add-property` writes no new file and changes the file of a domain class through `DomainFieldModifier`, as before.

Templates: all thirteen commands take them from one place, `ArtefactTemplates`: the file `src/main/templates/<location>` of the project when there is one under exactly those names, and otherwise the template of the CLI, a resource under `META-INF/grails-forge-cli/templates/<location>` (the eleven GSP views under `grails-app/views/forge/cli/command/templates` are replaced by these resources, and the GSP plugin is no longer applied to `grails-forge-cli`). No template is run. The locations are those of the shell, found in `RenderStep`, `TemplateRendererImpl.template` and the profiles: `artifacts/<Kind>.groovy` and `testing/<Kind>.groovy` with the tokens `@artifact.package@`, `@artifact.package.name@`, `@artifact.package.path@`, `@artifact.name@` and `@artifact.propertyName@` (`SimpleTemplate` in the shell, plain replacement), `artifacts/Script.groovy` among them, and `Job.groovy` of the Quartz plugin's `create-job`, in which `${className}` and the other names of `grails.codegen.model.Model` in braces are replaced, `\$` is a dollar sign and `\\` a backslash. A template of a profile and the template of the Quartz plugin therefore work under `src/main/templates` unchanged. In `Job.groovy` everything the template engine of Groovy would have evaluated is refused with its line and the words "Templates are not run": `<% %>`, `<%= %>`, an expression in braces that is not one name, `$name` without braces, any other backslash. A token `@artifact.…@` that is not one of the five fails the command with the line. A template of a project is read as UTF-8, at most 256 KiB, without NUL, with one byte order mark at its start left out, and only when its real path is inside the real path of the project (a link to a file of the project is read). A template that cannot be read or rendered ends the command with 1 and one line with the path of the template in the project and the line; nothing is written, and there is no fallback to the template of the CLI. The command says `Using the template <path> of the project` when it uses one. `install-templates` copies the templates of the commands registered beside it (so of the commands this project has) into `src/main/templates`, lists each, keeps one that is there unless forced and ends with 0. Covered by `ArtefactCommandsSpec` through `ForgeCli.execute`, in a directory that has only the record of a generated project: what each command writes is compared byte for byte with `src/test/resources/golden/artefacts`, of which `existing` is what the six earlier commands wrote from their GSP views, captured before the change; every golden source is parsed as Groovy and imports only classes of a fixed list, each checked to be in the framework; with the templates `install-templates` copies, every command of a web and of a REST API project writes byte for byte what it writes without them; and the templates are read through the class loader, one resource each.

Differences from the shell. The `render` step of the YAML commands never looked in the project (only scripts did, through `template()`), so for those commands the override is new. The shell ran `artifacts/Script.groovy` and `Job.groovy` through `GStringTemplateEngine`; here they are text (decided 2026-11-20: no code of a project is run by a code generation command). Without a package the shell left `@artifact.package@` in the output, where this leaves the token and the end of its line out. `book-store` is `BookStore` here and `Book-store` in the shell. `create-unit-test BookSpec` is `BookSpec`, because `Spec` is taken as the convention of the test commands, where the shell wrote `BookSpecSpec`. `create-script` on a file that is there warns and succeeds in the shell and ends with 1 here. What is generated differs from the profile templates where those are out of date for 9.0: explicit imports instead of `grails.rest.*`, `grails.converters.*` and `org.apache.grails.core.cli.*`; the functional test uses `java.net.http.HttpClient` and asserts the status of `/`, because a generated REST API application has neither the Micronaut HTTP client of the profile template (a feature that needs JDK 25) nor `grails-testing-support-http-client`.

Behaviour changes to the six earlier commands of the Forge CLI (`create-controller`, `create-service`, `create-domain-class`, `create-taglib`, `create-interceptor`, `create-job`): a file that is there without `-f` now ends the command with 1 and nothing written, where the file was kept with a warning, the other file written and the exit code 0; a name is held to the name of a class as for the newer commands (exit code 2, the name echoed without what a terminal acts on), where any text was made into directories and a file: refused now are a path (`a/b`, `a\\b`, `C:x`), a control character or an escape sequence, a name that is no identifier (`my class`, `1Book`, `Book!`), a package that is no name of Java (`my-pkg.Book`, a keyword as a part), a hyphen at the start or the end or two in a row (`Book-`, `book--store`), the name of a file (`a.B.groovy`, in lower case only), a device name of Windows where it names a directory or a file (`aux.Book` for all six, `con` for `create-domain-class` only), more than 200 characters, and letters that are not composed; `book`, `fooBar`, `book-store` and `org.shop.Book` give what they gave, `a.b.book-store` is `BookStore` in the package `a.b` where the package came out as `A.b`, and `micronaut`, which was refused as the name of an application, is a name like any other; every file is rendered before one is written; they write under the rule above, so not through a linked directory; and the test `create-interceptor` writes asks for the controller by the property name (`fooBar`), where it lower-cased the class name (`foobar`), which an interceptor of two words does not match. The files they generate are otherwise byte for byte what they were.

Not done: templates with logic are not supported; the shell executed `Job.groovy` and `artifacts/Script.groovy`, and a project template that relied on that has to be rewritten. An override is not fingerprinted: nothing records which template of the CLI a project template was copied from, so a template that has become stale is not noticed. A file with a second name (a hard link) is read and written like any file; one that is replaced keeps its other name with the old content. On macOS and Windows the instant between looking at a directory and using it remains, as said above. The commands read the current directory only (slice 2 moves them to `ConsoleProject`). `create-functional-test` for Geb is the script of the Geb plugin (section 2.5 item 5, slice 7) and is not offered in a web application here; in a web or plugin project `create-functional-test`, `create-restful-controller` and `create-domain-resource` are unknown commands with picocli's "Unmatched arguments" and no word that they belong to REST API applications, which needs a change to dispatch (slice 2). `rest-api-plugin` is no application type yet, so the three REST commands are not in a plugin. In a generated REST API application the tests `create-controller` and `create-interceptor` write do not compile, because such an application has no `grails-testing-support-web`. The completion script and the man pages are generated from the commands of `ForgeCommand` and list none of the code generation commands. The scaffolding plugin has an application command that is also called `install-templates` and copies `src/main/templates/scaffolding`; the two do not know of each other, and slice 5b settled the name (the command of the CLI keeps it, see below). picocli expands an `@file` argument before a command sees it, here as everywhere. The rule for links and names was run on macOS (APFS, which takes one case for another) only; not run on Windows.

Pack artefacts are not done, and the pack format is unchanged. For a pack to bring an artefact command, the descriptor needs a section the parser refuses today (`PackDescriptorParser.KEYS` is closed), for example `artefacts:`, a bounded list whose entries have: `command`, the name of the command (lower case with hyphens; the names of the built-in commands and of the commands of other selected packs are refused, with both origins); `description` and the label of the argument, each one line; `convention`, what the kind of class adds to its name, or nothing; `files`, a list of `from` (a template under `templates`, held to the rules `files` has) and `to` (a path below the project with the placeholders `{packagePath}`, `{className}`, `{propertyName}`, checked with `isPathBelowRoot` after substitution as well); and optionally the application types the command is for. Its templates are GSP by the pack rules and rendered by `PackPages`, so the template API needs a second model beside `PackModel`, for the names of the class (`artefact.packageName`, `artefact.packagePath`, `artefact.className`, `artefact.propertyName`) with `project` as it is, and `PackCheck` has to render each with sample names. Registration is in the CLI where the code generation commands are added (`ForgeCli.createCommandLine`, the catalog once slice 2 has it), one command per entry of every pack feature of `.grails/forge.lock.yml`: that needs the content of the pack at the time of the command, so a locked pack has to be found again without the network (bundled packs from the CLI itself; an external one from the artifact cache of `PackArtifacts` by the coordinates, version and digest of the lock, refused when the digest differs; a pack given by `--feature-pack-dir` has no such source recorded). The lookup then has three levels, as section 2.5 item 4 says: `src/main/templates/<pack name>/<from>` of the project, the pack, and for the built-in commands the CLI; a namespace per pack keeps packs from replacing one another's templates and those of the CLI. A pack template is code that is run, unlike the templates above, which is the trust a pack has already.

**Script commands, as implemented (step 4, slice 7).** Decided for this slice, against the cut, which put "a script host in the CLI": a script is run by the build of the project and never by the CLI. Item 5 of this section does not say where a script runs. A script `src/main/scripts/<name>.groovy` is code of the project, as its build file is, and the CLI runs code of a project nowhere else: it runs no template of a project (slice 6) and starts the wrapper of a project only for one it trusts and for a command that is typed (slices 1 and 3). A host in the CLI would have compiled and evaluated a file of the project in the JVM of the CLI for a name that was typed, and, for descriptions, for every `help`. So the host is in the command line tier of the framework (`grails-core/src/cli`, the `grails-core-cli` artifact that the Grails Gradle plugin already puts on `grailsCliClasspath`), a Gradle task starts it, and the CLI lists file names and hands a name and an argument vector to that task through `GradleRunner`. The CLI has no new way of executing anything.

What a script command is in the shell, from the code: a Groovy script compiled by `GroovyScriptCommandFactory` with `GroovyScriptCommand` as its base class and `GroovyScriptCommandTransform` moving `description(...)` into its constructor, found by `ResourceResolvingCommandFactory` in the profile, in `src/main/scripts` and `commands` of the project, and as `META-INF/commands/*` on the class path the shell fills from the `GrailsClasspath` tooling model, named by its file (`fn.contains('-') ? fn.toLowerCase() : GrailsNameUtils.getScriptName(fn)`), and dispatched through the profile.
