#### How Gradle is run: a hybrid, and why

This subsection is the decision as it stands (revisions 45 and 46). Where an "as implemented" paragraph above says that the Tooling API is not used, that `GradleRunner` is the one place that runs Gradle, or that a wrapper process was decided for a slice, this subsection holds and those paragraphs are the history of how it came about.

**The rule.** `GradleBuild.of` is the one place that chooses.

| Where a command is run | What runs Gradle | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A one-shot command line (`grails compile`, `grails test-app`, a foreground `grails run-app`, a command of the application) | The Gradle wrapper of the project, as a process | Gradle has the terminal: its own colours, progress and prompts, and every option of its command line. The Tooling API would save less than a tenth of a second of a command that starts a JVM anyway. |
| The same commands typed in the interactive console | The Gradle Tooling API, on one connection the console keeps | The console is one long-lived JVM that runs many builds: a further build takes about 50 ms where a wrapper process takes about 350, and a build is cancelled by a token and its end is observed, where a process is ended and the daemon is trusted to notice. |
| `grails gradle ...` | The wrapper, always | It hands Gradle what was typed. The Tooling API refuses or ignores what the `gradle` program reads itself (`--daemon`, `--no-daemon`, `--status`, `--stop`, `-g`), and a passthrough must behave like the program it passes to. |
| `run-app --detach`, and `run-app` in the console | The wrapper, always | The application has to outlive the command line; a Tooling API operation belongs to the JVM that started it. |
| `shell` | The wrapper, always | It needs the terminal and its input for itself. |
| Anything, with `GRAILS_GRADLE_RUNNER=process` or `=tooling-api` | What the variable names | The way out where one path misbehaves, and the way to choose the Tooling API for one-shot commands in a tool that wants plain, ordered output. |

**How it came about.** The design planned the Tooling API for every command of a build (the paragraphs on lifecycle commands and the table of execution targets in this section). Slice 1 of step 4 started the wrapper as a process instead, "for now", because the Tooling API was a dependency of the Grails shell only and not on the class path of the Forge CLI, and slice 4 kept the process when `run-app` and `stop-app` were built. That was a shortcut of the implementation, recorded as one, and not a judgement of the two: the absence of a dependency is no architectural reason. When the question was put, the two were compared on what they do.

**What is the same either way.** The Gradle daemon is reused and the build cache and the configuration cache apply: a wrapper process is a small client that talks to the daemon, as the Tooling API is. Neither says when an application is ready: a `bootRun` task that has started says nothing of Spring, so readiness is asked of the application (its startup progress, or its port) on both paths. The wrapper of the project is what decides the Gradle version on both, and the checks of `ConsoleProject.gradleWrapper` come before either.

**What the Tooling API is better at.** It needs no client JVM for a build, which is most of what a build that has nothing to do costs. It cancels a build by a token and reports that it was cancelled, where the process path ends the client and relies on the daemon to see it go (which it does: `WarmGradleDaemonSpec` holds that to a real daemon, in under a second). It has none of the limits of a batch file on Windows, where the process path refuses some arguments and some paths of a project.

**What the wrapper process is better at.** Gradle draws its own output on the terminal it is given. Through the Tooling API it is given streams: with colour on it draws its progress over what it writes to the error stream, which lost the end of a failure report in a terminal, so the Tooling API path writes plain text. The process takes every option of Gradle. It can be left running. And it reads the terminal itself, where the Tooling API reads the stream it is handed as soon as a build starts and keeps reading it, so that in the console a build is given no input: the line reader of the console and the build cannot both own it.

**Measured** (one machine, macOS, Gradle 9.9.2, a daemon that was running; the builds have nothing to do, so the figures are the cost of asking, not of building):

| | Wrapper process | Tooling API |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `./gradlew help`, and the same build on a kept connection | 362 ms, about 280 of it the client JVM | 25–32 ms |
| A one-shot `grails compile` of a generated application, from start to exit | about 890 ms | 780–820 ms |
| Each further `compile` in the interactive console | 341–388 ms | 49–62 ms |

**What the hybrid costs.** There are two paths, and each has to select the same tasks, give the same properties, report failure and cancellation alike, and be tested where it runs: the specifications run the commands through both, a stub wrapper for the process and a real build with a daemon of its own for the Tooling API. The two keep separate configuration cache entries, so a project that is built from the console and from a terminal configures once for each. The command line carries the Tooling API, about 2.5 MB. Neither path was run on Windows or Linux outside continuous integration, and the Tooling API was run against one version of Gradle.

**What would change it.** If Gradle's output through the Tooling API can be drawn on a terminal without losing what goes to the error stream, the one-shot commands lose their reason for the process. If a build in the console is to be given input, the console has to hand its input over for the length of the build and take it back, which is work that was not done. `GRAILS_GRADLE_RUNNER` exists so that either can be tried without a change of the code.


#### Multi-project builds

A Grails application can be one project of a larger Gradle build, at any depth below its root, beside other applications and beside projects that are no Grails projects. The rules, which the paragraph "Project detection" above asked for in one sentence:

1. *The project* is found as everywhere: the nearest directory from the current one upwards that has a `grails-app` directory or a record. Every command of a project reads and writes there, from the directory of the project and from any directory below it.
2. *The build* the project is a part of is found as Gradle finds it: a project whose directory has `settings.gradle` or `settings.gradle.kts` is a build of its own (also when another build includes it with `includeBuild`); otherwise the root of its build is the nearest directory above it that has one of the two, within the repository and the 32 levels of the search for the project. A project without settings and without such a directory above is a build of its own, as before.
3. *The wrapper* that is run for a project of a larger build is the one in the root of that build. A wrapper in the directory of the project, as an application that was moved into a build still has, is not run while the root has one, because the build decides which Gradle it is built with; when the root has none, the search of slice 1 is what it was (the nearest wrapper from the project upwards). The trust rule of slice 1 holds for the root as for any directory above the project: owner, mode and link counts of the directory, of its settings, of the wrapper script and of the jar and properties it runs, and `GRAILS_SAFE_DIRECTORY` as the way out.
4. *The tasks* a command of the CLI names are addressed to the project by its path in the build, and Gradle is run in the directory of the project: `compile` in `apps/shop` is `:apps:shop:classes`, `run-app` is `:apps:shop:bootRun`. An unqualified name in the directory of a project selects, by Gradle's rule, the task of that name in the project and in every project below it; the path selects the one. The path is the directories from the root to the project with colons, which is the path Gradle gives a project that is included by its directory (`include 'apps:shop'`). It is not read from the settings script: that would be evaluating a build script, which the CLI does not do, or guessing at its text. A project the settings give another path (`project(':shop').projectDir = ...`) is therefore not reached by these commands; Gradle then fails on the path and the command adds a note that says which path it asked for and which `include` line gives it. Where a directory on the way has a name that is no project name as it is (anything but letters, digits, `_`, `.` and `-`), no path is used and the task names are unqualified, as in a build of its own. What the user gives to `gradle`, and what follows `--` on a command line, is never rewritten.
5. *Outside a Grails project and inside a build*, at the root of a build of several projects or in a library of it: `gradle` runs Gradle in the current directory with the wrapper of the build, under the trust rule when the root is above. Every command of a project refuses with exit code 1 and names the Grails projects of the build, found by the names of directories only (five levels below the root, 20,000 entries, no link followed, not in `build`, `out`, `target`, `node_modules` or a directory whose name begins with a dot, not inside a project that was found; ten are named and the rest counted). It does not choose one, also when there is only one, and there is no option that names the project: the directory the command is given in is how a project is named, as for Gradle and Git. A root above the current directory is listed only when it is the user's own.
6. *Creating an application in a build* is asked for: `--subproject` on the create commands. It needs a build above the directory the application is created in (rule 2) and is refused, with nothing written, where there is none, where the directory would itself be the root of a build (`--inplace` in a directory with settings), and with the feature `gradle-settings-file`. The application is generated as always and written without the files of the root of a build: `gradlew`, `gradlew.bat`, `gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar`, `gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.properties`, `settings.gradle`, `settings.gradle.kts`, `grailsw`, `grailsw.bat`, `grails-wrapper.jar` and the root `.gitattributes` (which has rules for the wrapper scripts and nothing else), at the root of the application and compared without regard to case; and its `gradle.properties` has `grailsVersion`, `version` and what features add, without the `org.gradle.*` settings that Gradle reads at the root of a build only. `build.gradle` is unchanged: it has its own `buildscript` and repositories, so the build around it needs nothing for the application. The settings script of the build is never written to: the command prints the line to add, `include 'apps:shop'` or for a Kotlin settings script `include("apps:shop")`, and where the directories give no path it says that instead.
7. *The state* records it: `options` of the manifest and of the lock have `layout: subproject`, written only for such an application, so the state of every other application is byte for byte what it was and needs no new format number; a reader of the earlier code refuses the key, as it refuses every key it does not know. The baseline is the files that were written. `feature add` and `project regenerate` replay with the recorded layout, so the proposed tree has the same subset of files and the additivity check has nothing to say about wrappers.
8. *Creating an application in a build without the option* is not refused: the application is whole, and the command warns that Gradle will take its directory for a part of the build above, which does not include it, and names the two ways (`--subproject`, or the feature `gradle-settings-file` for a build of its own).

Implemented: all eight. `ConsoleProject` has the root of the build (`getBuildRoot`), the path (`getGradlePath`), `task(name)`, which every command that names a task uses (`compile`, `clean`, `assemble`, `dependency-report`, `test-app` with its clean tasks, `package-plugin`, `publish-plugin`, `run-app`, `console`, `shell`, `run-script`, `run-command`, the commands of the application and the scripts), `build(directory)` for `gradle` outside a project, and the list of the projects of a build inside `Search.none`, so every command that refuses outside a project says the same. `RunningApplication` believes a record when the command line of its process has the wrapper of the build and the task by the path of this project, so `stop-app` in one application of a build does not take the `bootRun` of another for its own; the record, the log and the files of arguments stay in `.grails/run` of the project. The generator leaves the files out in one place (`DefaultProjectGenerator`, by `ProjectState.isOfBuildRoot`), after the features were applied and before the paths are checked and the state is built; `Options.isSubproject` carries the choice, `GenerationOptions.subproject` records it, `FeatureAddition` replays it. Covered by `MultiProjectBuildSpec` (through `ForgeCli.execute`, the wrapper of a generated application at the root of a build with two Grails projects, one of them two directories down, one that the settings do not include, a library and an included build, every project with tasks that say which task of which project ran: each command from the project, from below it, from the root and from the library; the wrapper of the root preferred to one in the project; the only wrapper in the project; a root that is not the user's and the opt-in; `run-app --detach` and `stop-app` per project), `CreateSubprojectSpec` (what is and is not written, both settings syntaxes, that no existing file changes, the refusals, the warning, `feature add` giving file for file the application that is created with the feature, `create-controller` and `help --json` below the project) and a feature of `ProjectStateSpec`.

Not done: the path of a project is never read from the settings, as said in rule 4. There is no option that names the project from the root of a build. A project of a build is told by the directories alone: the settings are not asked whether they include it, so a project that is not included fails in Gradle, with the note. `help` at the root of a build lists no Grails projects; only a command that needs one does. Staleness of the list of application commands looks at the build files of the project and not at those of the root of its build. The hosted Forge has no such mode, and `--subproject` cannot be turned on or off afterwards: `layout` is part of what `feature add` replays. A `.gitignore` is written for the application as always, with the lines for a wrapper that is not there. `grailsw` is not written for such an application, so the Grails wrapper is the one at the root of the build or none. An application of an earlier version that was moved into a build and has no `.grails` is a project by its `grails-app`, and works by rules 1 to 5. Not run on Windows.

Run by hand, 2026-11-20, in a build made of applications this CLI generated (one moved into the build with its wrapper, two created with `--subproject`, one two directories down, one created inside the build without the option, one included as a build, and a library), against the framework published from this tree: `gradle projects` at the root; `compile` below an application (`:apps:admin:classes` and nothing else ran); `compile` in the application the settings do not include (Gradle: the directory is not part of the build; then the note); `compile` in the included build; `run-app --detach`, a request answered with 200, `stop-app` in another application of the build refused, `stop-app` below the application stopping it. The Gradle task `shell` of a generated web application, which failed with `TypeNotPresentException: Type org.jline.shell.CommandGroup not present` when slice 9 was run, starts now: `shell` in an application of the build came to the `groovy>` prompt.

**Historical command execution model.** These observations describe the reviewed pre-unification implementation. Profiles couple the dispatcher to generation metadata even when the command itself does not need a profile.

| Where a command is defined today | Author | Runs where | Needs a profile? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Profile jar `commands/*.yml` or `.groovy` | profile author | shell JVM | yes, by definition |
| App `src/main/scripts/*.groovy` | app developer | shell JVM | only because the shell dispatches every in-project command through the profile object (`GrailsCli.groovy:385`) |
| Plugin jar `META-INF/commands/*` (from `src/main/scripts`) | plugin author | shell JVM, found on the app's test runtime classpath | same |
| `ApplicationCommand` in a plugin's `-cli` companion (`src/cli/groovy`) | plugin author | one Gradle `JavaExec` task per command, created by `GrailsCliGradlePlugin`; the shell wraps each one in `GradleTaskCommandAdapter` | no on the Gradle path; the shell listing still goes through the profile |
| `ApplicationCommand` in the app (`grails-app/commands`) | app developer | `./gradlew runCommand -Pargs=...` only; no task, invisible to the shell | no |
| Legacy `grails.dev.commands.ApplicationCommand` with `legacyCommandSupport` | Grails 7 plugin | `runCommand` only; the shell lists it but calls a task that does not exist | no |

The runtime half of this system is `grails-console`, which the Gradle plugin auto-provisions into `grailsCli` because every command task's main class comes from it. `GrailsApplicationContextCommandRunner` (per-command tasks and `runCommand`) boots the application through `DevelopmentGrailsApplication`, which swaps the context class to `DevelopmentWebApplicationContext`: a `GenericWebApplicationContext` over a `MockServletContext` pointed at `src/main/webapp`. The command gets the full context, plugins, GORM, data sources and `BootStrap`, unless it carries `skipBootstrap = true` (the `SkipBootstrap` trait, currently copied into scaffolding and spring-security). No Tomcat, no port. `GrailsApplicationScriptRunner` (`runScript`), `GrailsSwingConsole` (`console`) and `GrailsShell` (`shell`) boot the same way with console-flavoured contexts; the profile commands named `console` and `shell` only invoked those Gradle tasks.

The inventoried `generate-open-api`, `dbm-*`, `schema-export`, `url-mappings-report`, `config-report`, `generate-*` and `s2-*` commands use an application context rather than an embedded server. `run-app` drives `bootRun` through the Tooling API, with asynchronous operation, readiness checks, PID handling and HTTPS setup; `stop-app` manages that process. `test-app` delegates to test tasks, whose JVM may start a server. Preserve these distinctions rather than introducing a second server-starting application-context runner.

**Target command extension contract.** Use one execution target per descriptor rather than two booleans with ambiguous combinations:

| Target | Execution and available services | Examples / Gradle exposure |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `console` | CLI host, no project application context; terminal, cancellation, project metadata, `GradleRunner`, readiness and running-process services | lifecycle orchestration, `sql-tail`, Forge codegen (generator context loaded on demand), migration; no per-command Gradle task that could launch a nested build |
| `standalone` | As built (revision 43): the marker `org.apache.grails.core.cli.StandaloneCommand` on an `ApplicationCommand`; the same `runCommand` task and `GrailsApplicationContextCommandRunner`, which calls the command without making an application context (`applicationContext` is `null`, nothing is injected, the configuration is not loaded); the descriptor says `"target":"standalone"`. Not built: running it inside the CLI, or as a plain `JavaExec` that does not compile the project first | `query-tail`, the seven commands of the Spring Security plugins that only write files; may have a per-command task |
| `application` | Gradle `JavaExec` with `GrailsApplicationContextCommandRunner`, mock servlet and full application context | existing `ApplicationCommand` default, including `dbm-*`, schema and mappings reports; named task or `runCommand` |
| `gradle` | Tooling API passthrough to a task, preserving argument tokens and cancellation | `gradle`, `compile`, `clean`, `assemble`, `war`, `test-app` |
| `session` | dedicated application JVM with appropriate stdin/stdout handling | `console`, `shell`, `run-script`; script execution need not be interactive |

The new profile-free extension API belongs to the CLI contract layer; Tooling API and terminal implementations stay in the CLI host. Existing `ApplicationCommand.handle(ExecutionContext)` implementations remain `application` commands unless explicitly adapted to the new contract. Do not infer safe in-process execution merely from `SkipBootstrap`: it suppresses `BootStrap`, not the application context. An old companion without new metadata takes the context-bound compatibility path, never an implicit fast path.

The console services supply project/base/build paths, declared configuration access, terminal output, cancellation registration, synchronous/asynchronous `GradleRunner`, readiness checks and `RunningApplication`. Configuration access does not pretend to be a fully booted Spring environment: values requiring application evaluation go through an application command. A console command that needs a context delegates to `runCommand` instead of booting the project inside the host. A server-oriented command starts or attaches through lifecycle services, records ownership and stops only a process it owns; cancellation must not kill an unrelated pre-existing server.

Use project-scoped, disposable extension loaders with shared CLI API types; restore the thread context classloader after execution and dispose loaders on project/classpath refresh. Isolate incompatible Groovy/application stacks in their appropriate worker JVM instead of merging them into the Forge application's classpath. Define and test linkage diagnostics and cleanup of listeners, threads and task handles. Keep a Tooling API connection per project session, close it on exit or refresh, and ensure a failing extension does not make built-in help unavailable. Runtime discovery currently instantiates commands (`ApplicationContextCommandRegistry`); that is a behavior to replace for metadata listing, not evidence of zero-cost execution.

**Discovery and JSON catalog.** Extend build-produced companion metadata with schema version, canonical command ID, name/aliases, description, argument/option schema, completion kind, execution target, API compatibility and origin. Read descriptors without loading implementation classes. Generate app-local command metadata from compiled `grails-app/commands`; invalidate cached metadata when source or classpath changes. Initial `help --json` uses built-ins, locked pack descriptors and valid cached companion/app metadata without invoking Gradle. It reports `complete: false` plus diagnostics when discovery is unavailable. An explicit refresh may resolve dependencies or compile app-local commands; failures leave the built-ins usable and are reported structurally. JSON stdout contains one versioned document, with ordinary logs on stderr. Refresh interactive completion from the same catalog after successful mutations.

**Lifecycle options.** A companion descriptor or selected pack may declare `runAppOptions`: name, type, description, default and the Gradle/system property mapping. Names are validated against built-ins and other providers; collisions fail with both origins. `run-app --sql-tail` is the reference use case. Translate values once through the existing property-forwarding rules and test the resulting application property; do not expose the doubled `grails.*` transport prefix as the public option contract. Help and completion use the declarations without instantiating commands. Pass-through `-D` and `-P` remain available.

Housekeeping: consolidate `SkipBootstrap` in the CLI contract layer, route legacy commands to `runCommand`, and surface app-local commands. Test supported already-compiled companion artifacts in addition to recompiling fixtures; source compatibility alone does not establish binary compatibility across Groovy/Grails versions.

**IDE integration.** The IntelliJ plugin is now `apache/grails-intellij-plugin` (donated by JetBrains, imported from `intellij-obsolete-plugins`), so its CLI coupling is work this plan schedules rather than a risk to confirm. What it does today, from the source:

- Commands run in a JVM the IDE starts itself, main class `org.grails.cli.GrailsCli`, classpath = the `org.apache.grails:grails-shell-cli:<grailsVersion>` artifact resolved through a detached Gradle configuration by the plugin's own tooling model builder (`libs/gradle-tooling/.../GrailsModuleModelBuilderImpl.java:54-78`, `plugin/.../runner/impl/Grails3GradleCommandExecutor.java:54`), with `--plain-output` appended. The `grails` launcher and `grails-forge-cli` are not involved.
- The command list is hard-coded: 34 names from the profile era (`bug-report`, `clean`, `compile`, `console`, `create-*`, `dependency-report`, `generate-*`, `gradle`, `install`, `install-templates`, `list-plugins`, `plugin-info`, `run-app`, `schema-export`, `shell`, `stats`, `stop-app`, `test-app`, `url-mappings-report`, `war`) in `plugin/.../commands/Grails3CommandProvider.java:31-70`.
- The Grails run configuration is `run-app` through the same executor (`runner/GrailsRunConfiguration.java:70`), so the IDE's Run button depends on the base profile's `run-app` script and its pid file.
- Project detection is the `grails-app` directory (`structure/GrailsApplicationManager.java:167`).
- The new-project wizard calls the hosted Forge API (`https://latest.grails.org`: `/select-options`, `/versions`, `/application-types/{type}/features`, `/create`) in `feature/forge/GrailsForgeModuleBuilder.kt:67-221`.
- The plugin's own `IMPROVEMENT-PLAN.md` section 2.6 already intends to move the run configuration to Gradle `bootRun`, rebuild the command layer from profile YAML plus forge-cli commands, and keep the wizard on the hosted API with an offline fallback via `grails-forge-cli`. The profile half of that plan is superseded by this one.

What changes on the Grails side so the plugin can follow:

1. *A stable programmatic entry point.* The proposed 9.0 contract is `org.apache.grails:grails-cli:<grailsVersion>` with a new `org.apache.grails.cli.GrailsCli` main class (distinct from the old `org.grails.cli.GrailsCli`). Keep the regular Maven artifact resolvable with its dependency metadata; the distribution may also supply its shaded artifact. The launcher supports the help-only fast path, `--plain-output`, `--non-interactive` and documented exit codes. The IntelliJ release selects the old shell contract for 8.1 and the new contract for 9.0. Test both project versions in that release; compatibility is not automatic merely because the artifact version varies.
2. *A machine-readable command catalog.* Use the versioned catalog above instead of hard-coded names, including its completeness and diagnostics fields. Unsupported descriptors or unavailable app-local compilation do not disable the IDE's built-in run/help actions.
3. *Project detection and type.* The plugin reads `.grails/project.yml` first (application type, default package, features), falling back to `grails-app`.
4. *Run configuration.* Use Gradle `bootRun` directly, as the plugin's plan proposes, while also testing CLI `run-app --non-interactive`. Both pass the same configured lifecycle-state location under `.grails/run/` on 9.0. Coordinate the PID writer, DevTools restart handling, port readiness and stop ownership; changing only the CLI's read path would break IDE launches. The 8.1 path remains `build/`.
5. *Wizard.* Retain the hosted API workflow for official features, with compatibility tests for the 9.0 type/feature metadata and generated project state. External packs and `feature add` remain CLI operations the IDE may surface through the same launcher.

Sequencing: the plugin release is lock-step with 9.0.0, which is why step 5 lists it.

<a id="feature-add"></a>
### 2.6 `feature add` on an existing app

Existing `FeatureDiffer` is presentation infrastructure: it compares a default project with a requested selection, recreates baseline options without preserving GORM/servlet choices, and uses `MapOutputHandler`, which discards binary contents. Extend it for arbitrary typed output trees; do not treat applying a unified patch as a three-way merge implementation.

The transaction is:

1. Read and validate the manifest, lock and committed baseline. Require `generationState: tracked`, matching generator identity and unchanged recorded inputs. A missing/corrupt baseline or stale lock fails before writing. Adding an already-selected identical feature is a no-op; requesting another version is an explicit upgrade request outside v1 `feature add`.
2. Re-render the locked selection with all recorded options, parameters and overrides. Compare bytes and modes with the committed baseline **A**. Abort on nondeterminism or unavailable locked inputs; never silently substitute current defaults, another artifact or a new generator version. Resolve only the new requested feature and its requirements, preserving existing selections and locks, to generate proposed tree **B**.
3. Compare **A**, current working-tree files **C**, and **B**. Use a true base/current/proposed text-merge engine (for example JGit's three-way merge APIs); use java-diff-utils for readable patch output. Explicitly test the library's conflict behavior. B does not contain the user's unrelated edits, so those edits must remain in C's merged result.
4. Plan every path before applying anything. A new B path already present in C is a collision unless content and mode are identical and adoption is recorded. User deletions, file/directory changes, symlinks, overlapping edits and binary changes on both sides are conflicts. Binary content and executable modes use whole-value comparisons; never decode binary bytes as text. Preserve unchanged user line endings and honor the locked policy for generated additions.
5. (Changed in revision 37: a default feature that the added feature displaces is replaced; see the change log.) V1 is additive: reject implicit removal of an existing selected feature or generated path, and report the proposed diff instead. `--dry-run` and `--diff` render the plan without applying project changes. On any conflict return a nonzero status with structured path/reason diagnostics and rejected changes; do not write successful hunks or update the manifest. Rendering still executes trusted templates and may populate caches, as section 2.2 explains.
6. For a conflict-free apply, acquire the project mutation lock, verify the recorded C preimages are still current, and stage all file changes plus the new manifest, lock and baseline. The new baseline is pristine **B**, not the merged user working tree. Use per-file atomic replacement plus a recovery journal/backups for the multi-file operation; ordinary filesystems do not provide an atomic project-wide rename. The journal, which alone says that there is a change to undo and names its files, is kept in a directory of the user outside the project (`~/.grails/forge/transactions/<digest of the project's real path>/journal`, owner-only), because the project tree is untrusted input: a clone or an archive can carry any file under `.grails/transactions`, and a journal read from there would let it replace or delete files of whoever runs the command. The staged files and the backups stay in the project, beside their targets, so that every replacement and every restoration is a rename within one file system; they are named and verified by the digests of the journal and are never trusted on their own. Recheck affected preimages before replacing them and roll back ordinary write failures only where content still matches what the transaction wrote. Detected external edits become recovery conflicts, not overwrite targets. The lock serializes CLI mutations; it cannot provide transaction isolation against arbitrary editors writing during the apply window. An interrupted transaction is recovered or explicitly resolved before another mutation; never report success for a partially installed feature.
7. Complete the journal only after all project and generation-state files are installed, release resources, and refresh the command catalog. A second identical invocation makes no changes. The CLI performs no Git commit and does not require Git to implement the merge.

Implemented: `feature add FEATURE... [--dry-run] [--diff]` of the Forge CLI (`FeatureCommand`, `FeatureAddCommand`), over `org.apache.grails.forge.core.change` (`FeatureAddition`, `ChangePlan`, `ProjectRecovery`, `ThreeWayMerge`, and the transaction, which is not public). It works on a project that has `.grails/project.yml`. Step 1: a change of this user that was interrupted is ended first (not with `--dry-run` or `--diff`, which refuse instead and change nothing); `ProjectState.read` and `verifyBaseline` have to pass; `generationState` has to be `tracked`; the lock's `generator.name`, `generator.version`, `grailsVersion` and `templateApi` have to be those of the running CLI, otherwise the message names the version to use; an application with a feature whose lock source is `pack` is refused, as is a name with a colon (coordinates). The names are resolved to canonical names with the features of the application type; when the lock has all of them the command says so, writes nothing, not the manifest either, and ends with 0. Step 2: the application is generated in memory through `ProjectGenerator.createGeneratorContext` and `generate`, as a create command does, from the manifest: type, name, package, the requested and the excluded features, and the options, every one of them marked as given (`Options.withGiven`), with no packs. The manifest, the lock and the index this replay writes have to be, byte for byte, those of the project, so one comparison covers nondeterminism, another `GRAILS_REPO_URL` and another build of the generator; the refusal names the files that differ. The proposed tree **B** is the same generation with the new names added to the requested features; a refusal of the selection is the create command's `FeatureSelectionException` with its message, as a usage error (exit 2). Step 5's additivity: **B** has to have the options of the manifest, every feature of the lock and every path of the index. A feature that does not (a default that stands aside for it, such as `h2` for `postgres` or `sitemesh3` for `grails-layout`; a feature the selection refuses because of an option of the application, such as `gorm-hibernate7`, for which **B** is then generated with the options not marked as given) gives a plan that is refused: it cannot be applied, the command ends with 1 and a message that names what would be lost and the two ways there are (create the application with the feature; change it by hand after `--diff`), and with `--dry-run` or `--diff` the plan is shown in full, with the lost features, the changed options and the files that would be left out (`remove`) and their removal in the diff. Steps 3 and 4: for every file of **B** whose bytes or executable flag are not those of **A** (the replayed bytes, which are the baseline's), the working file **C** is read without following links: no **A** and no **C** is `add`; no **A** and a **C** with the content of **B** (text compared with CR LF taken for LF, binary byte for byte) and its executable flag is `keep`, anything else a conflict; **A** and no **C**, or a **C** that is a directory, a link, below a link, larger than a baseline file, or listed in its directory under a name that differs from the path in the case or composition of its letters, is a conflict; a file either index calls binary is replaced when **C** is **A**, kept when **C** is **B**, and a conflict otherwise; a text file gets the three-way merge. The merge (`ThreeWayMerge`) is line based, on its own Myers shortest edit scripts of **A** to **B** and **A** to **C** (JGit is not managed by the Grails BOM and was not added; java-diff-utils only formats the unified diffs that are shown, from the changes the merge works out within its bounds). Because files with lines that repeat can be lined up in more than one way, the merge goes by anchors: a line of **A** that neither side changed and that, for each side, every longest common subsequence of **A** and that side has, and has with the same line of that side. That is worked out from the longest common subsequences before and after each line, for the part of two files that differs and the lines before and after it that are like a line of that part, up to four million pairs of lines; beyond that, which is a cliff at about 2,000 by 2,000 differing lines, a line is an anchor when it is there once in that part of both files. An empty line or a closing brace is an anchor like any other line where it can only be lined up one way, and is none where a line like it was added, removed or moved beside it. The anchors cut the files into stretches; where the nearest anchors of every line are is worked out once, so the merge is linear in the lines beyond the diffs. A stretch only one side changed is taken from that side; a stretch both changed is a conflict, unless both have the very same lines in it, which are then the result. That last is the one declared difference from `git merge-file`, which lines the two sides up apart and can have a conflict of two equal halves there: it is the change of the feature that was already made by hand, and it has to merge. Otherwise the merge is never bolder than Git: on ten seeds each of the reviewers' nine kinds of files (809,200 cases, the files the generator writes with changes of a user among them) and of the author's (40,000 cases) there is no result where Git has a conflict but in 35 cases that have a stretch both sides changed alike, and never another result than Git's. Agreement with Git is not enough, because Git doubles a block that both sides moved, each to another place: the removal is one stretch both changed alike, and the two additions are two stretches of one side each. Every result is therefore held, before it is returned, to a count of its lines that does not depend on how the files were lined up: (1) every line is in the result as often as in **A**, plus what **C** has more or less of it, plus what **B** has more or less of it, minus what the stretches both changed alike account for twice; (2) a line whose count a stretch both changed alike changes is not changed the other way by both sides elsewhere, which is a line both moved (it would be there twice) or both gathered in one place (it would be there once less than any file has it); (3) a line that **A** has not is not in the result more often than the side that has it most often has it, which is the same new line added by both, each in another place. A line that fails one of these is a conflict that names the line, not a result. Lines like many others that both sides add independently, a closing brace or an empty line, pass: the counts account for them. When a merge took a stretch both changed alike, `feature add` says under the file that the change of the project and that of the feature are the same there (`ThreeWayMerge.getChangedAlike`). What the merge cannot see, that two different lines declare one dependency, is looked for in the merged `build.gradle` by a bounded scan of the `dependencies` blocks for `<configuration> "group:artifact[:version]"` in either quote, and said as a note under the file when a coordinate is declared more than once for a configuration in the result and in neither input; nothing is changed for it. The same scan of a merged YAML file under `grails-app/conf` for a top-level key that is twice in one document, in the result and in neither input, is a conflict, because such a file is always wrong. A conflict says what the three files show and no more: how many lines the generated file had in the place, how many the project and the feature have there, and the lines. For the files the generator writes, a spec measures how often one change of the user (a line added at every place, every line removed, every line changed, an empty line added at every place, for every feature that changes `build.gradle`, `application.yml` and `README.md`) is in the way of a feature, and holds the numbers to upper bounds: `build.gradle` 123, 176, 180 and 125 of about 5,450, which is what the merge of adjacent changes had before there were anchors, `application.yml` 61, 83, 83 and 54 of about 540 and `README.md` 70, 101, 101 and 71 of about 850, which is less; a change of a feature that was made by hand never is. The work is bounded: a file of more than 200,000 lines, a side that differs from **A** in more than 2,000 lines, and a comparison of more than 50 million line pairs are not merged and are a conflict ("more lines than are merged"); lines that were only added in one place, or only removed, are one change whatever their number. Lines of **C** are kept byte for byte; lines from **B** are written with CR LF when every line ending of **C** is CR LF and as generated otherwise; files are handled as bytes, never decoded and re-encoded. The executable flag is one value: when **A** and **B** differ in it, the file gets **B**'s; a replaced file keeps its other permission bits. `grails-forge-cli.yml`, which is in no baseline, is handled the same way with the replayed file as **A**, and is left out when the project has none. A file whose **A** and **B** are equal is never read. With a conflict nothing is written; every conflict is listed with its path, its reason and details: for lines both changed, the line number in the project's file, the lines of both sides (five places, eight lines each, 160 characters a line) and what to do; for the others, what to do. `--diff` shows the rejected change of each conflict (**A** to **B**) and the change of every other file (**C** to result) as unified diffs, at most 400 lines of a file and 2,000 in all with a line that counts the rest, a difference beyond the bounds of the merge as one line, control and format characters (bidirectional controls among them) as `?`, and without the carriage return of CR LF lines. The status is 1, also for `--dry-run`. Step 6: `.grails/transactions/lock` has to be a regular file or absent (a pipe or a link of the name is refused before it is opened) and is taken with a file lock (a second command is refused; the directory is made without a window in which a second command fails on its existence). The journal is kept outside the project, in `~/.grails/forge/transactions/<SHA-256 of the project's real path>/journal`. The home directory has to be an absolute path of an existing directory that is the user's and whose real path is not the real path of the project or inside it (the invariant is that the directory of the journals is never inside the project, whatever links lead to it: a project could otherwise bring a journal along; a relative `user.home`, such as the `?` of a user without a passwd entry in a container, would be resolved in the current directory, which is the project). With a home directory that is not so, nothing is looked for, `--dry-run` works, and adding a feature is refused with a line that names the property and says which of these it is; every level below it (`.grails`, `forge`, `transactions`, the directory of the project) has to be a real directory, the user's and writable by nobody else, whether it is made, in which case it is made owner-only, or only read. The journal says the path of the project again, base64 encoded because a path can have a character that ends a line, and names the directory of the change. A first record (`staging`) is written before anything is staged, so that the staging directory of a change that ended early is known to be this user's. The new contents and copies of the replaced files, with their permissions, are written and forced to `.grails/transactions/<random name>/new` and `backup`, in the project so that every replacement and restoration is a rename within one file system; every preimage is compared by digest with what the plan read, the three state files included. Then the journal (`put <n> <digest before or -> <digest after> <path>`, `dir <path>` for a directory that will be made, `end`) replaces the first record by a rename and is forced, as are the directories, where the platform can force a directory. Each file is compared once more and replaced by an atomic rename, application files first, then the changed copies of the baseline, the index, the lock and the manifest last. The new baseline, lock and manifest are the bytes the generator's own `ProjectStateRecorder` wrote for **B**, read back with `ProjectState.of`, which is public for that. Step 7: the journal is removed, then the directory. A write that fails while the change is made puts back, last first, the files this run wrote, each of which has to have the digest it was given, and leaves a file it had not come to as it is, also when that file was changed meanwhile: the project is then as it was, without a journal. A change that is found by its journal is settled by the digests of its files alone, never by what is left in its directory, and nothing is done before every file was looked at: every file at its digest after (whoever wrote it, as when the change was completed under another home directory or after the project was moved) means the change is complete, and its journal and directory are removed; every file at its digest before (a fresh checkout at the same path, or a change undone by hand) means there is nothing to undo, and they are removed with a line that says so; a file at neither digest means that nothing is put back and the files are named; otherwise some files are at the digest after and the rest at the digest before, and the first are put back from their copies, each verified against the digest before, and the directories that were made are removed, as they are, when they are empty, with a journal that leaves nothing to undo. A file of which a change only changes the mode has one digest before and after: it counts as written when its mode is not that of the copy that was kept of it, and is put back, mode and all, only then. A journal of the first two kinds, and a record of a change that had only begun to stage, does not keep `--dry-run` and `--diff` from working. Recovery (`FeatureAddition.recover`, run by the next `feature add`, which prints what it did and every file it put back) acts only on a journal of this user and first reads the state: the three state files as they were before the change, each from the project or from its copy by the digest of the journal, have to be a state (`ProjectState.of`), and so have the three after it; every path of the journal has to be a state file, `grails-forge-cli.yml`, a file of one of the two baseline indexes or its copy, and every directory a parent of one; the directory of the change, `new` and `backup` have to be real directories. A journal that does not hold to that, that is of another project path, or that cannot be read puts nothing back. What a project carries under `.grails/transactions` is never read: no file there is an authority, the command says that it is there and leaves it. The hosted Forge has no part of this. Specs: every file, directory and mode and the state after an add equal those of the application created with the feature, for all four application types, non-default options, exclusions and requested features; edits kept; merge; conflicts leave the tree byte-identical; repeated add; the refusals and their previews; CRLF clones; binary and executable files; an interruption after every one of the writes of a change, each followed by a recovery that is itself interrupted; an edit during and after a change, to written and unwritten files; journals the command did not write, in the project (through the command) and in the user's directory; a failed write, a concurrent edit, two commands at once, a lock that is a pipe; a seeded property spec of the merge (identities, symmetry, CR LF, and never a result where `git merge-file` has a conflict or another result, on 7,500 short cases of five seeds and twelve long files that take the cheaper way to the anchors, whose outcome with Git 2.x is a committed file, so the spec needs no Git; the limits of 200,000 lines and 2,000 changed lines each from both sides); a generated application with an added feature builds with Gradle.

Not done: adding the feature of an external pack (by coordinates, `--feature-pack-dir` or jar), and adding anything to an application that has one, because the lock does not hold what replaying such a pack needs; `feature diff`, `feature list`, `search` and `info`; baseline adoption (`project init`), so an application that was not generated by Grails 9 cannot get a feature by command (`project migrate` writes it an untracked record, which `feature add` refuses); removal of a feature, change of an option, upgrade of the generator, presets and parameters; a feature that takes the place of a default (a database driver for `h2` first of all) is shown and refused, not applied. The project is the one the current directory is in, found as the other project commands find it (step 4, slice 2, in section 2.5); an application that is a project of a larger build is replayed as one (section 2.5, "Multi-project builds"). The replay check cannot tell two builds of one snapshot version apart unless their output differs, since the lock has no build identity. An adoption (`keep`) is said on the console and not recorded in the state, as with `--inplace`; a feature the application already has is not made a requested one. An interrupted change is found by the real path of the project and the home directory of the user: under another home directory, or after the project was moved, the journal is not found, the state may not read, and the staging directory in the project, which then holds the only copies of the replaced files, is reported and left alone; when the change is completed or undone there by other means, the journal is recognised as complete or as having nothing to undo once it is found again. A crash followed by an edit of a file the change had not reached is refused until that file is what it was, because after a crash nothing but the digests says which files the change wrote. The code generation commands and the create commands do not look for an interrupted change; they see a state that does not read. The lock is not held between the plan and the apply of one command (the preimages are compared instead), and there is no protection against an editor writing a file between the last comparison and the rename. Forcing a directory to disk is attempted and its failure ignored, so what survives a power failure is what the platform gives; a kill of the process at any point is covered. No Windows run: the paths taken there (no POSIX permissions, so executable flags are neither compared nor set and the directory of the journals is not checked for its owner; replacing a file that is open) are written and untested. The catalog refresh of step 7 has nothing to refresh yet. The merge has no notion of the structure of `build.gradle` or YAML: the generator writes the lines of a new feature where its ordering puts them, which for the configuration is the end of `application.yml` and for several dependencies the end of the `dependencies` block, and a line the user added in the same place is a conflict (measured over the features of a web application: a line appended to `application.yml` for 7 of the 14 features that change it, a dependency added as the last line of the block for 8 of 33, as its first line for none). Decisions left to the maintainer: whether the generator should keep a fixed last line in those places (a marker comment, or a blank line before the closing brace) so that the two do not meet; and whether two independent top-level blocks appended to the end of a YAML file by the two sides may be merged in a fixed order, which is sound for YAML mappings with different keys and is deliberately not done by a merge that knows only lines.

`feature diff` supports comparing an arbitrary recorded selection with proposed additions. For an untracked migrated project it is advisory and clearly says that there is no verified ancestor for automatic application. Feature removal and pack/generator upgrades are deferred; do not advertise an executable inverse-diff removal command in v1.

<a id="hosted"></a>
### 2.7 Hosted Forge and the UI

- The hosted service hosts the framework's own features only. External packs, the plugin index and `feature add` are CLI capabilities; the web API and `apache/grails-forge-ui` gain no plugin search and load nothing from outside their build.
- Official features converted to bundled packs retain their generated application output through golden tests; the ZIP additionally includes the 9.0 manifest, lock and baseline. Preview/diff presentation must explicitly handle that metadata and avoid recursively showing the baseline copy as application output. Keep existing routes and version slots; test response compatibility rather than claiming no API changes are necessary. `FeatureDTO` may expose `documentation` and stable string group IDs through a versioned/additive contract.
- The hosted 8.1 slots retain their released behavior. The 9.0 UI/wizard exposes new official application types/options and the new generation semantics; it does not expose external presets, catalog resolution or `feature add`.

<a id="removal"></a>
## 4. Removal inventory

All removals are on 9.0.x after their replacement gates. This is the October 13 inventory, not a claim that its line numbers and counts remain current; "Slices" under step 5 has the scan of `7f7d4ede58`, with the files of each slice and what the inventory did not have. Re-scan on the implementation branch and classify each reference as production dependency, compatibility adapter, migration fixture or historical documentation; migration tests must retain representative old inputs.

**Forge** (`grails-forge/`): `GrailsProfiles` is replaced by project-state production and the retained runtime default-package setting. Write the baseline only after application rendering, excluding generation state from its own snapshot. Remove `Scope.PROFILE`, `Phase.PROFILE`, `GradleConfiguration.PROFILE`; move legacy `CodeGenConfig` parsing into migration; replace tests coupled to profile skeletons with owned golden fixtures. Remove Forge's own profile configuration, obsolete dispatcher comments and profile-specific build workarounds after checking their remaining consumers.

**Shell** (`grails-shell-cli/`): everything under `profile/repository/`, `Profile`, `AbstractProfile`, `ResourceProfile`, `FileSystemProfile`, `Feature`, `DefaultFeature`, `ProfileRepository*`, `ProfileRepoConfig`, `ProfileUtil`, the `Create*Command`, `ListProfilesCommand`, `ProfileInfoCommand`, `ArgumentCompletingCommand`, `io/GradleDependency`, their service-file entries, `GrailsCli` members `:87-89,129-133,195-201,260-271,307-311,324-332,386-394,518-534`; the Spring Boot CLI port and the maven-resolver dependencies (`grails-shell-cli/build.gradle:86-100`). Re-home `CompilerAutoConfiguration`, which the hibernate boot-plugins compile against (`grails-data-hibernate5/boot-plugin/build.gradle:50`). Rewrite rather than delete: `CommandRegistry.findCommands`, the four `CommandFactory` implementations, `GradleTaskCommandAdapter`, `HelpCommand`, `GroovyScriptCommand`, `TemplateRendererImpl`, `RenderStep`, `DefaultStepFactory`'s `ProfileCommand` gate (`:44-47`), `populateContextLoader` (keep the classpath part, drop `:571,582-584`).

**Gradle plugins** (`grails-gradle/`): `GrailsGradlePlugin.groovy:166,461-490,782-784`; `GrailsPluginGradlePlugin.groovy:138-140`; `GrailsClasspathToolingModelBuilder.groovy:35,51-56` and `GrailsClasspath.getProfileDependencies`; the `grails-profile` and `grails-publish-profile` plugins (`plugins/build.gradle:143-160`, `profiles/*`, `ProfileCompilerTask`; `GroovyScriptCommandTransform` stays while scripts are supported); `ProfileCompilerTaskSpec`; `BomPlatformFunctionalSpec.groovy:76-98` and its fixture `test-projects/bom-platform-default-dependencies/build.gradle:98`.

**Root build**: the seven `grails-profiles/*` modules (`settings.gradle:465-485`), `grails-spring-security/rest/testapp-profile` (convert its features to packs or test fixtures), `gradle/publish-root-config.gradle:172-191`, `build.gradle:76,163-165` (`profileProjects`, unused), `PublishPlugin.groovy:58,146-151,191-197`, `gradle/rat-root-config.gradle:73-76,161-166`, `settings.gradle:661-666` and `end-to-end/settings.gradle:132-137` (DirectoryScanner workaround), `grails-bom/base/build.gradle:78-94` and the `forcedGroupPrefixes` entries in all five BOMs, `etc/bin/verify-jar-artifacts.sh:208-236`, `etc/bin/reset-verify.sh:47`, `.github/dependency-graph/external-references.yml:42-48,197-203` (the purls are wrong today anyway), `RENAME.md:164-182`, `etc/bin/rename_gradle_artifacts.sh:305-347`, `INSTALL:98`.

**In-repo consumers**: profile dependencies in example builds; `grails.profile` keys in application configuration; plugin-descriptor `profiles` declarations and Forge's plugin template. Audit active-profile behavior before removing configuration keys: they are not harmless metadata while the runtime injection exists. Preserve representative legacy inputs in migration fixtures.

**Docs** (`grails-doc/src/en/guide/`): the whole `profiles` chapter (`toc.yml:91-106`, twelve pages) is replaced by a "Feature packs and presets" chapter; `commandLine.adoc:20-71,112-122`, `interactiveMode.adoc:19-41`, `creatingCustomScripts.adoc`, `reusingGrailsScripts.adoc` and `gradleBuild.adoc` are deleted or rewritten; `creatingProject.adoc:19-37`, `creatingCustomCommands.adoc:21-69,219-272` (the `executionContext.environment` example at `:248` is wrong today), `gradleDependencies.adoc:27,61`, `gradleTasks.adoc`, `gradlePlugins.adoc:63-64`, `downloadingAndInstalling.adoc:24-93`, `creatingAnApplication.adoc`, `aHelloWorldExample.adoc`, `usingInteractiveMode.adoc`, `creatingArtefacts.adoc`, `generatingAnApplication.adoc`, `REST/restProfile.adoc`, `plugins/creatingAndInstallingPlugins.adoc:390-466`, `plugins/repositories.adoc:21-40`, `plugins/providingBasicArtefacts.adoc:21-68,127-141`; the 47 command reference pages (five are profile-only); `index.adoc:736` includes a path that does not exist. `GRAILS_PREFERRED_SHELL` and the wrapper environment variables are undocumented today and need a home in the new chapter.

<a id="phases"></a>
## 5. Delivery

The release decision is settled: **all steps after the completed Grails-application conversion target 9.0.x**. Intermediate development commits may coexist with old code on that branch, but the release has one generator and one command tree. Do not backport these new contracts or removals to 8.1. Implementation may proceed incrementally; profile removal is gated by migration, command coverage and distribution/IDE readiness, not by a calendar estimate.

<a id="status"></a>
### Implementation status

**Revision 51 integration.** The changes of 9.0.x through `c07a70d2d02` are included. Pack generation has locale-neutral names and help, stable equal-priority contributions and imports, an exact-spelling tie-break for numerically equal catalog versions, and timestamp-free Groovy extension metadata in both shadow stages. Public generation and artifact tests compare bytes under changed directories, locales, encodings, timestamps and permissions; the release conditions and intentional external inputs are documented in the feature-pack guide. Forge specs may use `-PprebuiltTestRepository` to resolve framework modules and Gradle plugins from a completed test repository instead of rebuilding them. Version, Java target, call-site variant and source fingerprint are checked; Forge-only changes do not invalidate the repository, and incomplete publications invalidate their old completion record. CI shares Java 21 indy/classic artifacts with the eligible Forge test lanes, while coverage reuses the core coverage build and no-tests lanes keep the ordinary composite. The migrated `s2-create-acl-domains` command consumes the templates fixed by #16968: emitted classes compare and serialize as the plugin's classes do, including `objectId` and superclass state in ACL identity equality. Existing application classes are not overwritten and need the documented manual update.

Historical delivery baseline as of 2026-11-20. Later revision paragraphs and the change log supersede this table; the "Implemented" and "Not done" paragraphs of the sections named hold the detail.

| Step | State | Where | Not done |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Known at that baseline, since superseded by the later test and Windows revisions: nothing was run on Windows, where the paths for it were written and tested only by setting `os.name`; the six specs that build an application for a `create-*` command had not been run where this was developed; a plain `create-plugin` or `create-web-plugin` application failed `:generateI18nDescriptor` against the published snapshot, and a REST API application had no `grails-testing-support-web` for the test `create-controller` wrote.

Decided while implementing, each recorded where it applies and open to reversal: no catalog is read that was not named (2.3); a template of a project is never run (2.5, slice 6); a repository with a password is not copied into the fixtures of `forgePackTest` unless it is listed (2.3); the Gradle wrapper is run as a process and the Tooling API is not used (2.5, slices 1, 3 and 4); a project above the current directory is used only when it is the user's own (2.5); a script of a project is run by the build of the project and never by the CLI (2.5, slice 7); in a build of several projects the tasks are addressed by the path the directories give, the settings script is neither read nor written, and a command at the root names the Grails projects and chooses none (2.5, "Multi-project builds").

### Step 0 — completed conversion and implementation baseline

PR #16551 merged on 2026-10-14 into 8.1.x, and the reviewed 9.0 branch contains the conversion. This is historical prerequisite work, not an outstanding merge. Before implementation, record the selected 9.0 commit, run the Forge build/tests there and refresh the inventory of old commands and profile consumers.

**Exit:** a reproducible 9.0 baseline and command/capability matrix; the 8.1 conversion remains the only part of this unification effort assigned to that line.

### Step 1 — selection, contribution and template contracts (9.0.x)

Implement canonical feature/group IDs, deterministic selection, dependency closure (a cycle is a mutual requirement) and conflict rules and the versioned descriptor/template API. Add the pack adapter, source-capable scoped GSP engine/cache, local-directory mode, exclusions, provenance-aware contribution merging and dynamic codegen descriptors. Move central build-template branches into their owning features and remove the `Dependency.extension` hook that was never rendered. Convert a small proof set (Quartz, cache-ehcache, Asciidoctor, web-console), adapting their template models as required.

**Exit:** mixed compiled/pack selection passes the edge-case matrix; converted features retain golden application outputs; build templates contain structure rather than feature-name branches; source compilation and generated-build checks pass on the declared supported stack. Hosted rendering remains bundled-only.

### Step 2 — pack/preset distribution and authoring (9.0.x)

Extend `grails-plugins-metadata` for versioned pack metadata, exact main-JAR checksums and pack-only presets. Implement CLI discovery, direct coordinates, repository routing, compatibility filtering and content-addressed caches. Add Grails Gradle authoring support with isolated check compilation/generation workers and configuration-cache tests. Publish synthetic plugin and company-preset fixtures into `build/local-maven` and exercise both catalog and direct-coordinate selection. Include the complete preset schema and precedence rules.

**Exit:** fixtures generate/build online from local repositories and offline from locked inputs; the main-JAR digest matches what the index records; private mappings, timestamped snapshots, incompatible APIs and checksum mismatches behave as specified. No live public catalog is required for deterministic tests. No external pack reaches hosted Forge.

### Step 3 — project state, migration and feature transactions (9.0.x)

Implement manifest, lock and baseline writers/readers, typed byte-preserving output trees, `project init`, file-only `project migrate --to 9.0`, arbitrary-selection diffs and transactional `feature add`. Add `.gitignore` entries only for machine-local state. Existing markers are migration inputs; new 9.0 projects write only the new state. Exercise built-in 8.0/8.1 profile and Forge projects, explicit custom-preset mappings, untracked/adopted baselines and active-Spring-profile migration guidance.

**Exit:** create → edit → add → build/run preserves user edits; conflicts leave project/state unchanged; repeated application is a no-op; binary/mode handling, interrupted recovery and fresh-clone replay pass. Missing ancestry and unsupported migration cases produce actionable diagnostics rather than guessed state.

### Step 4 — command API, unified launcher and functional coverage (9.0.x)

Implement execution targets, metadata-only discovery, profile-free `CommandCatalog`, lazy startup, lifecycle services, `runAppOptions`, command refresh and the stable launcher/JSON API. Adapt context-bound/legacy companions, add app-local discovery and supported script-DSL compatibility. Complete `rest-api-plugin`, the named artefact commands, all REST scaffolding operations and plugin publication adapters. Coordinate the IntelliJ 8.1/9.0 version switch and lifecycle state paths. Build a table for every old command: retained with tested behavior, intentionally changed with an upgrade note, or retired with a replacement hint.

**Exit:** every command in that table has coverage; context commands boot no server; console cancellation/process ownership and standalone execution are verified; built-in help survives broken application builds; supported precompiled companions work or receive explicit incompatibility diagnostics. CLI/IDE smoke tests cover both version contracts. Profile-versus-Forge generation semantics are documented major-version changes, not asserted equivalent output.

#### Slices

The cut is based on the code at `40c07df93c`, not on the line references of section 2.5 and Appendix B, which were re-checked for it. Nothing of the unmerged pull request #17018 is in the tree: `grails-forge-cli` has no plugin commands, and JLine is 3.30.16 with `picocli-shell-jline3`.

**What exists, per item of section 2.5.**

| Item | In `grails-forge-cli` | Only in `grails-shell-cli` / profiles | Nowhere |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Lifecycle commands | Nothing. Before slice 1 no command ran anything in a project. | Profile scripts `grails-profiles/base/commands/{run-app,stop-app,test-app,console,shell,run-command,run-script,stats,bug-report,dependency-report,assemble}.groovy`, `clean.yml`, `compile.yml`, `package.yml`, `plugin/commands/{package-plugin,publish-plugin,install}.yml`; the services they call: `GradleUtil`, `GradleInvoker`, `GradleAsyncInvoker`, `RunningApplicationProcess`, `ServerInteraction`, all over the Tooling API, which only `grails-shell-cli/build.gradle:67` declares (version from the root `gradle.properties`, not a BOM constraint). | Lifecycle state under `.grails/run/`, process ownership, `runAppOptions`, the execution targets as an API. |
| 2. Gradle passthrough | `gradle` since slice 1, as a wrapper process (see section 2.5). | `GradleCommand` with the one-string bug (`:60-66`), `ReadGradleTasks` completion with its cache. | Task-name completion outside the shell. |
| 3. Plugin `ApplicationCommand`s | Nothing. | `ApplicationContextCommandFactory` and `GradleTaskCommandAdapter`; both take a `Profile`, and the factory loads `ApplicationContextCommandRegistry` from a class loader the shell fills from the `GrailsClasspath` tooling model. The adapter sends all arguments as one `-Pargs=` string joined by spaces, which the task splits again with `CommandLineParser.translateCommandline` (`GrailsCliGradlePlugin.groovy:426-429`): an argument with a space or a quote does not survive. | The contract is in core and stays: `grails-core/src/cli` (`ApplicationCommand`, the registry, which instantiates commands, `META-INF/grails-cli.factories`), `grails-console` (the runners), and `GrailsCliGradlePlugin` (per-command tasks from the factories files of `grailsCliClasspath` jars, `:451-480`; `runCommand`). Not there: descriptors that can be read without loading classes (the factories file has class names only), app-local `grails-app/commands` discovery, a named route for legacy commands (`:411-414` leaves them to `runCommand`), an argument transport that keeps the vector. |
| 4. Artefact codegen | `create-controller`, `create-service`, `create-domain-class`, `create-taglib`, `create-interceptor`, `create-job`, `add-property`: a static list (`ForgeCli.groovy:49-57`), added when the current directory (not a parent) has a usable `.grails` state or earlier record (`CodeGenConfig.load`), templates compiled into the CLI (`grails-app/views/forge/cli/command/templates`). | `create-unit-test`, `create-integration-test`, `create-functional-test`, `create-restful-controller`, `create-domain-resource`, `create-command`, `create-script` as profile YAML and scripts with profile templates; the `src/main/templates` lookup (`TemplateRendererImpl`). `install-templates` is in `grails-scaffolding` (`InstallTemplatesCommand`, an `ApplicationCommand`, and a script). | Pack artefacts (the pack descriptor has no section for them), the three-level template lookup, fingerprinted overrides. |

Script commands | Nothing. | All of it: `GroovyScriptCommand`, `GroovyScriptCommandFactory`, `ResourceResolvingCommandFactory`, the steps, `profile.hasCommand` chaining; `GroovyScriptCommandTransform` in grails-gradle. `FileSystemInteraction` and `TemplateRenderer` already have copies in `grails-core/src/cli`. | A host for scripts without a profile. |
| 6. Catalog services | picocli's own: usage help at the root and per command (`--help`; there is no `help` command), a "Did you mean" line for a mistyped command with exit code 2, a generated completion script and `PicocliJLineCompleter` in the interactive shell, built once when the shell starts. Project commands are listed only where the directory has a record. The legacy-command hint is computed in core (`ApplicationCommandDiagnostics`, through the registry). | `CommandCatalog`, `help --json`, collision diagnostics, refresh after a mutation, help without the application context. |

The launcher is separate from all six: `grails-forge/grails-cli` has `DelegatingShellApplication` (`-t/--type`, `GRAILS_PREFERRED_SHELL`, default `shell`) and three start scripts; `org.apache.grails.cli.GrailsCli`, `--plain-output` and `--non-interactive` do not exist.

**The slices.** Each can be merged alone, leaves `grails-shell-cli`, the profiles, `DelegatingShellApplication` and the start scripts as they are, and is verified without building a generated application unless it says so.

1. *Gradle passthrough and project location* — done, see "Gradle passthrough, as implemented" in section 2.5. Delivers `gradle <args>` and `ConsoleProject`. Depends on nothing. Leaves for step 5: the shell's `GradleCommand`.
2. *Catalog for the commands the CLI has* — done, see "Catalog for the commands the CLI has, as implemented" in section 2.5, which also says what a command of a later slice does to be registered. Leaves for step 5: `AbstractProfile` dispatch and `HelpCommand` in the shell. Does not include help without the application context (slice 8).
